Written Questions & Answers · QP-2303

Engineering Management — March 2023

EXAMINATION OF MARINE ENGINEER OFFICER · Function: Marine Engineering Management at Management Level · M.E.O. Class I

Sr. No. QP-2303 3 hours Total marks 100 9 questions · answer six (India 2023)

  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage

Marks are largely UNPRINTED on this paper, which is its most significant printed characteristic. Only Q6 and Q7 print a complete 16 (8+8). Q4 prints (8) against limb A and nothing against limb B. Q1, Q2, Q3, Q5, Q8 and Q9 print no marks at all. total_marks is therefore carried as 16 per question BY RUBRIC, NOT BY PRINT: instruction 2 states all questions carry equal marks, six answered questions are required, and the paper prints 'Total Marks - 100'. Six equal questions against 100 gives 16.67, and every question on the MEO Class I Engineering Management papers that does print a figure prints (16). Six answered questions at 16 total 96 against the printed 100; that discrepancy is printed on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected.

Understand this first

Two quite different things are being asked, and the second one exists because of the first. Start with the definition, because it is much wider than an engineer's instinct. The Convention does not say pollution is oil in the water. It says it is the introduction by man - so a natural seep does not count - of substances or energy into the sea, which results or is likely to result in harm. Two things in that are easy to miss and both earn marks. Energy means heat and noise are pollution, with nothing spilt at all. And likely to result means nothing has to have gone wrong yet - which is precisely what allows the Convention to require States to prevent rather than merely to clean up. Then the obligations. The rest of UNCLOS is a map: it divides the sea into zones and says who may do what in each. Part XII is different, because the duty to protect the marine environment is not attached to a zone at all - it simply exists, everywhere, and it even qualifies a State's right to exploit its own resources. What a coastal State must actually do splits in two. It must make rules, and how much freedom it has depends on the source: for pollution from the land, which is its own business, it has wide discretion; for pollution from ships, which is everyone's business, it must work through IMO and may not set a lower standard than the international one. And it must enforce - but it is one of three enforcers, alongside the flag State, whose duty is primary, and the port State, which can prosecute a discharge that happened on the other side of an ocean. The last article of Part XII is the one that hands over to the second half of the question: States must make sure that somebody who suffers pollution damage can actually get paid. And the three tiers are how the world arranged that for oil. A tanker spill can cost more than any owner could ever insure, so the owner is made liable without proof of fault but only up to a limit, and everything above that comes from the industry that wanted the oil shipped in the first place - collected from the refineries and terminals that receive it. Ship first, cargo afterwards. That is the whole idea.

Bullet answer — points to write
A.
  1. The Convention's definition of pollution, and what makes it unusually wide
    • Article 1(1)(4) - the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries
    • which results or is likely to result in deleterious effects
    • "by man" excludes a natural seep; "indirectly" catches a river carrying waste to the sea
    • "or energy" - heat, noise and radiation are pollution
    • "or is likely to result" - no damage need have occurred
    • Five effects: harm to living resources and marine life; hazards to human health; hindrance to marine activities including fishing; impairment of quality for use of sea water; reduction of amenities
    • The definition is source-neutral - nothing in it is about ships
  2. The general obligation, and why it is not confined to any zone
    • Article 192 - States have the obligation to protect and preserve the marine environment
    • Free-standing and not zone-limited - the zones allocate rights; this imposes a duty across all of them
    • Article 193 - the sovereign right to exploit resources is expressly subject to that duty
    • Article 194 - all measures necessary, from any source, by the best practicable means and in accordance with capabilities
    • Activities under a State's jurisdiction or control must not cause damage by pollution to other States
    • No transferring damage from one area to another, and no transforming one type of pollution into another
  3. The coastal State's obligations - rule-making
    • The Convention deals with pollution source by source
    • Article 207 land-based sources - adopt national law, taking into account international rules. Wide discretion
    • Article 210 dumping - national law, and express prior approval of the coastal State within its zones
    • Article 211 vessels - act through IMO; national law must have at least the same effect as the international standard
    • Article 212 from or through the atmosphere
    • The discrimination that scores: stricter for your own ships, yes; laxer, no; and no construction standards on foreign ships in innocent passage
    • The rules are to be re-examined from time to time
  4. The coastal State's obligations - enforcement, and its limits
    • Article 217 flag State - compliance by its own ships wherever the violation occurs. The primary duty
    • Article 218 port State - where the ship is voluntarily in port, proceedings for discharges outside its own waters
    • Article 220 coastal State - violations in the territorial sea or EEZ, powers graded by seriousness and evidence
    • Information, then physical inspection, then detention on clear objective evidence of major damage
    • Article 221 - measures beyond the territorial sea after a maritime casualty, proportionate to the damage threatened
    • Article 235 - responsibility, liability, and recourse for prompt and adequate compensation
    • Article 235(2) is the bridge to limb B
    • The Part XII safeguards constrain how the powers may be exercised
B.
  1. Why the oil pollution compensation regime has three tiers
    • A major spill can cost more than any owner or insurer could reasonably carry
    • Unlimited liability would have made tankers uninsurable; leaving victims uncompensated was unacceptable
    • The compromise: the owner is strictly but limitedly liable
    • and the balance is placed on the cargo interest - those who receive the oil
    • The regime shares the cost between the ship and the cargo, which is its defining idea
  2. Tier one - the shipowner and his insurer
    • CLC 1992. The registered owner is strictly liable - no fault need be proved
    • Liability is channelled to him - not the master, crew, pilot, charterer, manager or salvor
    • Compulsory insurance for ships carrying persistent oil in bulk, evidenced by a certificate carried on board
    • Right of direct action against the insurer
    • Source of funds: the shipping side - the owner and his P&I club
    • He may limit by reference to tonnage. No figure is given - the limits are amended over time
    • The limit is lost for his personal act or omission, with intent or recklessly and with knowledge
    • Narrow exceptions: war, exceptional natural phenomenon, wholly a third party's intentional act, negligence of a navigational-aids authority
  3. Tiers two and three - the Funds, and who actually provides the money
    • Tier two - the 1992 IOPC Fund, where tier one gives nothing or is financially inadequate
    • Tier three - the Supplementary Fund under the 2003 Protocol, where tier two is insufficient
    • Source for both: contributions levied on RECEIVERS OF CONTRIBUTING OIL - crude and heavy fuel oil received by sea above a threshold quantity
    • The cargo interest pays, not the shipowner and not governments - the point most candidates miss
    • The tiers are cumulative and sequential, each with its own overall ceiling
    • Tier three is OPTIONAL and requires membership of the 1992 Fund first
    • So the compensation available for the same casualty depends on where it happens
  4. What the three tiers do NOT cover - and the trap
    • The regime is about persistent oil carried in bulk as cargo - the tanker case
    • Bunker oil from a non-tanker is the Bunkers Convention 2001 - a single tier with no fund behind it
    • Hazardous and noxious substances have their own convention, not in force at this sitting
    • Geography matters - the tiers respond only in States party, and the third only where opted into
    • Closing: the three tiers exist because UNCLOS article 235(2) requires recourse for prompt and adequate compensation

Memory cue. Limb A - define, duty, rules, enforcement. DEFINE (1): by man, substances or energy, results or is likely to result, five effects. DUTY (2): 192 free-standing, 193 sovereignty qualified, 194 best practicable means. RULES (3): 207 take into account, 211 through IMO and no less effective. ENFORCEMENT (4): 217 flag, 218 port, 220 coastal graded, 221 casualty, 235 compensation. Limb B - why, one, two and three, outside. WHY (5): ship first, cargo after. ONE (6): owner strict, insurer pays, direct action. TWO AND THREE (7): the Funds, from receivers of contributing oil. OUTSIDE (8): bunkers, HNS, geography.

Remember8 route headings
Cover53 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 53 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. The Convention's definition of pollution, and what makes it unusually wide

The stem asks for the definition as per UNCLOS, so it must be the Convention's own, and not a general description of dirty water. It is given at article 1, paragraph 1(4):

"Pollution of the marine environment" means the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities, including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water and reduction of amenities.

The marks are in taking that definition apart, because four separate elements in it are each wider than a candidate expects:

ElementWhat it catches
"by man, directly or indirectly"Human agency is required, so a natural seep is not pollution - but indirect introduction is caught, so a river carrying industrial waste to the sea is within the definition
"substances OR ENERGY"The most under-used word in the definition. Heat, noise and radiation are pollution. Thermal discharge from a power station and underwater noise from shipping both fall within it, without any substance being discharged at all
"results OR IS LIKELY TO RESULT"No damage need have occurred. Likelihood of deleterious effect is enough, which is what makes the definition capable of supporting preventive obligations rather than only remedial ones
The five deleterious effectsHarm to living resources and marine life; hazards to human health; hindrance to marine activities, including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea; impairment of quality for use of sea water; and reduction of amenities. The last two are economic and even aesthetic, not merely ecological

Two further points are worth a line each. The definition expressly includes estuaries, so it reaches inland of the coastline. And it is source-neutral: nothing in it is about ships. Ships are one source among several, which is exactly how Part XII goes on to treat them.

2. The general obligation, and why it is not confined to any zone

The stem's own opening - that one of the objectives of UNCLOS is protection of the marine environment - is answered by article 192, which is one sentence long and is the foundation of the whole of Part XII: "States have the obligation to protect and preserve the marine environment."

Three features of that make it unusual, and they are what the second half of limb A is really about.

  • It is free-standing and is not confined to any zone. The rest of the Convention allocates rights zone by zone - territorial sea, contiguous zone, exclusive economic zone, continental shelf, high seas. Article 192 imposes a duty, and it runs across all of them and beyond. A coastal State does not owe it only in its own waters.
  • It qualifies sovereignty itself. Article 193 confirms that States have the sovereign right to exploit their natural resources pursuant to their environmental policies and in accordance with their duty to protect and preserve the marine environment. The right to exploit is expressly made subject to the duty - which is the single most quoted proposition in this Part.
  • It is an obligation of conduct with a stated standard. Article 194 requires States to take all measures necessary to prevent, reduce and control pollution from any source, using the best practicable means at their disposal and in accordance with their capabilities. That formulation is deliberate: it does not demand the impossible of a developing State, and it does not excuse a capable one.

Article 194(2) adds a further duty that a good answer names: activities under a State's jurisdiction or control must be so conducted as not to cause damage by pollution to other States and their environment, and pollution arising from them must not spread beyond the areas where the State exercises sovereign rights. Article 195 is then a separate article in its own right - Duty not to transfer damage or hazards or transform one type of pollution into another - and it provides that in taking measures under this Part, States shall act so as not to transfer, directly or indirectly, damage or hazards from one area to another or transform one type of pollution into another. That is the provision an examiner will reach for if the answer strays into scrubber washwater or incineration, and it is worth citing by its own number rather than folding it into article 194.

3. The coastal State's obligations - rule-making

The Convention deals with pollution source by source, and the coastal State's obligation differs with the source. The pattern is consistent and is worth showing as a pattern rather than as a list:

SourceArticleWhat the coastal State must do
Land-based sources207Adopt national laws and regulations, taking into account internationally agreed rules and standards. The weakest link in the chain, and the largest source of marine pollution
Dumping210Adopt national law; and dumping within the territorial sea, the EEZ or on the continental shelf requires the express prior approval of the coastal State
Pollution from vessels211Act through the competent international organization - IMO - and national laws must have at least the same effect as the generally accepted international rules and standards. In the territorial sea, laws must not hamper innocent passage; in the EEZ, they must conform to those international rules
From or through the atmosphere212Adopt laws applicable to the air space under its sovereignty and to vessels of its flag or registry

The discrimination that carries the mark is between article 207 and article 211. For land-based sources, which are entirely within a State's own territory, the Convention leaves it a wide discretion and asks only that it take international standards into account. For pollution from vessels, which is inherently international, it requires the State to act through IMO and forbids it from legislating below the international standard. A coastal State may be stricter than the international rules for its own ships; it may not be laxer, and it may not impose its own construction standards on foreign ships in innocent passage. That single sentence explains the entire architecture of ship-source pollution regulation.

The Convention also requires that these rules be re-examined from time to time as necessary, which is why the IMO instruments they refer to are amendable while UNCLOS itself is not.

4. The coastal State's obligations - enforcement, and its limits

Enforcement under Part XII is divided three ways, and the coastal State holds one of the three - which is why an answer that treats the coastal State as the only actor misses the structure.

  • The flag State (article 217) must ensure that vessels flying its flag comply, wherever the violation occurs, and must investigate and where appropriate institute proceedings. This is the primary duty and it does not go away.
  • The port State (article 218) may, where a vessel is voluntarily in its port, investigate and institute proceedings in respect of discharges that occurred outside its own waters - a remarkable extension of jurisdiction, and the treaty foundation of port State control as an environmental instrument.
  • The coastal State (article 220) may act in respect of violations within its territorial sea or exclusive economic zone, and the powers available are graded: information may be required where there are clear grounds for believing a violation occurred; physical inspection where the response is inadequate or manifestly at odds with the facts; and detention and proceedings only where there is clear objective evidence of a discharge causing or threatening major damage.

Two further coastal-State features complete the picture. Article 221 preserves the right to take and enforce measures beyond the territorial sea, proportionate to the actual or threatened damage, to protect the coastline from pollution following a maritime casualty. And article 235 makes States responsible for the fulfilment of their international obligations concerning the marine environment and liable in accordance with international law.

Article 235(2) is where limb A hands over to limb B, and saying so joins the question together: States must ensure that recourse is available in accordance with their legal systems for prompt and adequate compensation or other relief in respect of damage caused by pollution by natural or juridical persons under their jurisdiction, and must cooperate in developing the law of liability and compensation further. The three tiers in limb B are how that obligation is actually discharged for oil.

Finally, the coastal State's powers are not unqualified. Part XII, section 7 carries its own safeguards, articles 223 to 233, and they constrain every enforcement power described above. The ones that bear on a ship are: enforcement against foreign vessels may be exercised only by officials or warships and aircraft on government service (article 224); the powers must be exercised without endangering the safety of navigation or the vessel (article 225); a foreign vessel shall not be delayed longer than is essential for the investigation, and physical inspection is limited to an examination of certificates unless there are clear grounds to go further (article 226); there must be no discrimination against the vessels of any other State (article 227); only monetary penalties may be imposed for pollution violations by foreign vessels beyond the territorial sea, and the recognized rights of the accused are to be observed (article 230); the flag State must be promptly notified (article 231); and States are liable for damage or loss attributable to enforcement measures that are unlawful or exceed those reasonably required (article 232). Naming two or three of these is what separates an answer that knows the coastal State's obligations from one that knows only its powers.

5. Why the oil pollution compensation regime has three tiers

Limb B asks for the three main tiers, who pays in each case, and the source of the funds. Before naming them it is worth one sentence on why the regime is layered at all, because it explains everything that follows.

A major tanker spill can cost far more than any shipowner or his insurer could reasonably be required to carry, and requiring unlimited liability would have made tankers uninsurable. But leaving victims uncompensated was unacceptable. The compromise was to make the shipowner strictly but limitedly liable, and then to place the balance on the cargo interest - the oil industry which benefits from the trade - through funds financed by those who receive the oil. The regime therefore shares the cost between the ship and the cargo, which is its defining idea.

6. Tier one - the shipowner and his insurer

TIER ONE - Civil Liability Convention 1992
Who is liableThe registered owner of the ship at the time of the incident. Liability is strict - no fault need be proved - and it is channelled to the registered owner, so claims may not ordinarily be brought against the master, crew, pilot, charterer, manager or salvor
Who actually paysThe registered owner, and in practice his liability insurer - normally a protection and indemnity club. Insurance or other financial security is compulsory for ships carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo above a stated size, evidenced by a certificate carried on board, and the claimant has a right of direct action against the insurer
Source of the fundsThe shipping side - the owner's own resources and his insurance, ultimately funded by the premiums of shipowners generally
The limitThe owner may limit his liability to an amount fixed by reference to the ship's tonnage, by constituting a fund with the competent court. No figure is given here - the limits are amended over time and MIW holds no text of the Convention
When the limit is lostWhere the damage resulted from the owner's personal act or omission, committed with intent to cause such damage, or recklessly and with knowledge that such damage would probably result. A very high threshold, and deliberately so
Exceptions to liabilityNarrow: act of war, hostilities or an exceptional natural phenomenon; damage wholly caused by a third party's intentional act; and negligence of an authority responsible for maintaining navigational aids. Contributory fault of the claimant may reduce the claim

The practical point for an engineer is the compulsory insurance certificate: it is a document carried on board, checked at port State control, and without it the ship does not trade.

7. Tiers two and three - the Funds, and who actually provides the money

TIER TWO - Fund Convention 1992TIER THREE - Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003
When it respondsWhere tier one gives no compensation - the owner is exempt, or cannot be identified - or where it is financially inadequate, including where the owner's limit is exhaustedWhere tier two is itself insufficient to meet established claims in full
Who paysThe 1992 International Oil Pollution Compensation Fund, an intergovernmental organisation with its own legal personalityThe Supplementary Fund, established by the 2003 Protocol
Source of the fundsContributions levied on the receivers of contributing oil - crude oil and heavy fuel oil - received by sea in the territory of a Member State above a threshold quantity in a calendar year. The cargo interest pays, not the shipownerThe same mechanism, but levied only on receivers in the States that have opted into the Protocol. It is optional, and a State must be a party to the 1992 Fund Convention first
Who it is available toClaimants in 1992 Fund Member StatesClaimants in Supplementary Fund Member States only

Three features of the Fund tiers are worth stating expressly, because they are what the limb's second question is aimed at.

  1. The money comes from the cargo, not from the ship. Contributions are collected from oil receivers - refineries and terminals - and not from shipowners, charterers or governments. That is the structural answer to "who is responsible for payment", and it is the point most candidates miss.
  2. The tiers are cumulative and sequential, not alternative. Tier two tops up tier one; tier three tops up tier two. Each has its own overall ceiling, and the third tier's ceiling is a total that includes the amounts payable under the first two.
  3. Tier three is optional; tiers one and two are the standard regime. A State that has not joined the 2003 Protocol has two tiers, not three - so the compensation available for the same casualty depends on where it happens.

No monetary figure is given anywhere in this answer, and that is deliberate rather than evasive. The tonnage-band limits and the fund ceilings are amended by tacit acceptance over time, so any figure carries a date with it. MIW holds no text of the three instruments and does not assert their limits. The architecture - strict limited liability on the owner, then the cargo interest twice over - is what is stable and is what the question asks for.

8. What the three tiers do NOT cover - and the trap

The limb says oil pollution damage, and the boundaries of the regime are where an examiner will press. Four exclusions are worth a line each:

  • The regime is about persistent oil carried in bulk as cargo - the tanker case. It is not a general pollution compensation scheme.
  • Bunker oil from a non-tanker is a different regime. The Bunkers Convention 2001 covers pollution damage from a ship's own fuel, and it is a single tier, not three: liability rests on the shipowner in a wider sense, backed by compulsory insurance, with limitation under the general limitation convention - and there is no fund behind it. That is the sharpest available discrimination, and it is why a question comparing Bunkers with CLC is a different question from this one.
  • Hazardous and noxious substances are dealt with by their own convention, which was not in force at this sitting.
  • Geography matters. The tiers respond only in States party to the relevant instrument, and the third tier only in States that have opted into it.

And the closing observation, which returns the answer to limb A: the three tiers exist because UNCLOS article 235(2) requires States to ensure that recourse is available for prompt and adequate compensation. Limb A states the duty; limb B is the machinery by which, for oil, it is actually met.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Two limbs, no printed marks, so treat them as eight and eight. Limb A contains two separate instructions - define pollution as per UNCLOS, and discuss the key features of coastal-State obligations - and both must be answered explicitly. A candidate who writes generally about marine environmental protection has answered neither. Limb B contains three: name the tiers, say who pays in each case, and state the source of the funds. Answer all three for all three tiers and the limb is complete.

The strongest single move is to give the definition as a definition - close to the Convention's own words - and then take it apart. Paraphrasing it loosely throws away the easiest marks on the paper.

The definition, and the four things inside it

The introduction BY MAN, directly or indirectly, of substances OR ENERGY into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results OR IS LIKELY TO RESULT in deleterious effects - harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water, and reduction of amenities.

  • "by man" - a natural seep is not pollution. But "indirectly" catches a river carrying waste to the sea.
  • "or energy" - heat, noise and radiation are pollution. The most under-used word in the definition and a reliable extra mark.
  • "or is likely to result" - no damage need have occurred. This is what lets the definition support preventive duties.
  • Five deleterious effects, and the last two - impairment of quality for use of sea water, and reduction of amenities - are economic and aesthetic, not merely ecological.

The three propositions that carry the coastal-State half

  1. Article 192 - the obligation to protect and preserve is free-standing and not confined to any zone. The zones allocate rights; this imposes a duty across all of them.
  2. Article 193 - the sovereign right to exploit is expressly subject to that duty. Sovereignty is qualified by it.
  3. Article 194 - all necessary measures from any source, by the best practicable means and in accordance with capabilities; no causing damage to other States; and no transferring damage from one area to another or transforming one type of pollution into another.

The rule-making discrimination worth a mark on its own

For land-based sources (207) a State need only take international standards into account - wide discretion. For vessel-source pollution (211) it must act through IMO and its national law must have at least the same effect as the international rules. So: a coastal State may be stricter than the international standard for its own ships; it may not be laxer, and it may not impose its own construction standards on foreign ships in innocent passage. That sentence explains the whole architecture of ship-source regulation.

Enforcement - three actors, not one

  • Flag State (217) - compliance by its own ships, wherever the violation occurs.
  • Port State (218) - where the ship is voluntarily in port, may proceed for discharges outside its own waters.
  • Coastal State (220) - violations in its territorial sea or EEZ, with powers graded from requiring information, to physical inspection, to detention and proceedings on clear objective evidence of major damage.
  • Article 221 - measures beyond the territorial sea after a maritime casualty, proportionate to the damage threatened.
  • Article 235 - responsibility, liability, and the duty to ensure recourse for prompt and adequate compensation.

The sentence that joins the two limbs

Article 235(2) requires States to ensure recourse is available for prompt and adequate compensation - and the three tiers are how that duty is discharged for oil. Write it at the join and the answer reads as one argument. It is also the single best way to show you have understood why these two limbs are on the same question.

The three tiers, in the form the limb asks for

TierInstrumentWho paysSource of the funds
OneCLC 1992The registered owner, strictly liable, and in practice his P&I club - with compulsory insurance and a right of direct action against the insurerThe shipping side - the owner's resources and his insurance
TwoFund Convention 1992The 1992 IOPC Fund, where tier one gives nothing or is inadequateContributions from receivers of contributing oil received by sea in Member States above a threshold quantity - the cargo interest
ThreeSupplementary Fund Protocol 2003The Supplementary Fund, where tier two is insufficientThe same levy, but only in the States that have opted in

The one-sentence answer to "who is responsible for payment": the ship pays first and the cargo pays afterwards - twice.

Quote no money

Give no limitation figure, fund ceiling or contribution threshold. The limits are amended by tacit acceptance, so every figure carries a date, and a remembered number from a textbook is very likely to be the wrong year's. Saying "limited by reference to tonnage, to an amount amended from time to time" is both safer and more accurate than a number.

Common mistakes

  • Describing pollution loosely instead of giving the Convention's definition. The stem says 'as per UNCLOS'.
  • Omitting "or energy" and "or is likely to result" - the two phrases that make the definition wide.
  • Writing about the maritime zones. They are not asked for here, and article 192 is precisely not zone-limited.
  • Treating the coastal State as the only enforcement actor. There are three, and the flag State's duty is primary.
  • Missing the 207 against 211 discrimination between land-based and vessel-source rule-making.
  • Naming the wrong three tiers - for example putting Bunkers 2001 in as a tier. It is a separate, single-tier regime.
  • Saying the Fund is financed by governments or by shipowners. It is financed by receivers of contributing oil.
  • Forgetting that tier three is optional, so the same casualty attracts different compensation in different States.
  • Quoting monetary limits.
  • Answering only 'who pays' and never 'the source of the funds' - the stem asks for both, and they are different questions for tiers two and three.

Examiner traps

  • "Is underwater noise pollution under UNCLOS?" Yes - the definition covers substances or energy.
  • "Must damage have occurred?" No - 'results or is likely to result'.
  • "Is a natural oil seep pollution?" No - the introduction must be by man, directly or indirectly.
  • "Can India apply its own construction standards to a foreign ship in innocent passage?" No - vessel-source rules must be adopted through IMO and must not hamper innocent passage.
  • "Who may prosecute a discharge on the high seas?" The flag State always; and a port State where the ship comes voluntarily into its port.
  • "Who is liable under CLC?" The registered owner, strictly, with liability channelled to him - not the master, crew, charterer or salvor.
  • "When does the owner lose the right to limit?" His own personal act or omission, with intent, or recklessly and with knowledge that such damage would probably result.
  • "Who funds the IOPC Fund?" Receivers of contributing oil - the cargo interest - not shipowners and not governments.
  • "Your ship's bunkers pollute a beach. Which tier?" None of them - that is the Bunkers Convention 2001, a separate single-tier regime with no fund behind it.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which certificate on your ship proves compulsory insurance for oil pollution, and who checks it?
  • Your ship is a bulk carrier. Which liability regime covers a fuel oil spill from her?
  • What is 'channelling of liability' and why does it matter to you personally?
  • Give me an example of pollution by energy rather than by substance.
  • If a coastal State detained your ship after a discharge in its EEZ, what would it have to establish?
  • Who ultimately pays for a very large tanker spill?

Memory framework

Limb A - define, then duty, then rules, then enforcement. DEFINE (1): by man, directly or indirectly, substances or energy, results or is likely to result, five effects. DUTY (2): 192 free-standing, 193 sovereignty qualified, 194 best practicable means. RULES (3): 207 take into account, 211 through IMO and no less effective. ENFORCEMENT (4): 217 flag, 218 port, 220 coastal graded, 221 casualty, 235 compensation. Limb B - why, then one, then two and three, then what is outside. WHY (5): ship pays first, cargo pays after. ONE (6): owner strictly liable, insurer pays, direct action. TWO AND THREE (7): the Funds, financed by receivers of contributing oil; three is optional. OUTSIDE (8): bunkers, HNS, and geography.

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from
The definition of pollutionUNCLOS article 1(1)(4) - read verbatim from the held official text
The general obligation, and sovereignty qualified by itUNCLOS articles 192 and 193 - read verbatim
All necessary measures, best practicable means, no transfer of damageUNCLOS article 194 - read verbatim
Rule-making, source by sourceUNCLOS articles 207, 210, 211 and 212 - cited by identity
Enforcement by flag, port and coastal States; casualtiesUNCLOS articles 217, 218, 220 and 221 - cited by identity
Responsibility, liability, and recourse for compensationUNCLOS article 235 - read verbatim. This is the bridge to limb B
The three tiersCLC 1992, Fund Convention 1992, Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003. No text held by MIW; cited by identity, no article asserted, no monetary figure stated

Uncertainty and currency

Limb A is the strongest evidential position on this paper and limb B is the weakest, and the contrast is worth stating. UNCLOS has been in force since 16 November 1994 and is unamended, so the text MIW holds is the text a candidate sat this examination on. There is no reversal of any kind to perform, and the definition and articles 192, 193, 194 and 235 were read verbatim at source for this answer. The remaining Part XII articles are cited by identity, with their substance carried from MIW's own verification of the same held text; and the Part XII safeguards at section 7 are referred to only in general terms, their individual articles not having been read.

By contrast, MIW holds no text of any of the three compensation instruments. Accordingly no convention text is quoted, no article number is asserted for any of the three, and NO MONETARY OR SPECIAL DRAWING RIGHT FIGURE APPEARS ANYWHERE - not a tonnage-band limit, not a fund ceiling, and not the contributing-oil threshold quantity. That is deliberate rather than evasive. The tonnage-band limits and the fund ceilings are amended by tacit acceptance, so every figure carries a date with it, and a figure learned for one year is wrong for another. Check the current values against the IOPC Funds' own published tables before quoting any of them in an answer. The architecture is what is stable, and the architecture is what the question asks for.

That discipline is right on the merits and not merely as a matter of evidence. The limitation amounts and fund ceilings are amended by tacit acceptance over time, so every figure carries a date with it, and a figure carried from a later source into a March 2023 answer would be wrong twice over. The architecture - strict limited liability on the registered owner, then the cargo interest twice - does not move, and it is what the printed limb actually asks about.

No list of parties is given and no claim is made about which States have opted into the third tier, because membership changes continuously. No claim is made about any State's declared maritime zones, including India's. The Indian statute giving effect to these conventions at this sitting is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958; the 2025 Act is three years after this examination and is not cited.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________A. The Convention's definition of pollution, and what makes it unusually wide
  2. __________A. The general obligation, and why it is not confined to any zone
  3. __________A. The coastal State's obligations - rule-making
  4. __________A. The coastal State's obligations - enforcement, and its limits
  5. __________B. Why the oil pollution compensation regime has three tiers
  6. __________B. Tier one - the shipowner and his insurer
  7. __________B. Tiers two and three - the Funds, and who actually provides the money
  8. __________B. What the three tiers do NOT cover - and the trap
Flashcards 10

Limb A: 1 the definition and the four elements that make it wide. 2 the general obligation - articles 192, 193, 194. 3 coastal-State rule-making, source by source. 4 enforcement three ways, closing on article 235 as the bridge. Limb B: 5 why the regime is layered. 6 tier one, the owner and his insurer. 7 tiers two and three, the Funds and the cargo levy. 8 what falls outside.

The stem contains five separate instructions and the commonest failure is to answer fewer than five.

The introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water, and reduction of amenities. Article 1(1)(4).

The stem says 'as per UNCLOS', so the Convention's own words are the answer and a loose paraphrase loses the easiest marks.

Yes to the first: the definition covers the introduction of substances or energy, so heat, noise and radiation are all within it without any substance being discharged. No to the second: the phrase is 'results or is likely to result', so likelihood of a deleterious effect is enough - which is what allows the Convention to support preventive obligations rather than only remedial ones.

The two phrases that make the definition wide, and the two most often dropped in a paraphrase.

It is free-standing and not confined to any zone - the rest of the Convention allocates rights zone by zone, while article 192 imposes a duty across all of them. Article 193 then makes the sovereign right to exploit natural resources expressly subject to that duty, and article 194 supplies the standard: all necessary measures against pollution from any source, by the best practicable means and in accordance with capabilities.

Three propositions that answer the 'key features of obligations' half of limb A.

For land-based sources (article 207) it adopts national law taking international rules into account - wide discretion. For pollution from vessels (article 211) it must act through the competent international organization, and its national law must have at least the same effect as the generally accepted international rules. So a coastal State may be stricter than the international standard for its own ships, may not be laxer, and may not impose its own construction standards on foreign ships in innocent passage.

This single discrimination explains the entire architecture of ship-source pollution regulation.

Three actors. The flag State (217) - compliance by its ships wherever the violation occurs, and its duty is primary. The port State (218) - where the ship is voluntarily in port, proceedings for discharges outside its own waters. The coastal State (220) - violations in its territorial sea or EEZ, with graded powers: require information, then physical inspection, then detention and proceedings on clear objective evidence of a discharge causing or threatening major damage. Article 221 adds measures beyond the territorial sea after a casualty.

Treating the coastal State as the only enforcer misses the structure the question is testing.

Tier one - CLC 1992: the registered owner, strictly liable, and in practice his liability insurer, with compulsory insurance and a right of direct action. Tier two - the 1992 IOPC Fund, where tier one gives nothing or is inadequate. Tier three - the Supplementary Fund under the 2003 Protocol, where tier two is insufficient - and it is optional for States.

The limb asks for the tiers and for who pays in each case; both must be answered explicitly.

From contributions levied on the receivers of contributing oil - crude oil and heavy fuel oil received by sea in the territory of a Member State above a threshold quantity in a calendar year. The cargo interest pays - refineries and terminals - not shipowners and not governments. Tier three uses the same mechanism but levies only in States that have opted into the Protocol.

The stem asks for the source of the funds, and this is the point most candidates get wrong.

Channelling means liability is directed to the registered owner alone, so claims may not ordinarily be brought against the master, crew, pilot, charterer, manager or salvor - which is why the Chief Engineer is not personally exposed. The owner loses the right to limit where the damage resulted from his own personal act or omission, committed with intent to cause such damage, or recklessly and with knowledge that such damage would probably result - a deliberately very high threshold.

Channelling is what protects you personally, and the loss-of-limitation test is a standard oral question.

None of the three. The three-tier regime covers persistent oil carried in bulk as cargo - the tanker case. Bunker oil from a non-tanker falls under the Bunkers Convention 2001, which is a single tier: liability on the shipowner in a wider sense, backed by compulsory insurance and limitation under the general limitation convention, with no fund behind it.

The clearest available boundary of the regime, and the discrimination that separates this question from the Bunkers-versus-CLC ones.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Limb A - the definition, UNCLOS article 1(1)(4). The introduction BY MAN, directly or indirectly, of SUBSTANCES OR ENERGY into the marine environment, including estuaries, which RESULTS OR IS LIKELY TO RESULT in such deleterious effects as harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water, and reduction of amenities. Energy = heat, noise, radiation. No damage need have occurred. Obligations. 192 - the duty to protect and preserve is free-standing and not zone-limited. 193 - the sovereign right to exploit is subject to it. 194 - all necessary measures from any source, by the best practicable means and in accordance with capabilities; no damage to other States; no transferring damage or transforming one type of pollution into another. Rule-making: 207 land-based, take international rules into account; 210 dumping, express prior approval; 211 vessels, act through IMO and national law must be no less effective; 212 atmosphere. Enforcement three ways: 217 flag State wherever the violation occurs; 218 port State for discharges outside its waters where the ship is voluntarily in port; 220 coastal State in territorial sea or EEZ, graded from information to inspection to detention on clear objective evidence of major damage; 221 measures after a casualty beyond the territorial sea; 235 responsibility, liability and recourse for prompt and adequate compensation. Limb B - the three tiers. ONE: CLC 1992 - the registered owner, strictly liable, liability channelled to him; compulsory insurance with a certificate on board and direct action against the insurer; limitation by tonnage, lost for his personal act with intent or recklessness. Source: the shipowner and his P&I club. TWO: 1992 IOPC Fund, where tier one gives nothing or is inadequate. THREE: Supplementary Fund (2003 Protocol), where tier two is insufficient - and it is optional. Source for both: contributions from RECEIVERS OF CONTRIBUTING OIL received by sea above a threshold quantity - the CARGO INTEREST, not shipowners and not governments. Bunker oil from a non-tanker is NOT one of the tiers - that is the Bunkers Convention 2001, single tier, no fund. Quote no monetary figures.
