Critical numbers
  • UNCLOS in force 16 November 1994 and UNAMENDED - the held text is the sitting text
  • The definition is at article 1, paragraph 1(4), and lists FIVE deleterious effects
  • Enforcement is divided THREE ways - flag State 217, port State 218, coastal State 220
  • THREE tiers: CLC 1992, Fund Convention 1992, Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003
  • NO MONETARY OR SPECIAL DRAWING RIGHT FIGURE IS QUOTED ANYWHERE - the limits are amended by tacit acceptance and MIW holds no text of the three instruments
Critical regulation
UNCLOS article 1(1)(4): pollution of the marine environment means the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water and reduction of amenities. And article 235(2): States shall ensure that recourse is available in accordance with their legal systems for prompt and adequate compensation or other relief in respect of damage caused by pollution of the marine environment by natural or juridical persons under their jurisdiction - which is the obligation that the three-tier compensation regime discharges for oil.
Major trap
Writing about the maritime zones instead of about the obligations. The zones are not asked for, and article 192 is precisely not zone-limited - introducing them works against the point limb A is making. Second trap: paraphrasing the definition loosely and losing 'or energy' and 'or is likely to result', which are the two phrases that make it wide. Third: treating the coastal State as the only enforcement actor, when there are three and the flag State's duty is primary. Fourth, and the biggest on limb B: saying the Funds are financed by shipowners or by governments. They are financed by receivers of contributing oil - the cargo interest. Fifth: naming the Bunkers Convention 2001 as one of the three tiers; it is a separate, single-tier regime with no fund behind it. Sixth: forgetting that tier three is optional, so the same casualty attracts different compensation in different States. Seventh: quoting monetary limits, which are amended by tacit acceptance and therefore carry a date.

Also on the platform: QP2303 Q3 - marine insurance, and the different mechanism by which loss to the ship herself is met · QP2303 Q7 - the port State enforcement powers that UNCLOS Part XII recognises

First in set First set here. The same task returns at June 2023 Q1, September 2023 Q8, April 2024 Q9.

Longer-term signalNot set in recent papersRecurs beyond MIW’s solved setFramework has since movedCurrent-framework answer in preparation
Understand this first

The reason this subject is hard is that carbon dioxide is not like the other things that come out of a funnel. Sulphur and nitrogen oxides and soot are local problems - they damage the air where the ship happens to be, which is why the rules for them are geographical and why you can fit a scrubber and remove them. Carbon dioxide harms nobody standing next to it, and it cannot be removed at the funnel at all, because it is what burning a carbon fuel produces. Take the carbon out of the exhaust and you have taken it out of the fuel. So there are only three things anyone can do: burn less fuel, burn a fuel with less carbon in it, or burn none. Everything in this question is one of those three. IMO's plan for getting there is set out in a Strategy - which is worth understanding for what it is, because it binds nobody. It is a statement of what the Organization intends, with targets attached, and the targets only become real when somebody writes a regulation into MARPOL Annex VI to deliver them. At the time of this examination that plan had three ambitions: emissions should stop rising, the amount of carbon per tonne of cargo carried a mile should fall a long way by 2030, and the total emitted should be halved by 2050. Notice the difference between the last two, because it is the point of the whole thing: the second is a ratio and the third is a quantity, and improving the ratio while the fleet grows can leave the quantity higher than before. And then the two measures. The first, EEXI, is a piece of arithmetic done once on paper: given her engine, her hull and her design speed, how much carbon would this ship emit per tonne-mile? If the answer is too high she must be improved - and in practice most ships were made compliant simply by capping the engine power, which lowers the design speed and therefore the number. Which raises the obvious objection: a sum done on paper says nothing about how the ship is actually run. That is exactly why the second measure exists. CII looks at what she really burned and how far she really went over a whole year, and gives her a grade from A to E - a grade that charterers can see. Do badly for long enough and you must write a plan and have it approved. One measures what the ship could do; the other measures what she did.

Knowledge map
Decarbonisation in Shipping, IMO Ambitions and the Two Short-Term Measures
  1. 1What decarbonisation means in shipping
    • The progressive reduction, towards elimination, of greenhouse gas emissions from the operation of ships
    • Principally CO2, with methane and nitrous oxide, increasingly assessed across the whole fuel life cycle
    • A climate measure, not an air-quality measure - SOx and NOx are local, CO2 is global
  2. 2The instrument that frames it at this sitting
    • The Initial IMO Strategy on reduction of GHG emissions from ships, resolution MEPC.304(72), adopted 2018
    • A strategy, not a regulation - nothing in it binds a ship
    • What binds a ship is what is written into MARPOL Annex VI as a result of it
  3. 3The levels of ambition
    • Three levels, all against a 2008 baseline
    • One - peaking: GHG emissions should peak as soon as possible and decline thereafter
    • Two - carbon intensity: CO2 per transport work down at least 40 per cent by 2030, pursuing 70 per cent by 2050
  4. 4The pathway - short-term, mid-term and long-term
    • Short-term - agreed and in force: the design index, the data collection system, and EEXI and CII with the enhanced SEEMP
    • Mid-term - CANDIDATE measures under consideration, nothing adopted at this sitting
    • An implementation programme for alternative low-carbon and zero-carbon fuels
  5. 5The first short-term measure - the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index
    • Both measures were introduced by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022, applying from 1 January 2023
    • Technical, one-off, design-based - it extends the new-ship design discipline to existing ships
    • It computes the ship's theoretical CO2 per tonne-mile at a reference condition - a calculation, not a measurement
  6. 6The second short-term measure - the Carbon Intensity Indicator
    • Operational, annual, continuing - it measures what the ship actually did
    • The attained annual operational CII: CO2 from fuel actually consumed, over capacity and distance actually sailed
    • The data comes from the fuel oil consumption data collection system, which already existed
  7. 7The guidelines that make the two measures operable
    • The regulation states the requirement; the guidelines carry the arithmetic
    • G1 - MEPC.352(78): the method of calculating the attained annual operational CII
    • G2 - MEPC.353(78): the reference lines. G3 - MEPC.338(76): the reduction factors
  8. 8What the two measures do and do not achieve
    • The first mandatory GHG measures applied to the EXISTING fleet - everything earlier applied to ships not yet built
    • Both are intensity measures - neither caps total emissions, and the third ambition is absolute
    • Neither touches the fuel - they make a ship burning fossil fuel burn less of it
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

Bullet answer — points to write
  1. What decarbonisation means in shipping
    • The progressive reduction, towards elimination, of greenhouse gas emissions from the operation of ships
    • Principally CO2, with methane and nitrous oxide, increasingly assessed across the whole fuel life cycle
    • A climate measure, not an air-quality measure - SOx and NOx are local, CO2 is global
    • CO2 cannot be scrubbed out - it is the intended product of burning carbon
    • So the only routes are burn less, burn less carbon, or burn none
    • An efficiency problem before it is a fuel problem - which is why both short-term measures are efficiency measures
    • International shipping was excluded from national commitments, which is why IMO carries the mandate
  2. The instrument that frames it at this sitting
    • The Initial IMO Strategy on reduction of GHG emissions from ships, resolution MEPC.304(72), adopted 2018
    • A strategy, not a regulation - nothing in it binds a ship
    • What binds a ship is what is written into MARPOL Annex VI as a result of it
    • It carries its own programme of follow-up actions - short-term, mid-term and long-term
    • The measures' own founding resolution expressly recalls the adoption of the Initial Strategy in its preamble
    • So the two short-term measures are, in terms, delivered under that Strategy
  3. The levels of ambition
    • Three levels, all against a 2008 baseline
    • One - peaking: GHG emissions should peak as soon as possible and decline thereafter
    • Two - carbon intensity: CO2 per transport work down at least 40 per cent by 2030, pursuing 70 per cent by 2050
    • Three - total annual GHG: at least 50 per cent lower by 2050
    • Phase-out as soon as possible in this century, consistent with the Paris temperature goals
    • Level two is a RATIO; level three is a QUANTITY
    • A fleet can meet the intensity ambition and go backwards on the absolute one by carrying more
  4. The pathway - short-term, mid-term and long-term
    • Short-term - agreed and in force: the design index, the data collection system, and EEXI and CII with the enhanced SEEMP
    • Mid-term - CANDIDATE measures under consideration, nothing adopted at this sitting
    • An implementation programme for alternative low-carbon and zero-carbon fuels
    • Further operational efficiency measures; new and innovative emission reduction mechanisms, possibly including market-based measures; R&D and capacity building
    • Long-term - directional: zero-carbon or fossil-free fuels, and further innovative mechanisms
    • Two running principles: no more favourable treatment, and assessment of impacts on States
    • The honest position: the mid-term mechanism was unresolved at this sitting
  5. The first short-term measure - the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index
    • Both measures were introduced by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022, applying from 1 January 2023
    • Technical, one-off, design-based - it extends the new-ship design discipline to existing ships
    • It computes the ship's theoretical CO2 per tonne-mile at a reference condition - a calculation, not a measurement
    • Attained EEXI must not exceed required EEXI, derived from the reference line with a reduction factor. No factor quoted
    • Demonstrated once, at the first periodical survey on or after 1 January 2023, endorsed on the IEEC
    • Compliance route one: engine or shaft power limitation, sealed and verifiable, with an overridable power reserve the master may release for safety, recorded when used
    • Route two: technical improvement - energy-saving devices, propeller and hull work, waste heat recovery. Route three: a lower-carbon fuel
    • The honest criticism: it says what the ship is CAPABLE of, not what she does
  6. The second short-term measure - the Carbon Intensity Indicator
    • Operational, annual, continuing - it measures what the ship actually did
    • The attained annual operational CII: CO2 from fuel actually consumed, over capacity and distance actually sailed
    • The data comes from the fuel oil consumption data collection system, which already existed
    • Compared with a required CII from a reference line with an annual reduction factor that tightens each year
    • Result: a rating A to E, documented and visible to charterers and financiers
    • D for three consecutive years, or E for one year - a plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP, approved
    • The enhanced SEEMP carries the implementation plan, the calculation methodology and the corrective action plan, and is verified
    • Improving it: speed above all, then hull and propeller condition, trim, weather routeing, port efficiency, shore power
    • The honest criticism: it divides by capacity and distance, not by cargo carried - and the regulation provides for review
  7. The guidelines that make the two measures operable
    • The regulation states the requirement; the guidelines carry the arithmetic
    • G1 - MEPC.352(78): the method of calculating the attained annual operational CII
    • G2 - MEPC.353(78): the reference lines. G3 - MEPC.338(76): the reduction factors
    • G4 - MEPC.354(78): the rating boundaries. G5 - MEPC.355(78): the interim correction factors and voyage adjustments
    • The EEXI set - calculation, survey and certification, and shaft or engine power limitation - named by function only; no resolution number asserted
    • The 2022 SEEMP guidelines, MEPC.346(78), govern the enhanced plan at this sitting
    • Why it is structured that way: the method can be revised without amending the Convention
  8. What the two measures do and do not achieve
    • The first mandatory GHG measures applied to the EXISTING fleet - everything earlier applied to ships not yet built
    • Both are intensity measures - neither caps total emissions, and the third ambition is absolute
    • Neither touches the fuel - they make a ship burning fossil fuel burn less of it
    • They were administratively light and quickly achievable, because the data and the index already existed
    • Which is also their limitation - what could be agreed, not what would be sufficient
    • They shift commercial pressure onto the operator through a visible rating
    • A floor, not a destination - adopted under a Strategy itself under revision, and expressly subject to review

Memory cue. Define, frame, three ambitions, three phases, two measures, five guidelines, limits. DEFINE (1): climate not air quality - burn less, less carbon, or none. FRAME (2): the Initial Strategy; a strategy binds nobody, the Annex does. AMBITIONS (3): peak; intensity -40% by 2030 pursuing -70% by 2050; total GHG -50% by 2050, all on 2008. PHASES (4): short-term in force, mid and long-term candidates. MEASURES: EEXI technical, one-off, what she could do (5); CII operational, annual, A to E, what she did (6). GUIDELINES (7): calculation, reference lines, reduction factors, rating boundaries, corrective action. LIMITS (8): both intensity, neither touches the fuel.

Remember8 route headings
Cover58 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 58 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. What decarbonisation means in shipping

Decarbonisation is the progressive reduction, towards elimination, of the greenhouse gas emissions arising from the operation of ships - principally carbon dioxide from the combustion of fossil fuel, together with methane and nitrous oxide, and increasingly assessed across the whole life cycle of the fuel rather than only at the funnel.

Three distinctions belong at the start, because they decide what the rest of the answer is about:

  • It is a climate measure, not an air-quality measure. Sulphur oxides, nitrogen oxides and particulate matter are local pollutants - they harm the place they are emitted. Carbon dioxide is a global problem and is harmless to breathe. That is why the two are regulated on entirely different principles, and why an emission control area makes sense for one and not for the other.
  • Carbon dioxide cannot be scrubbed out at the funnel in the way sulphur can. It is the intended product of burning a carbon fuel. So the only routes are to burn less fuel, to burn a fuel with less carbon in it, or to burn none at all - and that is the whole of the subject.
  • It is therefore an efficiency problem before it is a fuel problem. Every tonne of fuel not burned is an emission that requires no new technology, no new bunkering infrastructure and no new safety regime. The two short-term measures in this question both attack efficiency, and neither touches the fuel.

It is worth adding one sentence of scope. International shipping was excluded from the national commitments of the climate treaties, because emissions from a ship built in one country, owned in a second, flagged in a third and trading between a fourth and a fifth cannot sensibly be allocated to any of them. That is why IMO, rather than any national government, carries the mandate - and it is why the instruments in this answer are IMO instruments.

2. The instrument that frames it at this sitting

IMO's decarbonisation policy is set out in a Strategy, which is not itself binding but which drives everything that becomes binding. The operative instrument at this sitting is the Initial IMO Strategy on reduction of GHG emissions from ships, resolution MEPC.304(72), adopted in 2018.

Two points about it are worth making explicitly, because they are what a strong answer knows:

  • It is a strategy, not a regulation. Nothing in it binds a ship. What binds a ship is what is subsequently written into MARPOL Annex VI as a result of it - which is exactly what the two short-term measures in this question are.
  • It carries its own programme of follow-up actions, dividing candidate measures into short-term, mid-term and long-term. That programme is the 'pathway' the stem asks about, and it is the reason the two measures exist at all.

The relationship is confirmed by the measures' own founding instrument: the resolution that introduced EEXI and CII into Annex VI expressly recalls the Committee's adoption of the Initial Strategy in its preamble. The two short-term measures are, in terms, delivered under that Strategy.

3. The levels of ambition

The Initial Strategy sets out three levels of ambition, all measured against a 2008 baseline. They should be given as three, and in this order:

LevelThe ambitionWhat kind of target it is
One - peakingGHG emissions from international shipping should peak as soon as possible, and be reduced thereafterA turning point, with no date attached
Two - carbon intensityThe CO2 emissions per transport work of international shipping should fall by at least 40 per cent by 2030, pursuing efforts towards 70 per cent by 2050Relative - per unit of work done, so it improves even if the fleet grows
Three - total GHGTotal annual GHG emissions from international shipping should be at least 50 per cent lower by 2050Absolute - the actual tonnage emitted, whatever the fleet does

The Strategy also expresses the ambition to phase out GHG emissions from international shipping as soon as possible in this century, and states that its levels are to be pursued in a manner consistent with the temperature goals of the Paris Agreement.

The distinction between the second and third levels carries a mark on its own and is where most candidates are vague. Carbon intensity is a ratio and total emissions are a quantity. A fleet that becomes forty per cent more efficient per tonne-mile while carrying seventy per cent more cargo has met the second ambition and gone backwards on the third. Both are needed precisely because neither implies the other, and it is the reason the two short-term measures - which are both intensity measures - are described as a first step and not as an answer.

4. The pathway - short-term, mid-term and long-term

The Strategy's programme of follow-up actions divides candidate measures into three phases. At this sitting the short-term measures are the only ones that have become law; the mid-term and long-term measures are candidate measures under consideration, and describing them as anything more would be wrong.

PhaseCharacterContent at this sitting
Short-termAgreed and in force. Technical and operational, applying to the existing fleet burning existing fuelsStrengthening the design index for new ships; the fuel oil consumption data collection system; and, as the two measures this question asks about, EEXI and CII with the enhanced SEEMP
Mid-termCandidate measures under consideration. Nothing adoptedAn implementation programme for the uptake of alternative low-carbon and zero-carbon fuels; further operational energy efficiency measures; new and innovative emission reduction mechanisms, possibly including market-based measures; research and development; technical cooperation and capacity building; and a feedback mechanism
Long-termDirectional. Beyond 2030Pursuit of the development and provision of zero-carbon or fossil-free fuels, and encouragement of other new and innovative emission reduction mechanisms

Two supporting principles run through the whole programme and are worth naming: no more favourable treatment, so that measures apply to all ships regardless of flag; and the recognition of the impacts on States, particularly developing countries, small island developing States and least developed countries, which is assessed before a measure is adopted.

The honest position at March 2023 is that the mid-term measures are the argument, not the answer. Whether the emission reduction mechanism becomes a levy, a trading scheme, a fuel standard or some combination was, at this sitting, unresolved - and a candidate who says so is more accurate than one who describes a mechanism as settled.

5. The first short-term measure - the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index

Both short-term measures were introduced into MARPOL Annex VI by resolution MEPC.328(76), which entered into force on 1 November 2022, with the requirements applying from 1 January 2023 - ten weeks before this examination. They are the first mandatory greenhouse gas measures ever applied to the existing fleet.

EEXI
What kind of measureTechnical, one-off, design-based. It extends to existing ships the discipline that the design index already imposed on new ones
What it measuresThe ship's theoretical CO2 output per tonne-mile at a reference condition, computed from installed power, specific fuel consumption, deadweight and reference speed. It is a calculation, not a measurement of the ship in service
The testThe attained EEXI must not exceed the required EEXI, which is derived from the design-index reference line for the ship type and size with a reduction factor applied. No factor is quoted here - they are type and size specific
Who it applies toExisting ships of and above the applicable size, in the ship types the design index covers - broadly the cargo-carrying fleet
When it is demonstratedOnce, at the first periodical survey on or after 1 January 2023, and endorsed on the International Energy Efficiency Certificate. Having passed, a ship does not repeat it

How a ship actually complies is the practical heart of the measure, and there are three routes:

  1. Limit the power. By far the commonest route, because it is the cheapest. Engine power limitation or shaft power limitation caps the maximum power available, which lowers the reference speed and therefore the attained index. The limitation must be verifiable and sealed, and it is accompanied by an overridable power reserve that the master may release in circumstances where the ship's safety requires full power - which is the provision every engineer should know about, and which must be recorded when used.
  2. Improve the ship technically. Energy-saving devices, propeller and hull-form modification, waste heat recovery, hull coatings, air lubrication. Effective and expensive, and generally justified only where the ship has years of trading life ahead of her.
  3. Change the fuel to one with a lower carbon factor, which changes the calculation directly.

The obvious criticism should be stated rather than avoided, because it is what an examiner is probing for. EEXI is a theoretical index calculated at a reference condition. A ship may pass it by limiting her power and then be operated inefficiently, and a ship may fail it while being operated extremely well. It says what the ship is capable of, not what she does. That gap is precisely why the second measure exists.

6. The second short-term measure - the Carbon Intensity Indicator

CII
What kind of measureOperational, annual, continuing. It measures what the ship actually did in the year
What it measuresThe attained annual operational carbon intensity - the CO2 emitted in the calendar year, derived from the fuel actually consumed, divided by the ship's capacity and the distance actually sailed
Where the data comes fromThe IMO fuel oil consumption data collection system, which the fleet had already been reporting into. The data existed before the measure did - which is why it could be introduced quickly
The testThe attained CII is compared with a required CII, obtained from a reference line for the ship type and size with an annual reduction factor that tightens each year
The resultA rating from A to E, A being the best. The rating is documented and is visible - to charterers, to financiers and to cargo interests, which is a large part of how the measure is expected to bite
Who it applies toCargo and cruise ships of and above the applicable size - a higher threshold than EEXI, so a ship may be caught by one and not the other
The consequence of a poor ratingA ship rated D for three consecutive years, or E for one year, must develop a plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP, which must be approved. This is the enforcement mechanism, and it is the reason the SEEMP was enhanced

The SEEMP is the third component and is not optional. From the same date, an enhanced Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan is required to contain the ship's implementation plan for achieving the required CII, the methodology for calculating and reporting the attained value, and, where the rating requires it, the plan of corrective actions. It is subject to verification, which is what converts the SEEMP from a document into a requirement.

How a ship actually improves her rating: speed optimisation and voyage planning - which dominates everything else, because the relationship between speed and power is steep; hull and propeller cleaning and coating condition; trim and draught optimisation; weather routeing; efficient port operations and reduced waiting at anchor; and use of shore power where it is available. The single most powerful lever an operator has is speed, and it costs nothing to install.

And the criticisms of the CII should be stated too, because they were live at this sitting. The metric divides by capacity and distance rather than by cargo actually carried, so a ship can improve her rating by sailing further, and a ship waiting fully loaded at anchor is penalised while a ship steaming empty is not. Certain trades - short sea, offshore support, ships with long idle periods - are treated poorly by a metric designed around laden voyages. The regulation itself provides for the arrangement to be reviewed, which is the correct answer to the criticism: it is a first attempt, and it is designed to be corrected.

7. The guidelines that make the two measures operable

The stem asks for the measures with their guidelines, and this is where a strong answer separates itself, because the regulation does not contain the arithmetic. The guidelines carry it, and without them neither measure could be applied to a single ship.

For the CII, the set is known as G1 to G5, and all five were in place at this sitting:

GuidelineResolutionWhat it supplies
G1MEPC.352(78)The method of calculating the attained annual operational CII - what counts as fuel, capacity and distance
G2MEPC.353(78)The reference lines against which each ship type and size is measured
G3MEPC.338(76)The reduction factors, which tighten the required value year by year
G4MEPC.354(78)The rating boundaries - where A ends and B begins, and so on
G5MEPC.355(78)The interim correction factors and voyage adjustments - what a ship may legitimately deduct from the CII calculation, and for which ship types and voyage conditions

For the EEXI, a corresponding set governs the method of calculating the attained EEXI, the survey and certification of it, and the limitation of shaft or engine power including the operation and recording of the overridable power reserve. Those guidelines are named here by function rather than by resolution number, deliberately: their numbers were not established for this answer and inventing one to match the symmetry of the CII table would be worse than leaving the gap visible.

And the SEEMP guidelines operative at this sitting are the 2022 set, resolution MEPC.346(78), which govern the content and verification of the enhanced plan. A later edition exists; it is not this one, and a candidate answering for this year should not reach for it.

The general point is worth stating in the answer: IMO regulates by putting the requirement in the Annex and the method in a guideline, so that the arithmetic can be revised as experience accumulates without amending the Convention. Recognising that structure is worth more than memorising the resolution numbers.

8. What the two measures do and do not achieve

The stem's opening asks what decarbonisation is, so the answer should close by saying honestly where these measures sit within it.

  • They are the first mandatory GHG measures applied to the existing fleet, and that is a genuine achievement. Everything before them applied to ships not yet built.
  • They are both intensity measures. They improve emissions per unit of transport work. Neither caps the total tonnage emitted, and the third level of ambition is an absolute one - so on the Strategy's own terms these two measures cannot deliver it.
  • They do not touch the fuel. They make a ship burning fossil fuel burn less of it. Decarbonisation in the full sense requires fuels that are not carbon-based, and that belongs to the mid-term and long-term measures.
  • They are administratively light and were achievable quickly, which is why they came first: the data collection system already existed, and the design index already existed. That is also their limitation - they are what could be agreed, rather than what would be sufficient.
  • They shift commercial pressure onto the operator. A published rating changes chartering behaviour faster than a regulation changes engineering, and much of the intended effect is expected to arrive through the charter market rather than through enforcement.

The fair conclusion at this sitting is that the short-term measures are a floor and not a destination. They were adopted under a Strategy that was itself under revision, they are explicitly subject to review, and the decisive questions - what the mid-term measure will be, and where zero-carbon fuel will come from - remained open. An answer that says so, and says why, is a better answer than one that presents the two measures as the solution to the problem the stem opened with.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Three instructions in one stem, and they are of different sizes. Define decarbonisation - a short paragraph. Discuss the ambitions and pathways with focus on short, mid and long-term goals - about a third of the answer. Explain the two short-term measures with their guidelines - about half. The last two words of the stem are the ones candidates skip, and they are worth real marks: the guidelines are where the arithmetic lives.

Note also that the stem prints "long tern goals". It is a printing error for 'long term' and is reproduced as printed in the transcribed question. Nothing turns on it.

Open with the three distinctions

  • Climate, not air quality. SOx, NOx and particulates are local; CO2 is global. Different problems, different regulatory logic - which is why there are emission control areas for one and not for the other.
  • CO2 cannot be scrubbed out. It is the intended product of burning carbon. So: burn less, burn less carbon, or burn none. That sentence is the whole subject.
  • Efficiency before fuel. Fuel not burned needs no new technology and no new infrastructure - which is exactly why the two short-term measures are both efficiency measures.

The three levels of ambition, and the distinction inside them

  1. Peak GHG emissions from international shipping as soon as possible.
  2. Carbon intensity - CO2 per transport work - down at least 40 per cent by 2030, pursuing 70 per cent by 2050.
  3. Total annual GHG down at least 50 per cent by 2050.

All against a 2008 baseline, with phase-out as soon as possible in this century, consistent with the Paris temperature goals. And the distinction that earns the mark: level two is a RATIO, level three is a QUANTITY. A fleet can meet the intensity ambition and go backwards on the absolute one by simply carrying more. That is why both exist - and it is why the two short-term measures, which are both intensity measures, cannot deliver the third ambition.

The pathway - and the word that keeps it accurate

Short-term = agreed and in force. Mid-term and long-term = CANDIDATE MEASURES under consideration. Use the word 'candidate' and the whole limb becomes accurate. Mid-term: an implementation programme for alternative low-carbon and zero-carbon fuels; further operational efficiency measures; new and innovative emission reduction mechanisms, possibly including market-based measures; research and development; capacity building. Long-term: zero-carbon or fossil-free fuels, and further innovative mechanisms. Add the two running principles: no more favourable treatment, and assessment of impacts on States.

The two measures in one comparison

EEXICII
TypeTechnical, design-basedOperational
FrequencyOne-off - pass it onceAnnual, and it tightens every year
What it tells youWhat the ship could doWhat the ship actually did
DataCalculated from design particulars at a reference conditionMeasured - fuel consumed, distance sailed, from the data collection system
ResultPass or fail against the required EEXI, endorsed on the IEECA rating A to E, published and visible to the market
Consequence of failingThe ship cannot be certified until she compliesD three years running, or E once - a plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP, approved
How you complyPower limitation (with an overridable reserve), technical improvement, or a lower-carbon fuelSpeed and voyage optimisation, hull and propeller condition, trim, weather routeing, port efficiency

The sentence that ties them together: EEXI says what the ship is capable of; CII says what she does. The second exists because the first can be passed and then ignored.

The guidelines - the part most candidates omit

The regulation states the requirement; the guidelines carry the arithmetic. For the CII the set runs G1 to G5: G1 calculation method, G2 reference lines, G3 reduction factors, G4 rating boundaries, G5 correction factors and voyage adjustments. If you can name the five functions you have the mark, whether or not you can recite the resolution numbers. For the EEXI there is a corresponding set on calculation, survey and certification, and shaft or engine power limitation. And the enhanced SEEMP has its own guidelines governing content and verification.

Say why it is structured that way: the requirement goes in the Annex and the method goes in a guideline, so that the arithmetic can be revised without amending the Convention. That is a structural insight and it is worth more than the numbers.

The criticisms - state them, do not avoid them

  • EEXI is theoretical. A ship can pass it by limiting power and then be run badly. It measures capability, not conduct.
  • CII divides by capacity and distance, not by cargo carried. So sailing further can improve the rating, and a fully loaded ship waiting at anchor is penalised while an empty ship steaming is not.
  • Certain trades are treated poorly - short sea, offshore, ships with long idle periods.
  • Both are intensity measures, so neither caps total emissions - which is the Strategy's own third ambition.
  • Neither touches the fuel.
  • And the fair answer to all of it: the arrangement is expressly subject to review. It is a first attempt designed to be corrected.

Common mistakes

  • Treating CO2 like SOx - proposing a scrubber, or an emission control area for carbon.
  • Giving two levels of ambition instead of three, or omitting the 2008 baseline.
  • Confusing the intensity ambition with the absolute one. They are a ratio and a quantity.
  • Describing mid-term measures as though they were adopted. They are candidate measures.
  • Naming the wrong two short-term measures - EEDI is the measure for new ships and was already there; the two asked for are EEXI and CII.
  • Saying EEXI is calculated annually. It is one-off.
  • Saying CII is a design calculation. It is operational, from actual fuel and actual distance.
  • Forgetting the D-three-years-or-E-once trigger, which is the whole enforcement mechanism.
  • Forgetting the SEEMP, which is where the corrective action plan lives.
  • Ignoring the guidelines, when the stem asks for them in terms.
  • Quoting reduction factors, reference-line values or rating boundaries as though they were general - they are ship-type and size specific.

Examiner traps

  • "Can you scrub CO2 out of your exhaust the way you do sulphur?" No - it is the intended product of combustion. Burn less, burn less carbon, or burn none.
  • "Which two short-term measures?" EEXI and CII - not EEDI, which applies to new ships and came earlier.
  • "How often do you calculate your EEXI?" Once. It is demonstrated at a survey and endorsed on the certificate.
  • "Your ship is rated E. What happens?" A plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP, which must be approved. Also D for three consecutive years.
  • "You limited engine power for EEXI. What if you need full power in heavy weather?" The overridable power reserve may be released where the ship's safety requires it - and its use must be recorded.
  • "What is the fastest way to improve your CII?" Speed. The relationship between speed and power is steep, and it costs nothing to install.
  • "Does CII measure cargo carried?" No - it divides by capacity and distance, which is the metric's principal criticism.
  • "Will these two measures achieve the total-emissions ambition?" No - both are intensity measures, and the third level of ambition is absolute.
  • "Where do the reduction factors and rating boundaries come from?" The guidelines, not the regulation - which is what lets them be revised without amending the Convention.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What is your ship's CII rating this year, and what did you do about it?
  • Show me your SEEMP and tell me what is in each part of it.
  • How was your EEXI compliance achieved, and is there a power limitation fitted?
  • How is your fuel consumption data collected and reported, and by whom is it verified?
  • What would you tell the charterer who wants an extra half knot?
  • What difference does a foul hull make to your carbon intensity, and how would you show it?

Memory framework

Define, frame, three ambitions, three phases, two measures, five guidelines. DEFINE (1): climate not air quality; burn less, less carbon, or none. FRAME (2): the Initial Strategy - a strategy binds nobody; the Annex does. AMBITIONS (3): peak; intensity -40% by 2030 pursuing -70% by 2050; total GHG -50% by 2050, all on 2008. PHASES (4): short-term in force, mid and long-term candidates. MEASURES: EEXI technical and one-off - what she could do (5); CII operational and annual, rated A to E - what she did (6). GUIDELINES (7): calculation, reference lines, reduction factors, rating boundaries, corrective action. LIMITS (8): both are intensity measures, and neither touches the fuel.

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from
The policy frame and the three levels of ambitionThe Initial IMO Strategy, resolution MEPC.304(72) of 2018 - the operative instrument at this sitting. Not held as a document; its identity is verified from the preamble of the resolution that created the two measures
The two short-term measures themselvesMARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, as introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022 and applying from 1 January 2023. Cited by identity; no provision quoted
The CII arithmeticGuidelines G1 to G5 - MEPC.352(78), MEPC.353(78), MEPC.338(76), MEPC.354(78), MEPC.355(78). All in place at this sitting
The EEXI arithmetic, survey and power limitationThe corresponding EEXI guideline set, named by function only; no resolution number is asserted
The enhanced SEEMPThe 2022 SEEMP guidelines, MEPC.346(78) - the operative set at this sitting
The data behind the CIIThe IMO fuel oil consumption data collection system, cited by identity
The Indian frameMerchant Shipping Act, 1958, administered by DG Shipping

Uncertainty and currency

This is the most date-sensitive question on the paper, and almost everything a reader will recognise from later material is wrong for it. IMO adopted a revised greenhouse gas strategy four months after this examination, and that revision replaced the levels of ambition wholesale - introducing a net-zero formulation, intermediate checkpoints for 2030 and 2040, and a vocabulary that did not exist at this sitting. None of it appears in this answer, and none of it may be added. The instrument in force here is the Initial Strategy of 2018, and its three levels of ambition are the ones stated. A reviewer reading this after July 2023 must not 'update' it.

MIW holds no copy of the Initial Strategy itself, and the way round that is worth knowing because it is a technique you can use. The resolution that introduced the two short-term measures into MARPOL Annex VI - MEPC.328(76) - expressly recalls, in its preamble, the Committee's adoption of the Initial Strategy. So the Strategy's identity, and its relationship to the two measures, are established from the instrument that created them: an IMO resolution's preamble names the policy it is delivering, which is the strongest evidence available that the two sit together at this date. The levels of ambition themselves are stated as the Strategy's levels and are not attributed to any numbered paragraph, because the document was not read directly.

The entry-into-force date is the single fact this whole question stands on, and it was taken from the resolution itself. Resolution MEPC.328(76) records deemed acceptance on 1 May 2022 at its operative paragraph 2 and entry into force on 1 NOVEMBER 2022 at its operative paragraph 3, with the requirements applying from 1 January 2023. Secondary summaries of this amendment circulate with the year wrong; if a date a year later is met anywhere, it would place both measures after this examination and make the question unanswerable, which is the quickest way to know it is wrong. Go to the resolution's operative paragraphs.

Two instruments are easy to get the wrong way round at this sitting. The 2022 SEEMP guidelines, MEPC.346(78), are the operative set here even though a later edition exists; their 2024 replacement is two years future and must not be substituted. And no provision of MARPOL Annex VI is quoted anywhere: MIW's licensed copy may be cited but not reproduced, so the measures are described in substance. No numeric reduction factor, reference-line value or rating boundary is asserted anywhere, all being ship-type and size specific; the EEXI guidelines are named by function only because their resolution numbers were not established for this answer, and inventing one to match the CII table would have been worse than showing the gap.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What decarbonisation means in shipping
  2. __________The instrument that frames it at this sitting
  3. __________The levels of ambition
  4. __________The pathway - short-term, mid-term and long-term
  5. __________The first short-term measure - the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index
  6. __________The second short-term measure - the Carbon Intensity Indicator
  7. __________The guidelines that make the two measures operable
  8. __________What the two measures do and do not achieve
Flashcards 10

1 what decarbonisation is, and the three distinctions. 2 the framing instrument - a strategy, not a regulation. 3 the three levels of ambition against 2008. 4 the three-phase pathway, with mid and long-term as candidates. 5 EEXI - technical and one-off. 6 CII - operational and annual. 7 the guidelines that carry the arithmetic. 8 what the measures do not achieve.

The stem carries three instructions of unequal size, and the last two words - 'with their guidelines' - are worth about a quarter of the question.

Because SOx, NOx and particulates are local pollutants that harm the air where they are emitted, while CO2 is a global one that is harmless to breathe - so geographic controls make no sense for it. And CO2 cannot be scrubbed out: it is the intended product of burning a carbon fuel. The only routes are therefore to burn less fuel, burn a fuel with less carbon, or burn none.

This distinction is the whole logic of the subject and explains why both short-term measures are efficiency measures.

Three, all against 2008. One: GHG emissions from international shipping should peak as soon as possible and decline thereafter. Two: carbon intensity - CO2 per transport work - down at least 40 per cent by 2030, pursuing efforts towards 70 per cent by 2050. Three: total annual GHG emissions at least 50 per cent lower by 2050. With phase-out as soon as possible in this century, consistent with the Paris temperature goals.

Three levels and the 2008 baseline are the core of the ambitions limb, and candidates routinely give only two.

Carbon intensity is a RATIO - emissions per unit of transport work - so it improves as ships get more efficient even if the fleet grows. Total annual GHG is a QUANTITY - the actual tonnage emitted. A fleet forty per cent more efficient per tonne-mile while carrying seventy per cent more cargo has met the intensity ambition and gone backwards on the absolute one. Both short-term measures are intensity measures, so neither can deliver the third ambition.

It is the sharpest analytical point available in the question and it explains the limits of the two measures.

EEXI - the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index - and CII - the Carbon Intensity Indicator - both introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022 and applying from 1 January 2023, with an enhanced SEEMP alongside them. EEDI is NOT one of them: it is the pre-existing design index for new ships, and it supplies the reference line from which the required EEXI is derived.

Naming EEDI as one of the two is the commonest factual error in this question.

A technical, one-off requirement: the ship's theoretical CO2 per tonne-mile at a reference condition, calculated from installed power, specific fuel consumption, deadweight and reference speed. The attained EEXI must not exceed the required value, derived from the design-index reference line with a reduction factor. Demonstrated once at the first periodical survey on or after 1 January 2023 and endorsed on the IEEC. Compliance by engine or shaft power limitation (sealed, with an overridable power reserve for safety), by technical improvement, or by a lower-carbon fuel.

One-off, theoretical and power-limitation are the three things that must be right, and the power reserve is the engineer's part of it.

An operational, annual requirement: the attained annual operational CII is the CO2 from the fuel actually consumed over the capacity and the distance actually sailed, taken from the fuel oil consumption data collection system, and compared with a required value that tightens each year. The ship is rated A to E. A rating of D for three consecutive years, or E for one year, requires a plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP, which must be approved.

The D-three-or-E-once trigger and the SEEMP plan together are the entire enforcement mechanism of the measure.

G1 the method of calculating the attained annual operational CII; G2 the reference lines; G3 the reduction factors; G4 the rating boundaries; G5 the interim correction factors and voyage adjustments. In resolution terms: MEPC.352(78), MEPC.353(78), MEPC.338(76), MEPC.354(78), MEPC.355(78). The regulation states the requirement and the guidelines carry the arithmetic, so the method can be revised without amending the Convention.

The stem asks for the measures 'with their guidelines', and naming the five functions earns the mark even without the numbers.

It divides by capacity and distance, not by cargo actually carried. So a ship can improve her rating by sailing further, a fully loaded ship waiting at anchor is penalised while an empty ship steaming is not, and short-sea, offshore and long-idling trades are treated poorly. The fair answer is that the arrangement is expressly subject to review - it is a first attempt designed to be corrected.

Stating the criticism with the review provision is more accurate than presenting the measure as settled, and examiners probe for it.

No, and they were never expected to. They are the first mandatory GHG measures applied to the existing fleet, which is a real achievement - but both are intensity measures, so neither caps the total tonnage emitted, and the third level of ambition is an absolute one. Neither touches the fuel. They are a floor rather than a destination, adopted quickly because the data and the index already existed, and they are expressly subject to review.

The honest limit is the closing mark, and it ties the measures back to the ambitions the stem opened with.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Decarbonisation = progressive reduction towards elimination of GHG from ship operation - mainly CO2, increasingly on a life-cycle basis. A climate measure, not air quality: SOx and NOx are local, CO2 is global and cannot be scrubbed out, because it is the product of burning carbon. So: burn less, burn less carbon, or burn none. Frame: the Initial IMO Strategy, MEPC.304(72) of 2018 - a strategy, not a regulation; what binds is MARPOL Annex VI. Three levels of ambition, all against 2008: peak as soon as possible; carbon intensity at least -40% by 2030, pursuing -70% by 2050; total annual GHG at least -50% by 2050; phase out as soon as possible this century, consistent with Paris. Level two is a ratio, level three a quantity. Pathway: short-term in force; mid-term and long-term are CANDIDATE measures - alternative low- and zero-carbon fuels, further operational efficiency, new and innovative mechanisms possibly including market-based measures, R&D and capacity building; long-term zero-carbon or fossil-free fuels. Principles: no more favourable treatment and assessment of impacts on States. The two short-term measures, introduced by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022 and applying from 1 January 2023: EEXI - technical, one-off, theoretical CO2 per tonne-mile at a reference condition; attained must not exceed required; demonstrated once at survey and endorsed on the IEEC; complied with by engine or shaft power limitation (with an overridable power reserve), technical improvement, or a lower-carbon fuel. CII - operational, annual, from actual fuel and actual distance via the data collection system; rated A to E; D for three years or E for one requires an approved plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP; improved chiefly by speed. Guidelines G1-G5: MEPC.352(78) calculation, MEPC.353(78) reference lines, MEPC.338(76) reduction factors, MEPC.354(78) rating boundaries, MEPC.355(78) correction factors and voyage adjustments. SEEMP guidelines MEPC.346(78). Both are intensity measures; neither touches the fuel.

Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, as introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), which entered into force on 1 November 2022 with the requirements applying from 1 January 2023. It carries the two short-term measures: the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index, a one-off technical requirement under which a ship's attained EEXI must not exceed the required EEXI derived from the design-index reference line with a reduction factor; and the Carbon Intensity Indicator, an annual operational requirement under which the attained annual operational CII is compared with a required value and the ship rated A to E, a rating of D for three consecutive years or E for one year requiring a plan of corrective actions in an enhanced SEEMP. The policy frame is the Initial IMO Strategy, resolution MEPC.304(72) of 2018. No provision of Annex VI is quoted here and no numeric factor is asserted.
Major trap
Answering on the wrong Strategy. IMO replaced its greenhouse gas strategy four months after this examination, and nearly every source a candidate will have seen is written on the later one - with a net-zero formulation and 2030 and 2040 checkpoints that did not exist at this sitting. The operative instrument here is the Initial Strategy of 2018, with its three levels of ambition against a 2008 baseline. Second trap: naming EEDI as one of the two short-term measures. EEDI is the pre-existing measure for new ships; the two asked for are EEXI and CII. Third: describing mid-term measures as agreed - they are candidate measures. Fourth: saying EEXI is annual (it is one-off) or that CII is a design calculation (it is operational, from actual fuel and distance). Fifth: forgetting the D-three-years-or-E-once trigger and the SEEMP corrective action plan, which together are the entire enforcement mechanism. Sixth: ignoring the guidelines, when the stem asks for them in terms - the regulation states the requirement and the guidelines carry the arithmetic. Seventh: confusing the intensity ambition with the absolute one, and so failing to see that neither short-term measure can deliver the third level of ambition.

Also on the platform: QP2303 Q4 - NOx control under the same Annex, and the difference between an air-quality measure and a climate measure · QP2303 Q5 - the power chain and the sea trial data on which any efficiency claim ultimately rests

First in set First set here. The same task returns at November 2024 Q7.

Longer-term signalNewly recurring topicActive in recent papersFramework since amendedCurrentness check pending
Understand this first

Almost everything in this question follows from one idea: the law's phrase 'perils of the sea' is much narrower than an engineer's instinct suggests. It does not mean bad luck at sea. It means something accidental, caused by the sea itself - the ship grounds, she is holed, she is knocked about by weather beyond the ordinary. Rust is not a peril of the sea, because rust was always going to happen. Cargo that was loaded wet and spoils is not a peril of the sea, because the sea had nothing to do with it. And, most importantly for you, a broken shaft or a burst boiler is not a peril of the sea either - a fact established in a real case a very long time ago, and one that left shipowners with the uncomfortable discovery that the most expensive thing that could happen to their machinery was not covered at all. The market's answer was to write an extra clause naming those machinery casualties expressly, together with the negligence of the people operating the ship. That clause still carries the name of the ship in the case that exposed the gap. But it comes with a condition: the cover holds unless the loss came from a failure of diligence by the owner and his shore management. Notice who is left out of that condition - the Master, the officers and the crew. Their carelessness is the very thing being insured, so it cannot also be the thing that voids the cover. And because that condition is a limitation on the insurer's promise rather than a precondition of it, it is the insurer who has to prove it, not you who has to disprove it. Which is why the whole argument, in the end, is fought over your maintenance records.
