Remember6 route headings
Cover38 core points beneath them

Memorise the 6 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 38 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. What 'perils of the sea' actually means

The phrase has a narrow legal meaning, and almost every mark in the first part of this question turns on knowing that it is narrow. It is not "anything bad that happens at sea". The Rules for Construction of Policy in the Schedule to the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 define perils of the seas as fortuitous accidents or casualties of the seas, and expressly exclude the ordinary action of the winds and waves.

Two tests come out of that definition, and applying them in order is the whole technique:

  • Test one - fortuity. Was the loss accidental? A loss that is inevitable, or that is the natural and expected consequence of sending a ship to sea, is not fortuitous and is not covered. Rust is not a peril of the sea. Neither is a hull that was always going to leak.
  • Test two - of the sea, not merely at sea. The sea must be the cause, not the location. A cargo that spoils in the hold because it was loaded wet has not been damaged by a peril of the sea, however far from land the spoiling happened.
Covered as a peril of the seaNOT a peril of the sea
Stranding, grounding, sinking and founderingWear and tear, and ordinary leakage and breakage
Collision, and striking a submerged object or wreckInherent vice - the goods' own tendency to deteriorate
Heavy weather damage of an extraordinary kindThe ordinary action of the winds and waves
Accidental entry of seawater through a hull opening or fittingCorrosion, and damage by vermin
Capsizing, and loss of the vessel in a casualtyMachinery damage not proximately caused by a maritime peril

The last line of the right-hand column is the one that matters most to an engineer, and it is the reason the whole of section 4 below exists. A broken crankshaft is not a peril of the sea. If it is covered at all, it is covered by a different provision of the policy - and that provision comes with the due-diligence qualification the question is ultimately asking about.

2. The maritime perils that are covered but are NOT perils of the sea

Candidates lose marks here by assuming that "perils of the sea" is the whole of the cover. It is not. It is only the first item in the perils clause of a hull and machinery policy. Everything else in that clause is listed separately precisely because the sea did not cause it, and would otherwise be uninsured:

  • Fire and explosion. Listed on its own, because fire is not a casualty of the sea at all.
  • Violent theft by persons from outside the vessel - which is theft with violence by outsiders, not pilferage and not theft by the crew.
  • Jettison - the deliberate throwing overboard of property to save the adventure.
  • Piracy.
  • Contact with land conveyance, dock or harbour equipment or installation - the ship struck by a crane or a gangway, or striking a quay fitting.
  • Earthquake, volcanic eruption or lightning.
  • Accidents in loading, discharging or shifting cargo or fuel - a cargo unit dropped into a hold, a bunker transfer that goes wrong.

So the correct shape of an answer to the first two sentences of the stem is: here is the narrow class of perils of the seas; here is the wider class of maritime perils that the policy also names; and here is what to do about everything outside both.

3. How an insured covers a peril that is not an insured peril - with examples

This is the middle limb of the question, and it wants practical answers with examples, not theory. There are four routes, and they are not alternatives to each other - a well-covered owner uses all four at once.

RouteHow it worksWorked example
Extend the existing policyAgree an additional clause with the underwriter for an additional premium, widening what the same policy responds toAdditional perils cover, which extends latent-defect protection to pay for the defective part itself and not only the damage it caused - the single most valuable extension an engineering department can ask for
Buy a separate policyThe risk is written on its own contract, because it is priced and underwritten quite differentlyWar and strikes cover, which is excluded from the ordinary hull policy; loss of hire; increased value and hull interest; and freight insurance
Cover it mutually through a P&I clubThird-party liabilities are pooled among shipowner members rather than bought from a commercial underwriterPollution liability, cargo claims, crew injury and repatriation, wreck removal, and damage to fixed and floating objects. None of these is a hull risk at all - the hull policy insures the ship, the club insures what the ship does to others
Adjust the terms rather than the perilsThe peril is already covered; what is inadequate is the amount or the condition attached to itRaise the sum insured, lower the deductible, or negotiate removal of a restrictive warranty such as a trading or lay-up warranty

And two limits must be stated, because an examiner will test them:

  • An inevitable loss is not insurable at all. Insurance transfers risk, and where there is no fortuity there is no risk to transfer - only a certainty, which is a cost.
  • A known existing defect is a repair bill, not a risk. If the owner knows the shaft is cracked, no underwriter will insure the crack. And the duty of disclosure under section 20 of the 1963 Act means concealing it puts the whole policy in jeopardy, not merely that claim.

4. The perils whose cover is qualified by due diligence - the Inchmaree group

This is the third limb, and it is the one the printed question is really driving at. The market's answer to the problem identified at the end of section 1 - that machinery casualties are not perils of the sea - is a clause the market itself calls the Inchmaree clause, after the case in which a donkey-engine casualty was held not to be a peril of the seas. The clause exists to insure exactly the losses that the narrow definition leaves out.

The perils it adds are these, and they are the direct answer to the stem's final sentence:

  1. Bursting of boilers.
  2. Breakage of shafts.
  3. Latent defect in the machinery or hull.
  4. Negligence of Master, Officers, Crew or Pilots.
  5. Negligence of repairers or charterers, provided they are not an assured under the policy.
  6. Barratry of master, officers or crew - a wilful wrongful act done to the prejudice of the owner.

And every one of them is subject to a single proviso: that the loss has not resulted from want of due diligence by the assured, owners, managers or superintendents, or any of their onshore management. Three features of that proviso carry marks on their own:

  • The Master, Officers and Crew are NOT in the diligence list. That is deliberate and it is the heart of the clause. Their negligence is the insured peril - it is what is being covered. It would be incoherent to insure crew negligence and then defeat the claim because the crew were negligent.
  • Latent defect cover pays the resulting damage, not the renewal of the defective part. If a defective bolt fails and wrecks the gearing, the gearing is paid for and the bolt is not - unless additional perils cover has been bought, as in section 3.
  • A latent defect is a defect not discoverable by the exercise of reasonable care. A defect that a proper survey or a proper inspection would have found is not latent, and is very likely to be an example of want of diligence into the bargain.

5. Where the onus of proof lies, and why it matters

The stem asks specifically about the onus of proof being on the insurer, so the allocation of the burden has to be stated as a rule and not merely asserted for this clause:

  • The assured proves that the loss occurred and that it was proximately caused by a peril insured against. That is his case, and until he makes it there is nothing to answer.
  • The insurer proves any exception, exclusion or proviso on which it seeks to rely. He who relies on a limitation must establish it.

The due-diligence provision is a proviso limiting liability, not a condition precedent to cover. It follows directly that the onus of proving want of due diligence lies on the underwriters. The assured does not have to prove he was diligent; the insurer has to prove he was not - which is a materially harder thing to do, and is why the clause is as valuable to owners as it is.

So the direct answer to the printed question is: boilers, shafts, latent defect, negligence of master, officers, crew and pilots, negligence of repairers and charterers, and barratry. Those are the perils that are covered but where the insurer bears the burden of proving want of due diligence in order to defeat the claim.

In practice these claims are fought on documents, and that is the point at which this ceases to be a legal question and becomes an engineering one. What decides them is the planned maintenance records, the defect reports and what was done about them, the superintendent's reports and correspondence, any condition of class, and the safety management system records. A defect reported by the ship, acknowledged ashore and not attended to is the most damaging document that can appear in a machinery casualty file, because it is precisely the want of diligence by shore management that the proviso is aimed at.

6. The Indian frame, and what it means on board

  • The governing statute is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963. It is modelled on the UK Marine Insurance Act 1906, and the English authorities are persuasive - but the operative statute in India is the 1963 Act, and saying "the Marine Insurance Act 1906" in an Indian examination is a straightforward error.
  • Section 19 makes marine insurance a contract of utmost good faith. Section 20 requires disclosure of every material circumstance known to the assured before the contract is concluded. Section 66 deals with general average loss.
  • The perils are in the policy, not in the statute. The Act supplies the framework and the definitions; the perils clause and the Inchmaree clause are market wording. Indian hull policies are ordinarily written on London market forms, which is why the substance is common across the market even though no single form is quoted here.
  • The shipping statute in force is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, administered by DG Shipping.
  • Your own negligence as an engineer is insured. Shore management's failure to act is what puts the claim at risk. That asymmetry is the practical lesson of the whole question.
  • On a casualty: notify promptly, preserve the failed component, photograph before anything is disturbed, and do not dismantle beyond what is necessary to make the ship safe until the surveyor has attended. The evidence that establishes a latent defect is destroyed by a tidy engine room.
Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks and three distinct questions in one stem, which is why the structure matters more here than the content. Question one: what are perils of the sea. Question two: what does an insured do about a peril that is not an insured peril. Question three: which covered perils put the onus of proof on the insurer to show want of due diligence. Answer them in that order and the answer builds; answer them as one essay on marine insurance and two of the three go unanswered.

The single most common failure is to treat "perils of the sea" as meaning everything covered by a marine policy. It is the narrowest term in the question, and the marks are in showing that you know how narrow it is.

The two tests, and why they are the whole of limb one

Fortuity, and of the sea rather than merely at sea. Rust fails the first because it is inevitable. Wet-loaded cargo fails the second because the sea did not cause it. If you can state those two tests and give an example of each failing, limb one is essentially answered before you list a single peril.

The line that leads into the rest of the question

Machinery damage is not a peril of the sea. Write that sentence, because it is the hinge of the whole answer: it is the reason the Inchmaree clause exists, and the reason the third limb has anything to ask about. A broken shaft is covered - but by a different provision, and that provision comes with a proviso.

The Inchmaree six

  1. Bursting of boilers
  2. Breakage of shafts
  3. Latent defect in machinery or hull
  4. Negligence of Master, Officers, Crew or Pilots
  5. Negligence of repairers or charterers not assured under the policy
  6. Barratry

Learn them as a list of six, because the stem's last sentence is asking for exactly this list. And remember the origin of the name: the Inchmaree was the case in which a donkey-engine casualty was held not to be a peril of the seas - the clause was written to fill the gap the decision exposed.

The proviso, and the omission inside it

The proviso is want of due diligence by the assured, owners, managers or superintendents. Notice who is not in that list: the Master, Officers and Crew. Their negligence is the insured peril itself, so it cannot also be the thing that defeats the claim. If you can explain why they are absent, you have shown that you understand the clause rather than memorised it.

Burden of proof - state it as a general rule first

The assured proves the loss was caused by an insured peril. The insurer proves any exception, exclusion or proviso it relies on. The due-diligence provision is a proviso, not a condition precedent - so the onus of proving want of due diligence is on the underwriters. Deriving the answer from the general rule scores better than simply asserting it, because it shows the reasoning that makes it true.

Common mistakes

  • Treating "perils of the sea" as everything a marine policy covers. It is the narrowest term in the question.
  • Forgetting the ordinary action of the winds and waves exclusion, which is expressly in the statutory definition.
  • Answering the middle limb with theory instead of examples - the stem asks for them by name.
  • Omitting the P&I club entirely, and so leaving pollution, cargo, crew and wreck-removal liabilities with no home.
  • Listing the Inchmaree perils but not the proviso, or the proviso but not the perils.
  • Putting the Master and crew into the due-diligence list. Their negligence is the insured peril.
  • Saying latent defect cover pays for the defective part. It pays for the resulting damage unless additional perils cover is bought.
  • Putting the onus on the assured. It is on the insurer, because it is relying on a proviso.
  • Citing the UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 as the governing statute in an Indian examination. It is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963.

Examiner traps

  • "Is heavy weather a peril of the sea?" Extraordinary heavy weather, yes. The ordinary action of the winds and waves, expressly no.
  • "Your main engine crankshaft breaks at sea. Peril of the sea?" No - but very probably covered under the Inchmaree clause, subject to due diligence.
  • "Who proves want of due diligence?" The underwriters. It is a proviso limiting liability, and he who relies on a limitation must prove it.
  • "Is the Chief Engineer's negligence covered?" Yes - crew negligence is an insured peril, and the crew are deliberately absent from the diligence list.
  • "Latent defect - do you get a new part?" No, only the resulting damage - unless additional perils cover has been purchased.
  • "Which Act governs in India?" The Marine Insurance Act, 1963. The 1906 Act is the model, not the law.
  • "How do you cover pollution liability?" Not on the hull policy at all - through the P&I club.
  • "What kills a machinery claim faster than anything else?" A defect reported by the ship, acknowledged ashore and never attended to.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Give me an example of a loss at sea that is not a loss by a peril of the sea.
  • What is barratry, and give me an example of it.
  • Your turbocharger fails from a manufacturing flaw nobody could have found. What is covered, and what is not?
  • What documents would an underwriter's surveyor ask you for after a bearing failure?
  • Why is war risk not on the hull policy?
  • What would you do in the first hour after a machinery casualty, before anyone tells you to?

Memory framework

Narrow, wider, outside, and the proviso. NARROW (1): perils of the seas - fortuitous, and of the sea. WIDER (2): the rest of the perils clause - fire, theft, jettison, piracy, contact, earthquake and lightning, loading accidents. OUTSIDE (3): extend the policy, buy a separate one, go to the club, or change the terms. THE PROVISO (4 and 5): the Inchmaree six - boilers, shafts, latent defect, negligence of crew, negligence of repairers, barratry - covered unless the insurer proves want of diligence by the assured, owners, managers or superintendents. Then the Indian frame (6).

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from
The definition of perils of the seasRules for Construction of Policy in the Schedule to the Marine Insurance Act, 1963. The rule number is deliberately not asserted
Utmost good faith and the duty of disclosureMarine Insurance Act, 1963 sections 19 and 20
General average lossMarine Insurance Act, 1963 section 66
The perils clause and the Inchmaree clauseLondon market policy forms - market wording, not legislation. No clause number and no edition is asserted
Third-party liabilities - pollution, cargo, crew, wreck removalP&I club mutual cover, an established market structure rather than a statutory scheme
The burden of proof allocationSettled legal principle. No case authority is cited, because none is held
The Indian shipping frameMerchant Shipping Act, 1958, administered by DG Shipping

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

This is one of the most temporally stable questions on the paper, and deliberately so. The governing statute is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 and it did not move in the relevant period; the doctrine relied on - the fortuity test, the burden rule and the diligence allocation - is settled and long predates the sitting. The UK Insurance Act 2015 reformed the avoidance remedy in English law and appears nowhere here, because it is not Indian law. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 is three years after this sitting and is not cited.

The clause layer is deliberately undated, and that is a strength rather than an evasion. MIW holds no Institute Time Clauses, no International Hull Clauses and no policy wording. Several editions ran concurrently in the market and their numbering differs between them, so no clause number and no edition is asserted anywhere and the perils are enumerated by their substance and their grouping - which is common to all the forms in use. An answer that named a clause number would be more precise-looking and less accurate.

Equally, the statutory rule number carrying the definition of perils of the seas is not asserted, because the Act's text is not held: the rule is stated as law and its address is not. Only sections 19, 20 and 66 are cited, being the sections that could be established with confidence; the section governing subrogation is not cited, because its number could not be confirmed and a wrong section number is worse than none. No Indian case authority is cited, because none is held - the Inchmaree case is named only as the origin of the market's own label for the clause.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What 'perils of the sea' actually means
  2. __________The maritime perils that are covered but are NOT perils of the sea
  3. __________How an insured covers a peril that is not an insured peril - with examples
  4. __________The perils whose cover is qualified by due diligence - the Inchmaree group
  5. __________Where the onus of proof lies, and why it matters
  6. __________The Indian frame, and what it means on board
Flashcards 10

1 What perils of the sea means - the two tests. 2 The wider maritime perils in the same clause. 3 How to cover an uninsured peril, with examples. 4 The Inchmaree group and its proviso. 5 Where the onus of proof lies. 6 The Indian frame and conduct after a casualty.

The stem contains three separate questions. Answering them in order builds the answer; running them together leaves two of the three unanswered.

Fortuitous accidents or casualties of the seas, expressly not including the ordinary action of the winds and waves - the Rules for Construction of Policy in the Schedule to the Marine Insurance Act, 1963. Test one, fortuity: accidental, not inevitable. Test two, of the sea and not merely at sea: the sea must be the cause and not just the place.

The narrowness of the term is the whole of the first limb, and both tests are needed to apply it.

No. Machinery damage not proximately caused by a maritime peril is not a peril of the sea - which is the very point established in the Inchmaree case. It is very probably covered nonetheless, under the Inchmaree clause as breakage of shafts or as latent defect, subject to the due-diligence proviso.

This is the hinge of the question: the gap in the narrow definition is exactly what the third limb is about.

Extend the policy for extra premium - additional perils cover, which buys back the defective part itself. Buy a separate policy - war and strikes, loss of hire, increased value, freight. Cover it mutually through a P&I club - pollution, cargo claims, crew injury and repatriation, wreck removal, damage to fixed and floating objects. Or adjust the terms - sum insured, deductible, removal of a restrictive warranty.

The middle limb asks for examples by name, and the P&I club is the one most often omitted entirely.

Bursting of boilers; breakage of shafts; latent defect in machinery or hull; negligence of Master, Officers, Crew or Pilots; negligence of repairers or charterers not assured under the policy; and barratry. All six are subject to the proviso that the loss did not result from want of due diligence by the assured, owners, managers or superintendents.

This list IS the direct answer to the final sentence of the printed stem.

Because their negligence is the insured peril. The clause exists to cover crew and pilot negligence, so it would be incoherent to then defeat the claim on the ground that the crew were negligent. The diligence obligation is placed on the assured, owners, managers and superintendents - the shore management.

Explaining the omission proves the clause is understood rather than memorised, and it is a standard oral question.

The underwriters. The general rule is that the assured proves the loss was proximately caused by an insured peril, and the insurer proves any exception, exclusion or proviso on which it relies. The due-diligence provision is a proviso limiting liability, not a condition precedent to cover - so the burden of establishing it falls on the party relying on it.

Deriving the answer from the general rule scores better than asserting it, and the stem asks about the burden specifically.

The resulting damage to the gearing - not the renewal of the defective part itself. The part is bought back only if additional perils cover has been purchased as an extension. And a defect that a reasonable inspection would have found is not latent at all, and is likely to be evidence of want of diligence into the bargain.

The distinction between the resulting damage and the defective part is where the money is, and it is regularly got wrong.

The Marine Insurance Act, 1963. The UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 is the model on which it was drawn and is not the operative statute. Name section 19 (utmost good faith), section 20 (disclosure of every material circumstance) and section 66 (general average loss). The perils themselves are in the policy, which is market wording, not in the statute.

Citing the 1906 Act in an Indian examination is a straightforward error, and the statute/policy division is a real mark.

Notify promptly through the Master and the Company. Preserve the failed component and photograph everything before it is disturbed. Do not dismantle beyond what is needed to make the ship safe until the surveyor has attended. Gather the planned maintenance records, defect reports and correspondence. The claim will be decided on documents and on the failed part, and a tidy engine room destroys both.

The whole due-diligence argument is fought on the maintenance record, so preserving it is an engineering duty with a commercial consequence.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Perils of the sea = fortuitous accidents or casualties of the seas, per the Rules for Construction of Policy in the Schedule to the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 - NOT the ordinary action of the winds and waves. Two tests: fortuity, and of the sea rather than merely at sea. In: stranding, grounding, sinking, foundering, collision, striking a submerged object, extraordinary heavy weather, accidental seawater entry, capsizing. Out: wear and tear, ordinary leakage and breakage, inherent vice, corrosion, vermin - and machinery damage. The perils clause also names, separately: fire and explosion, violent theft by outsiders, jettison, piracy, contact with dock or harbour equipment, earthquake and lightning, accidents in loading and discharging. To cover an uninsured peril: extend the policy (additional perils cover buys back the defective part), buy a separate policy (war and strikes, loss of hire, freight), go to the P&I club (pollution, cargo, crew, wreck removal, fixed and floating objects), or change the terms (sum insured, deductible, warranties). The Inchmaree six: bursting of boilers, breakage of shafts, latent defect, negligence of Master/Officers/Crew/Pilots, negligence of repairers or charterers, barratry - all subject to want of due diligence by the assured, owners, managers or superintendents. The crew are deliberately NOT in that list, because their negligence is the insured peril. Latent defect pays the resulting damage, not the part. Burden: the assured proves the insured peril; the insurer proves the proviso - so the onus of proving want of due diligence is on the underwriters. Indian statute: 1963, not 1906.
Keywords

Critical numbers
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 - the Indian statute. NOT the UK Act of 1906, which is the model only
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 sections 19, 20 and 66 - the only section numbers asserted in this answer
  • The Inchmaree group is SIX perils: boilers, shafts, latent defect, negligence of crew and pilots, negligence of repairers and charterers, barratry
  • NO clause number and NO policy edition is asserted anywhere - the perils are enumerated by substance
Critical regulation
The Rules for Construction of Policy in the Schedule to the Marine Insurance Act, 1963: 'perils of the seas' refers only to fortuitous accidents or casualties of the seas and does not include the ordinary action of the winds and waves. The rule number is deliberately not asserted, because the Act's text is not held by MIW - the rule is stated as law and its address is not.
Major trap
Treating 'perils of the sea' as everything a marine policy covers. It is the narrowest term in the question, and the marks are in showing how narrow. Second trap: putting the Master, Officers and Crew into the due-diligence list - they are deliberately absent, because their negligence is the insured peril itself. Third: putting the onus on the assured. The due-diligence provision is a proviso limiting liability, so the underwriters must prove want of diligence. Fourth: saying latent defect cover replaces the defective part - it pays the resulting damage unless additional perils cover has been bought. Fifth, and fatal in an Indian examination: citing the UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 as the governing statute. It is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963.

Also on the platform: QP2303 Q1 - the compensation conventions, where liability is met by a different mechanism from insurance of the ship · QP2303 Q8 - the classification society whose condition of class can decide a due-diligence argument

First in set First set here. The same task returns at March 2025 Q5, July 2025 Q5.

Longer-term signalNewly recurring topicActive in recent papersRising topicNo currentness risk flagged
Understand this first

The first thing to get straight is where the nitrogen comes from, because it explains everything else. It is not in the fuel in any quantity that matters - it comes out of the air the engine breathes, which is four-fifths nitrogen and normally quite happy to stay that way. What changes that is heat. Make the flame hot enough and the nitrogen and oxygen in the charge start combining, and the hotter it gets the more dramatically that happens. So every method of controlling NOx is really a way of doing one of three things: making the flame cooler, giving it less oxygen to work with, or giving it less time at its hottest. And here is the cruel part, which is the reason this is an engineering problem rather than a solved one: a hot, fast, oxygen-rich burn is exactly what a thermally efficient diesel engine wants. Everything you do to reduce NOx costs you fuel, one way or another. That is why the industry ended up with two philosophies. Either you interfere with the combustion and accept the penalty - which people call a primary measure - or you let the engine burn as it pleases and clean up the exhaust afterwards, which is a secondary measure. And the classification is about what happens to the NOx, not about where the machinery is bolted. Exhaust gas recirculation has a scrubber, a cooler and a blower all sitting outside the engine, and it is still a primary measure - because what it actually does is send some used exhaust back round into the charge, which both cools the burn and starves it of oxygen, so the NOx is never made in the first place. Scavenge air moisturizing does the same job more simply, by loading the incoming air with water vapour: the water soaks up heat as it evaporates, the burn runs cooler, and less NOx forms. Both leave you with the same practical problem down in the engine room - water and acid in places the engine was not designed to have them, which is why the cylinder oil and the scrape-down samples become the thing you watch.

Memory cue. Three levers, two families, two systems. LEVERS (1): temperature, oxygen, time, and NOx trades against fuel. FAMILIES: PRIMARY = less created - timing, Miller, water, EGR (2); SECONDARY = destroyed after - essentially SCR alone (3); compare on where, engine, plant, consumable, reach, failure (4); then the tier frame (5). SYSTEMS: SAM = water vapour, temperature lever, mist catcher (6); EGR = cleaned exhaust, two levers, scrubber and blower (7); both leave water and acid in the cylinder (8).

Remember8 route headings
Cover57 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 57 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. Why NOx forms at all, and the three levers every measure pulls

Nothing in this question can be organised sensibly until one fact is stated: the nitrogen in marine diesel exhaust comes overwhelmingly from the air, not from the fuel. At the temperatures reached in the flame, atmospheric nitrogen and oxygen combine. The rate at which they do so rises very steeply with peak temperature, and depends besides on how much oxygen is available and on how long the gas stays hot.

So there are exactly three levers, and every measure described in the rest of this answer pulls at least one of them:

LeverWhy it mattersHow it is pulled
Peak temperatureBy far the strongest influence - formation rises very steeply with itIntroduce something into the charge that absorbs heat or raises its specific heat; spread the burn out in time
Oxygen availabilityNitrogen cannot oxidise without free oxygen at the flame frontDisplace part of the oxygen in the charge with an inert gas
Residence timeThe reaction needs time at temperature; a short, sharp peak produces less than a long plateauChange injection timing, rate and duration so the peak is shorter

And one trade-off governs the whole subject. The conditions that suppress NOx - a lower, later, cooler burn - are broadly the conditions that reduce thermal efficiency. NOx and fuel consumption pull against each other, and every primary measure described below is bought partly with fuel. That trade-off is what makes the primary-versus-secondary comparison the limb asks for a real engineering decision rather than a preference.

2. A) Primary measures - preventing NOx from forming

A primary measure acts inside the engine, on the combustion process, so that less NOx is ever created. The exhaust leaving the cylinder is already cleaner. They divide into three families:

(i) Measures acting on the combustion event itself.

  • Retarded injection timing. Injecting later moves the burn further down the expansion stroke, so it takes place in a larger volume at lower peak pressure and temperature. It is the simplest measure of all, it needs no new plant, and it is the classic illustration of the trade-off: it costs fuel directly.
  • Rate shaping and injection pressure. Higher injection pressure with a shaped or multi-event delivery gives better atomisation and shortens the diffusion burn, reducing the time spent at peak temperature. Common-rail injection makes timing and rate independent of engine speed and load, which is what allows an engine to be optimised at part load rather than only at full.
  • Miller timing with high-pressure charging. Closing the inlet early gives an expansion inside the cylinder before compression begins, lowering the charge temperature at the start of compression and therefore the peak. It requires a higher charge-air pressure to keep the mass of air up, so it is bought with turbocharger capability rather than with fuel.
  • Combustion chamber, nozzle and swirl geometry, and higher compression ratio with adjusted timing - all design measures rather than operating measures, fixed at the drawing board.

(ii) Measures adding water to the process. Water is the most effective single tool available in the cylinder, because it absorbs a very large quantity of heat as it evaporates and because water vapour has a higher specific heat than air:

  • Fuel-water emulsion - water emulsified into the fuel before injection, so it enters exactly where the flame is.
  • Direct water injection - water injected into the cylinder through its own nozzle, separately timed from the fuel.
  • Scavenge air moisturizing - water evaporated into the scavenge air, so the whole charge carries it in. This is limb B and is treated at section 6.

(iii) Measures acting on the composition of the charge. Principally exhaust gas recirculation, which displaces oxygen and raises the specific heat of the charge at the same time - two levers with one system. It is limb B and is treated at section 7. Gas operation in a dual-fuel engine belongs in this family too, since a lean premixed gas burn is inherently a lower-temperature one.

3. A) Secondary measures - removing NOx after it has formed

A secondary measure lets the engine burn as it likes and treats the exhaust afterwards. The combustion is untouched, so the fuel penalty of the primary measures is avoided - but a plant now sits in the exhaust system and has to be paid for, accommodated, and kept running.

Selective catalytic reduction is the only mature secondary measure for NOx, and in practice the limb's second half is about it. A reducing agent - ammonia, normally carried and dosed as an aqueous urea solution - is injected into the exhaust stream upstream of a catalyst. Over the catalyst the nitrogen oxides and the reagent react to nitrogen and water vapour, both harmless. The essential characteristics are these:

  • It is temperature-sensitive. The reaction requires the exhaust to be within a working temperature band. Too cold and conversion collapses; too hot and the catalyst degrades. On a two-stroke engine that constraint largely dictates where in the exhaust system the reactor is placed - before the turbocharger, where the gas is hot, or after it, where it is not - and each position brings its own difficulties of pressure, sizing and transient behaviour.
  • It is sulphur-sensitive. Sulphur in the fuel leads to salts forming on the catalyst at low temperature, which blind it. Low-sulphur fuel and a maintained temperature are therefore part of the operating condition, not optional refinements.
  • Dosing must be right. Too little reagent and the conversion is incomplete; too much and unreacted ammonia passes out of the funnel - ammonia slip - which is both an emission in itself and a smell nobody can ignore.
  • It brings a consumable and a tank. Reagent has to be bunkered, stored, heated in some climates and dosed, and the ship has a new supply chain to manage.
  • It occupies space and creates back pressure, both of which are severe constraints in a retrofit and are why the reactor is often the single largest item in a Tier III installation.

Exhaust gas scrubbing is not a NOx measure and must not be offered as one - a scrubber addresses sulphur oxides and particulate matter and has essentially no effect on nitrogen oxides. Non-thermal plasma and other electro-chemical routes exist in development but are not the answer to a question about ships in service.

4. A) Primary against secondary - the comparison the limb asks for

The stem says vis-a-vis, so the marks are in the comparison and not in two descriptions placed side by side. Six axes carry it:

PRIMARYSECONDARY
Where it actsInside the cylinder, on the combustion process. Less NOx is createdIn the exhaust system, after the cylinder. NOx is destroyed once created
Effect on the engineThe combustion is deliberately altered, so fuel consumption generally rises - the NOx-efficiency trade-offCombustion is untouched, so no direct fuel penalty from the process itself
Plant requiredLittle or none for timing measures; a water or gas system for the othersA substantial installation - reactor, dosing plant, reagent tanks - with weight, space and back pressure
ConsumablesWater, treated, for the water-based measures. No chemical reagentA chemical reagent that must be bunkered, stored and dosed, with its own supply chain
Reduction achievableModest to substantial. Timing measures alone are modest; water-based measures and EGR reach much furtherThe largest single reduction available, which is why it is the standard Tier III route
Failure modeDegrades gradually and shows up as consumption or as combustion conditionCan fail as a system - a blinded catalyst or a dosing failure removes the whole benefit at once

Three points are worth stating explicitly because they are what the comparison is for.

  1. Primary measures are cumulative and are used together. No single one is enough; an engine meeting a strict limit is using several at once, and they were designed in rather than added on.
  2. The dividing line is not where the equipment sits but where the NOx goes. EGR is fitted outside the cylinder and looks like after-treatment, but it is a primary measure, because it prevents formation rather than removing what has formed. That is precisely the discrimination this limb is testing, and it is the commonest error in the question.
  3. They are complements rather than rivals. A modern installation uses primary measures to reach the ordinary limit economically, and adds a secondary measure - or a heavy primary one such as EGR - only to reach the strict limit, and often only in the areas where the strict limit applies.

5. A) The regulatory frame that decides which you need

The stem opens on exhaust emission control being a major global issue under serious consideration by world shipping, so the answer should place the engineering in its regulatory frame - briefly, and without pretending to quote.

NOx from marine diesel engines is controlled by MARPOL Annex VI regulation 13. At this sitting the applicable text is the Revised Annex VI introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), which entered into force on 1 November 2022. The structure is a tiered one:

  • There are three tiers of progressively stricter limits, and the limit in each tier is expressed as a function of the engine's rated speed - a slow-running engine being permitted more than a fast-running one, because of how the limits were framed.
  • Which tier applies is fixed by the ship's construction date, and for the strictest tier also by where she is operating: it bites when the ship is inside a designated NOx emission control area, and the less strict tier applies outside it.
  • Because the strict tier can be geographic, an engine may run in two modes, and the changeover - with date, time and position - has to be recorded.
  • The regulation applies above a stated engine output threshold and excludes certain emergency and survival-craft engines.
  • Compliance is established at certification under the revised NOx Technical Code 2008, and is demonstrated in service through the engine's NOx Technical File and its record book of engine parameters - which is why an unrecorded modification to injection timing or to a nozzle is a compliance matter and not merely an engineering one.

No limit value is quoted here, and that is deliberate. The limits are set out in the instrument as functions of rated speed; MIW holds Annex VI in a form that may be cited but not quoted from, so the tiers are given by what fixes which one applies and by their relative severity rather than by their numbers. What the candidate must carry away is the practical consequence: the strictest tier is a step change rather than an increment, and it is not reachable by injection timing and combustion design alone. It requires either a heavy primary measure such as exhaust gas recirculation, or a secondary measure - which is exactly why limb B asks about the two systems it does.

6. B) Scavenge Air Moisturizing

Principle. Water is introduced into the scavenge air after the charge air cooler and evaporated into it, so that the air delivered to the cylinder is heavily laden with water vapour. It acts on the temperature lever in two ways at once: the water absorbs a large quantity of latent heat as it evaporates, and the vapour it forms has a higher specific heat than air, so the same heat release produces a lower temperature rise. The peak combustion temperature falls, and with it the rate of NOx formation.

Arrangement. The essentials are the same whatever the commercial implementation:

  • A humidification section in the scavenge air path, downstream of the cooler, in which water is atomised into the air stream and evaporated - often in more than one stage, and often with the air re-heated between stages so that it can absorb more.
  • A water supply and treatment plant. Seawater may be used for the bulk duty in some arrangements, but the water that reaches the cylinder must be fresh and clean: salt carried into the cylinder is an unmitigated disaster for the liner, rings and exhaust valve.
  • A water mist catcher after the humidification section, so that what enters the cylinder is vapour and not droplets. This is the safety-critical item in the system.
  • A circulating, collecting and drain arrangement for the water that does not evaporate, and control of the humidification against engine load.

What it costs, and what has to be watched.

  • Corrosion. A wet charge in the presence of combustion acids is corrosive. The scavenge space, the receiver, the liner and the exhaust side all see more moisture than they were designed around, and the cylinder oil is asked to do more.
  • Cylinder lubrication. Water washing the liner disturbs the oil film. Feed rate, oil selection and residual base number need watching, and scrape-down analysis is the way it is watched - which is the direct link to the oil analysis question on this paper.
  • Fresh water demand, and the generating or bunkering capacity to meet it.
  • A small efficiency penalty, since the charge is being deliberately cooled and part of the heat release is going into water rather than into work.
  • Control complexity - the humidification must follow load changes, and a system that lags on a rapid manoeuvre either over-wets or does nothing.

Where it sits. Scavenge air moisturizing is a primary measure, and a substantial one - considerably more effective than timing measures alone. But on its own it does not usually reach the strictest tier, and it is used as part of a package rather than as a complete answer to it.

7. B) Exhaust Gas Recirculation

Principle. A proportion of the exhaust gas is cleaned, cooled and returned to the scavenge air. The charge entering the cylinder therefore contains recirculated products of combustion in place of part of the fresh air, and that does two things simultaneously - which is what makes EGR unusually powerful:

  1. It reduces the oxygen concentration of the charge. Less free oxygen at the flame front means less nitrogen oxidised. That is the oxygen lever.
  2. It raises the specific heat of the charge, because carbon dioxide and water vapour both absorb more heat per degree than air does. The same heat release therefore produces a lower peak temperature. That is the temperature lever.

Arrangement on a two-stroke main engine. This is where EGR becomes a system rather than a pipe, and the reason is that marine exhaust is dirty:

  • A scrubber in the recirculation loop, washing sulphur oxides, soot and particulate out of the gas before it goes anywhere near the scavenge air. Without it the recirculated gas would carry acid and abrasive straight back into the cylinder.
  • A cooler, since hot recirculated gas would defeat the purpose.
  • A water mist catcher, so that scrubbing water does not follow the gas into the engine.
  • An EGR blower, because in a uniflow two-stroke the exhaust is at a lower pressure than the scavenge air and the gas will not flow the right way on its own.
  • Shut-off and change-over valves, so that the system can be brought in and out as the ship enters and leaves the area where the strict limit applies.
  • Turbocharger arrangements to suit, since diverting exhaust away from the turbine upsets the air balance - typically by cutting out a turbocharger, by variable geometry, or by running auxiliary blowers.
  • A bleed-off water treatment plant. The scrubbing water becomes acidic and laden with soot and metals; it must be treated and monitored before any discharge, and the residue lands in the sludge tank.

What it costs, and what has to be watched.

  • Fouling of the cooler and the loop, which is continuous and is the main maintenance burden.
  • Acid corrosion throughout the wet parts of the loop, and an increased acid load in the cylinder if the scrubbing is not doing its job.
  • Cylinder condition. Recirculated soot reaching the cylinder increases wear and loads the oil - again, scrape-down analysis is how it is watched.
  • Complexity and reliability. There is a great deal of plant, much of it wet and acidic, and it must work when the ship enters the area, not merely when it was last tested.
  • Some efficiency penalty, and a real one in blower power.
  • Waste streams - bleed-off water and sludge - and the monitoring and record-keeping that go with them.

Where it sits, and the point most often got wrong. EGR is a PRIMARY measure. All its equipment is outside the cylinder, so it looks like after-treatment - but it acts by preventing formation, not by removing what has formed, and that is what classifies it. It is capable of reaching the strictest tier on its own, which is why it stands alongside selective catalytic reduction as one of the two practical routes to it.

8. B) SAM against EGR - what each costs to fit and to run

Scavenge Air MoisturizingExhaust Gas Recirculation
Lever pulledTemperature only - latent heat and higher specific heatTemperature AND oxygen - which is why it reaches further
What is added to the chargeWater vapourCleaned, cooled exhaust gas
Plant complexityModerate - humidifier, water treatment, mist catcher, controlsHigh - scrubber, cooler, mist catcher, blower, valves, turbocharger arrangements, bleed-off water plant
ConsumableFresh waterNo reagent, but blower power, treatment chemicals and sludge handling
ReachSubstantial, but normally part of a package rather than a complete answer to the strictest tierCapable of the strictest tier on its own
Principal riskWater carry-over into the cylinder, and disturbance of the cylinder oil filmFouling and acid corrosion in the loop, and soot returned to the cylinder
Waste streamDrain water onlyAcidic bleed-off water and sludge, with monitoring and records

What they have in common is the more useful observation. Both work by making the combustion cooler; both put water and acid where the designer of a dry engine did not intend them; and both therefore transfer a burden onto cylinder lubrication and monitoring. Whichever is fitted, the engineer's routine work is the same - keep the water where it belongs, keep the mist catcher in order, watch the cylinder condition, and read the scrape-down analysis. The system reduces the emission; the watchkeeping is what stops it costing a liner.

And the closing point, which answers the stem's opening sentence: neither of these is an optional refinement. They exist because the tiered limits under Annex VI cannot be met by combustion tuning alone, and because the strictest tier applies precisely where ships are closest to the people affected by what comes out of the funnel.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Limb A prints (8) and limb B prints no marks at all. Read that as eight and eight and treat them as equal; it is what the rubric requires, and the printed asymmetry is a printing feature of this paper rather than a marking instruction. Limb A says vis-a-vis, which means compare - two descriptions placed side by side is not a comparison, and the marks are in the axes along which they differ. Limb B names two technologies and expects each to be treated properly: principle, arrangement, and consequences.

Open with the formation mechanism, because it makes everything afterwards derivable rather than memorised. If the three levers are written down first, every measure in the answer can be explained by which lever it pulls.

The three levers

TEMPERATURE, OXYGEN, TIME. Nitrogen and oxygen from the air combine at flame temperature; the rate rises very steeply with peak temperature and depends on available oxygen and on residence time. Every primary measure pulls one of those three. Write the three words at the top of the answer and refer back to them.

The trade-off that governs the whole subject

NOx and fuel consumption pull against each other. The conditions that suppress NOx - lower, later, cooler burning - are broadly the conditions that reduce thermal efficiency. That single sentence explains why anyone bothers with after-treatment at all, and it is the reason the comparison in limb A is a real engineering decision.

The definition that decides limb A

PRIMARY = less NOx is created. SECONDARY = NOx created is then destroyed. Not 'inside the engine' against 'outside the engine' - that formulation gets EGR wrong, and getting EGR wrong is the single commonest error in the question. EGR is a PRIMARY measure even though every piece of its equipment is outside the cylinder, because it prevents formation.

Primary measures - three families

  • Combustion event - retarded timing (the classic, and it costs fuel); injection pressure and rate shaping; common rail; Miller timing with high-pressure charging; chamber, nozzle and swirl design.
  • Adding water - fuel-water emulsion, direct water injection, scavenge air moisturizing. Water is the most effective in-cylinder tool because of latent heat plus higher specific heat.
  • Changing the charge - EGR, and lean premixed gas operation in a dual-fuel engine.

Secondary - essentially one technology

Selective catalytic reduction. Reagent (urea solution, giving ammonia) injected upstream of a catalyst; NOx and reagent react to nitrogen and water. Remember four things: it needs a temperature window (which decides whether the reactor goes before or after the turbocharger); it is sulphur-sensitive, because salts blind the catalyst; dosing must be right or you get ammonia slip; and it brings a reagent, a tank, space and back pressure. And say plainly that a scrubber is not a NOx measure - it is for sulphur oxides and particulates.

The comparison in six axes

Where it acts; effect on the engine; plant required; consumables; reduction achievable; failure mode. Build the answer to limb A around those six and the comparison writes itself. Then add the three closing points: primary measures are cumulative; the dividing line is where the NOx goes, not where the equipment sits; and the two are complements, primary for the ordinary limit and a heavy primary or a secondary measure for the strict one.

The regulatory frame in five lines

  • NOx is controlled by MARPOL Annex VI regulation 13; at this sitting the text is the Revised Annex VI, in force 1 November 2022.
  • Three tiers, each limit a function of the engine's rated speed.
  • Construction date fixes the tier; the strictest tier also depends on where you are - inside a designated NOx emission control area.
  • Because it is geographic, the engine runs in two modes and the changeover must be recorded with date, time and position.
  • Certification is under the revised NOx Technical Code 2008, and compliance in service rests on the NOx Technical File and the record book of engine parameters.

Do not quote limit values. They are functions of rated speed and are set out in the instrument. What earns the mark is the practical consequence: the strictest tier is a step change, not an increment, and combustion tuning alone will not reach it - which is exactly why limb B asks about EGR and why SCR exists.

SAM and EGR - the shortest correct account of each

SAM: water evaporated into the scavenge air after the charge air cooler; latent heat plus higher specific heat lower the peak temperature. Needs a humidification section, clean fresh water, and above all a water mist catcher - vapour into the cylinder, never droplets. Watch corrosion and cylinder lubrication.

EGR: exhaust cleaned, cooled and returned to the scavenge air; less oxygen AND higher specific heat - two levers at once, which is why it reaches Tier III alone. Needs a scrubber, cooler, mist catcher, blower, shut-off valves, turbocharger arrangements and bleed-off water treatment. Watch fouling, acid corrosion and soot in the cylinder.

Common mistakes

  • Calling EGR a secondary measure. It is primary - it prevents formation.
  • Defining the primary/secondary split as inside against outside the engine, which produces exactly that error.
  • Saying the nitrogen comes from the fuel. Overwhelmingly it comes from the air.
  • Offering an exhaust gas scrubber as a NOx measure. It addresses sulphur oxides and particulates.
  • Describing the two technologies but never comparing primary with secondary, when the stem says vis-a-vis.
  • Omitting the NOx-fuel consumption trade-off, which is the reason the whole choice exists.
  • Forgetting the water mist catcher on SAM, or the scrubber on the EGR loop - the two items that make each system survivable.
  • Quoting limit values, percentage reductions or recirculation rates as general figures.
  • Forgetting that the strict tier is geographic, so the engine has two modes and the changeover is recorded.
  • Ignoring what these systems do to cylinder lubrication, which is the part that lands on the Chief Engineer.

Examiner traps

  • "Is EGR primary or secondary?" Primary. The plant is outside the cylinder but it prevents formation.
  • "Where does the nitrogen come from?" The air.
  • "Why does retarding the timing cost fuel?" Because the burn happens later in the expansion, at lower pressure and temperature - which is the same reason it lowers NOx.
  • "Will a scrubber reduce your NOx?" No - it addresses SOx and particulates.
  • "What is ammonia slip and why does it matter?" Unreacted reagent passing out of the funnel, from overdosing or from poor conversion. It is an emission in itself.
  • "Why does SCR care about fuel sulphur?" Salts form on the catalyst at low temperature and blind it.
  • "What is the most important single component in a SAM system?" The water mist catcher - vapour into the cylinder, never droplets.
  • "Why does EGR need a blower?" In a uniflow two-stroke the exhaust is at lower pressure than the scavenge air; the gas will not flow the right way unaided.
  • "What do you do with the EGR bleed-off water?" Treat and monitor it before any discharge - it is acidic and carries soot and metals.
  • "How do you know EGR is hurting your liners?" Cylinder condition and scrape-down analysis - iron and residual base number.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which tier is your main engine certified to, and how do you know?
  • Show me your NOx Technical File and tell me what is in it.
  • You change an injector nozzle to a different specification. What is the compliance issue?
  • How does your engine know it has entered an emission control area, and what is recorded?
  • If your EGR system failed on passage into the area, what would you do?
  • What happens to your cylinder oil feed rate when the moisturizing system is in service?

Memory framework

Three levers, two families, two systems. LEVERS (1): temperature, oxygen, time - and NOx trades against fuel. FAMILIES: PRIMARY = less created - timing, Miller, injection, water, EGR (2); SECONDARY = destroyed after - essentially SCR alone (3). Compare on where, engine effect, plant, consumable, reach, failure (4), then the tier frame (5). SYSTEMS: SAM = water vapour, temperature lever, mist catcher (6); EGR = cleaned exhaust, temperature AND oxygen, scrubber and blower (7); and both put water and acid where the engine did not want them (8).

Uncertainty and currency

The entry-into-force date carries this whole regulatory frame, and it must come from the resolution rather than from a summary. Resolution MEPC.328(76) records deemed acceptance on 1 May 2022 at operative paragraph 2 and entry into force on 1 NOVEMBER 2022 at operative paragraph 3. Secondary summaries of this amendment circulate with the year wrong, giving 1 November 2023. Taken at face value that would place the Revised Annex VI eight months after this examination and make the whole frame inapplicable to it - which is the quickest way to recognise the error. Go to the operative paragraphs.

No provision of MARPOL Annex VI is quoted anywhere in this answer, and no NOx limit value is given. MIW holds Annex VI in a form that may be cited but not quoted from, so regulation 13 is named and its content described in substance. The limits are functions of the engine's rated speed and are set out in the instrument; the tiers are therefore given by what fixes which one applies and by their relative severity. That is a limitation, and it is stated rather than disguised - but it costs the answer very little, because the examinable content is the structure and not the numbers.

The regulatory text is the one applicable at this sitting and not a later consolidation. Regulation 13 as consolidated to 1 May 2024 incorporates a 2024 amendment to the major-conversion provision. That amendment is after this examination and is excluded entirely; do not 'update' this answer to the later consolidation, and be careful with any printed copy of Annex VI dated after the sitting you are answering for.

No percentage reduction, water ratio, recirculation rate, catalyst temperature window or reagent consumption figure is asserted anywhere, because every one is engine, fuel, load and installation specific. No engine builder, licensor, catalyst supplier, system trade name or proprietary arrangement is named - both of the technologies in limb B exist in more than one commercial implementation, and the answer describes the principle rather than any one product. The treatment and discharge requirements for EGR bleed-off water are stated in substance without citing the governing guidelines, which MIW does not hold in readable form. The revised NOx Technical Code 2008 is likewise cited by identity only.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Why NOx forms at all, and the three levers every measure pulls
  2. __________A. A) Primary measures - preventing NOx from forming
  3. __________A. A) Secondary measures - removing NOx after it has formed
  4. __________A. A) Primary against secondary - the comparison the limb asks for
  5. __________A. A) The regulatory frame that decides which you need
  6. __________B. B) Scavenge Air Moisturizing
  7. __________B. B) Exhaust Gas Recirculation
  8. __________B. B) SAM against EGR - what each costs to fit and to run
Flashcards 10

Limb A: 1 formation and the three levers. 2 primary measures in three families. 3 secondary measures - essentially SCR. 4 the comparison on six axes, placing EGR on the primary side. 5 the tier frame that decides what you need. Limb B: 6 scavenge air moisturizing. 7 exhaust gas recirculation. 8 the two compared, and what they cost to run.

The stem prints 'vis-a-vis', so limb A is a comparison and needs axes; limb B needs principle, arrangement and consequences for each system.

The nitrogen comes overwhelmingly from the air, not the fuel. At flame temperature nitrogen and oxygen combine, and the rate rises very steeply with peak temperature and depends besides on oxygen availability and on residence time at temperature. Those three are the only levers there are, and every control measure pulls at least one of them.

Stating the mechanism first makes every measure in the answer derivable instead of memorised.

Primary acts on the combustion so that less NOx is ever created - the exhaust leaving the cylinder is already cleaner. Secondary leaves the combustion alone and destroys the NOx after it has formed, in the exhaust system. The dividing line is where the NOx goes, not where the equipment sits - which is why EGR, whose plant is entirely outside the cylinder, is a primary measure.

The classification rule is the whole of limb A, and the wrong formulation of it produces the question's commonest error.

Primary. Every part of an EGR installation - scrubber, cooler, mist catcher, blower - sits outside the cylinder, which makes it look like after-treatment. But it works by returning cleaned exhaust to the charge, which reduces oxygen concentration and raises the charge's specific heat, so the NOx is never formed. Prevention, not removal.

The single commonest error in the question, and the discrimination the limb is built to test.

Water is evaporated into the scavenge air after the charge air cooler, so the charge carries a large mass of water vapour into the cylinder. The water absorbs latent heat as it evaporates and the vapour has a higher specific heat than air, so the peak combustion temperature falls and less NOx forms. It needs a humidification section, clean fresh water, and a water mist catcher - vapour must enter the cylinder, never droplets.

It pulls only the temperature lever, and the mist catcher is the component that makes it survivable.

Part of the exhaust is cleaned, cooled and returned to the scavenge air, which reduces oxygen and raises specific heat - two levers at once, which is why it can reach the strictest tier alone. The plant is large because marine exhaust is dirty: a scrubber to remove sulphur oxides and soot, a cooler, a water mist catcher, an EGR blower (the exhaust is at lower pressure than the scavenge air), shut-off valves, turbocharger arrangements, and bleed-off water treatment.

The arrangement follows from the mechanism, and the blower and the scrubber are the two items candidates omit.

A reducing agent - normally an aqueous urea solution giving ammonia - is injected into the exhaust upstream of a catalyst, over which the nitrogen oxides and the reagent react to nitrogen and water. It is sensitive to temperature (which decides whether the reactor sits before or after the turbocharger), to fuel sulphur (salts form at low temperature and blind the catalyst), to dosing (too much gives ammonia slip), and to space and back pressure.

SCR is effectively the whole of the secondary side, so its characteristics carry half of limb A's second part.

No. A scrubber addresses sulphur oxides and particulate matter and has essentially no effect on nitrogen oxides. The confusion arises because an EGR loop also contains a scrubber - but there the scrubber is cleaning gas that is about to go back into the engine, not cleaning the exhaust on its way out.

A frequent and expensive confusion, and the EGR loop's own scrubber is what causes it.

By MARPOL Annex VI regulation 13 - at this sitting, the Revised Annex VI introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. There are three tiers, each limit a function of the engine's rated speed. The ship's construction date fixes the tier, and the strictest tier applies besides only within a designated NOx emission control area - so the engine runs in two modes and the changeover is recorded with date, time and position.

The tier structure is the frame; the values are not quotable and are not what is being examined.

Both work by making the combustion cooler, and both put water and acid where the designer of a dry engine did not intend them. Both therefore transfer a burden onto cylinder lubrication: the oil film is disturbed, the acid load rises, and feed rate and residual base number need watching. Scrape-down analysis is how it is watched, whichever system is fitted.

It joins this question to the oil analysis question on the same paper and is the practical consequence an examiner will press on.

Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI regulation 13 controls nitrogen oxides from marine diesel engines. At this sitting the applicable text is the Revised Annex VI introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), which entered into force on 1 November 2022 - established from the resolution's own operative paragraph 3, because secondary summaries of this amendment circulate with the date one year late. The regulation sets three tiers of progressively stricter limits, each expressed as a function of the engine's rated speed; which tier applies is fixed by the ship's construction date, and the strictest tier depends besides on operation within a designated NOx emission control area, so the engine runs in two modes and the changeover is recorded with date, time and position. Major trap
Calling exhaust gas recirculation a secondary measure. All of its plant sits outside the cylinder, which makes it look like after-treatment - but it works by preventing formation, so it is primary. Classify by where the NOx goes, not by where the equipment is bolted. Second trap: offering an exhaust gas scrubber as a NOx measure - it addresses sulphur oxides and particulates and does essentially nothing for NOx. Third: saying the nitrogen comes from the fuel; it comes overwhelmingly from the air. Fourth: describing primary and secondary measures separately and never comparing them, when the stem prints vis-a-vis. Fifth: omitting the NOx-fuel consumption trade-off, which is the whole reason the choice exists. Sixth: forgetting the water mist catcher on a SAM system or the scrubber on an EGR loop - the two components that make each system survivable. Seventh: quoting limit values or percentage reductions as though they were general figures.

Longer-term signalNewly recurring topicActive in recent papersFramework since amendedCurrentness check pending
Understand this first

Think about what actually has to happen for a ship to move. Fuel burns in a cylinder and pushes a piston down - that is all the power there will ever be, and every single thing that happens afterwards takes a share of it. The bearings take some. The stern tube takes some. Then the propeller, which is by far the biggest thief, because the only way it can push the ship forward is by throwing water backwards, and all the energy in that thrown water is gone for good. What finally arrives is a force pushing the hull through the sea against its resistance - and if you multiply that resistance by the ship's speed you get the effective power, which is simply the power somebody would need to tow her along at that speed with a rope. So the first limb is just the accounting: name each stage, and name what is lost between them. There is one lovely complication worth understanding rather than memorising. The hull drags a layer of water along with itself, so the propeller is not working in still water - it is working in water already moving in the same direction, which makes its job easier. But the propeller, by sucking water past the hull, also increases the hull's drag, which makes its job harder. Those two effects both exist, they pull opposite ways, and the helpful one usually wins - which is why the 'hull efficiency' can come out greater than one and no law of physics has been broken. The second limb is what happens on the day the ship first goes to sea. Everything up to then has been a calculation. The trials are where the ship is made to prove it: how fast she really goes for a given power, how tightly she turns, how quickly she stops swinging when you reverse the wheel, and how far she runs before she stops. And the reason it matters to you rather than only to the shipyard is that those numbers become the poster on the bridge wall for the next twenty-five years.

    3. 3A) Brake power to shaft power to delivered power - the transmission
      • Thrust block, intermediate and line shaft bearings, and the stern tube - usually the largest single element
      • Reduction gearing where fitted; the electrical chain in a diesel-electric plant
      • Shaft power measured by torsionmeter - torque × angular velocity
    4. 4A) Delivered power to thrust power to effective power - the propeller and the hull
      • Open-water efficiency - accelerated water astern, rotational slipstream energy, blade friction. Normally the biggest loss in the chain

      • Relative rotative efficiency - the propeller behind the hull is not the propeller in open water
