Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

Bullet answer — points to write
A.
  1. What the question is asking - the power chain, and where effective power sits
    • A chain of named powers, each smaller than the last, with a named efficiency across every step
    • Indicated - in the cylinder, from the indicator diagram
    • Brake - at the coupling; Shaft - on the line shafting, by torsionmeter
    • Delivered - at the propeller; Thrust - thrust × speed of advance
    • Effective - resistance × ship speed, the towrope power
    • The naval architect reads the chain upwards; the engineer measures downwards
  2. A) Indicated power to brake power - the engine's own losses
    • Indicated power from the indicator diagram - work on the piston per cycle
    • The only power in the chain measured inside the engine
    • Losses: friction at rings, crosshead, crankpin and main bearings - much the largest
    • Engine-driven auxiliaries - camshaft, pumps, governor, scavenge arrangement
    • Pumping and windage within the engine
    • The ratio is mechanical efficiency
    • It is not constant - friction is steady while output varies, so the engine is worst at low load
  3. A) Brake power to shaft power to delivered power - the transmission
    • Thrust block, intermediate and line shaft bearings, and the stern tube - usually the largest single element
    • Reduction gearing where fitted; the electrical chain in a diesel-electric plant
    • Shaft power measured by torsionmeter - torque × angular velocity
    • The last point in the chain where power can be measured directly
    • Delivered power is what reaches the propeller and cannot be measured - it is inferred
    • The ratio is transmission or shaft efficiency, and it is sensitive to alignment
  4. A) Delivered power to thrust power to effective power - the propeller and the hull
    • Open-water efficiency - accelerated water astern, rotational slipstream energy, blade friction. Normally the biggest loss in the chain
    • Thrust power = thrust × SPEED OF ADVANCE, not ship speed
    • Relative rotative efficiency - the propeller behind the hull is not the propeller in open water
    • Wake fraction: the hull drags water along, so the propeller works in slower water - it HELPS
    • Thrust deduction fraction: the working propeller raises the hull's resistance - it HURTS
    • Together they give hull efficiency, which can exceed unity, and the reason is explainable
    • Effective power = resistance × ship speed - the destination
  5. A) The efficiencies pulled together - the propulsive coefficient
    • Quasi-propulsive coefficient = effective ÷ delivered - hull and propeller only
    • Propulsive coefficient = effective ÷ shaft or brake - the whole installation
    • The second is the first multiplied by the transmission efficiency
    • No typical value is offered for any efficiency - all are installation specific
    • The diagnostic use: shaft power up for the same speed means hull or propeller
    • Fuel up for the same shaft power means the engine
B).
  1. B) What sea trials are for, and the conditions that make them valid
    • To verify by measurement what has until then been predicted
    • Four purposes at once: contractual, statutory, operational and baseline
    • Deep, unrestricted water - shallow water changes resistance, squat and turning
    • Low sea state and wind, recorded; the specified loading condition and trim
    • A steady approach before the manoeuvre begins
    • Reciprocal headings, so current and tide cancel in the mean
    • Clean hull and propeller, and calibrated instruments
  2. B) The navigation trials, and what each establishes
    • Progressive speed trial - the power-speed curve, the contract speed, and the service datum
    • Endurance and consumption - specific fuel consumption against the guarantee
    • Turning circle - advance, transfer, tactical diameter, drift angle, speed loss. Both sides
    • Zig-zag - overshoot angles, giving yaw-checking and course-changing ability
    • Spiral, reverse spiral and pull-out - course stability and the width of the loop
    • Crash stop - head reach, track reach and time to dead in the water
    • Astern and minimum steerage trials - astern power, consecutive starts, minimum manoeuvring speed
    • Steering gear trial - hard over to hard over, main and auxiliary, changeover and emergency steering
    • Anchoring trial - windlass capability and brake holding power
  3. B) What the results become
    • The manoeuvring booklet - the full trial results, loaded and ballast, deep and shallow
    • The wheelhouse poster - the essential figures where they will be read
    • The pilot card - completed by the master and handed over on boarding
    • The speed-power curve and trial datum - what makes a fouling or speed-claim argument settleable
    • The manoeuvrability standards and the manoeuvring-information requirement, cited by identity only
    • The joining sentence: limb A is the theory, and the speed trial is the measurement that proves it

Memory cue. Limb A - six powers, five efficiencies. Indicated to Brake is mechanical (2); Brake to Shaft to Delivered is transmission (3); Delivered to Thrust to Effective is the propeller and the hull, where wake helps and thrust deduction hurts (4); then combine as QPC and propulsive coefficient (5). Limb B - conditions, trials, documents. Deep water, calm, correct loading, reciprocal headings (6); speed, turning, zig-zag, spiral, crash stop, astern, steering gear, anchor (7); booklet, poster, pilot card and the datum (8).

Remember8 route headings
Cover54 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 54 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. What the question is asking - the power chain, and where effective power sits

Limb A asks for the stages by which the power produced in the cylinder becomes the power that actually moves the ship. The answer is a chain of named powers, each smaller than the one before it, with a named efficiency across every step. Setting the chain out first and then walking down it is what turns this into a structured answer rather than a list of definitions.

StageWhat it isWhere it is measured
Indicated powerThe power developed in the cylinders, from the work done on the pistonsFrom the indicator diagram - in the engine itself
Brake powerThe power delivered at the engine coupling, after the engine's own frictionAt the output flange - historically on a brake, which is where the name comes from
Shaft powerThe power transmitted along the shaftingBy torsionmeter, on the line shaft
Delivered powerThe power actually reaching the propeller, after the stern tubeNot directly measurable in service - inferred from shaft power
Thrust powerThe power in the thrust the propeller producesThrust multiplied by speed of advance
Effective powerThe power needed to move the hull at that speedTotal resistance multiplied by ship speed - the towrope power

Effective power is the destination, and its definition is the key to the whole limb. It is the power that would be required to tow the hull through the water at that speed - resistance multiplied by speed - which is why it is also called towrope power. It is the useful output of the entire installation, and everything between the cylinder and it is loss.

Note the direction of travel, because it is where candidates get confused. The naval architect works upwards: he predicts the resistance of the hull, obtains the effective power required, and divides by the efficiencies to find what the engine must produce. The engineer measures downwards: he reads what the engine is producing and asks how much of it is reaching the water. They are the same chain read in opposite directions.

2. A) Indicated power to brake power - the engine's own losses

Indicated power is derived from the indicator diagram - the pressure-volume trace taken from the cylinder. The area of that diagram gives the work done on the piston in one cycle; multiplied by the number of cycles in unit time and by the piston area and stroke, it gives the power developed inside the cylinder. It is the true gross output of the combustion process and it is the only power in the chain that is measured in the engine rather than outside it.

Between the piston and the coupling flange, the engine consumes part of its own output:

  • Friction at the piston rings and skirt, at the crosshead, crankpin and main bearings, and in the running gear generally - by a wide margin the largest of these losses.
  • Driven auxiliaries hung on the engine - camshaft drive, lubricating and cooling pumps, governor drive, and the scavenge or supercharging arrangement where it is engine-driven.
  • Pumping and windage losses within the engine itself.

What is left at the coupling is brake power, and the ratio between them is the mechanical efficiency of the engine. Two practical points belong here. First, mechanical efficiency is not constant - the friction loss is relatively steady while the output varies, so the engine is mechanically least efficient at low load, which is one reason slow steaming is not free. Second, the difference between indicated and brake power is exactly the quantity a friction power or motoring test measures, and it is the figure that changes when the running gear deteriorates.

3. A) Brake power to shaft power to delivered power - the transmission

From the coupling the power passes down the shafting, and each element in it takes a share:

  • Thrust block - which transfers the propeller's thrust into the hull and, in doing so, absorbs power in its bearing surfaces.
  • Intermediate and line shaft bearings - a small loss each, but there may be many of them in a long shaft line.
  • Stern tube bearing and seals - the last and often the largest single element of the transmission loss, and the one most affected by alignment and by wear.
  • Reduction gearing, where fitted - a medium-speed or geared installation adds a gearbox loss that a direct-coupled slow-speed engine does not have.
  • The electrical chain, in a diesel-electric installation - generator, switchboard and converter, propulsion motor - which replaces the gearbox with a longer chain of its own.

Shaft power is the power measured on the line shafting, normally by a torsionmeter reading the angle of twist over a known length: torque multiplied by angular velocity. It is the most useful measurement in the whole chain, because it is the last point at which power can be measured directly and it is measured in service, continuously, on a modern ship.

Delivered power is what remains at the propeller after the stern tube. It cannot be measured directly - there is nothing to instrument aft of the stern tube - so it is inferred from shaft power and the estimated stern tube loss. The ratio of delivered to brake power is the transmission or shaft efficiency, and its most important practical property is that it is sensitive to alignment: a shaft line out of alignment loses power continuously and heats bearings while it does so.

4. A) Delivered power to thrust power to effective power - the propeller and the hull

This is where the largest losses occur, and it is the part of the chain candidates most often skip. Three separate effects sit between the delivered power and the effective power, and each has its own named efficiency.

First, the propeller in open water. A propeller turning in undisturbed water converts delivered power into thrust power - thrust multiplied by the speed of advance, which is the speed of the water into the propeller and not the speed of the ship. The ratio is the open-water efficiency, and the losses are real and unavoidable: the water is accelerated astern and carries kinetic energy away with it, there is rotational energy in the slipstream, and there is blade friction. This is normally the single biggest loss in the entire chain.

Second, the propeller behind the hull is not the propeller in open water. It works in the wake, in a flow that is neither uniform nor at ship speed, so its efficiency there differs from its open-water efficiency. The ratio between the two is the relative rotative efficiency.

Third, the hull and the propeller interact, in two opposite directions. This is the interesting part of the limb and it is worth stating carefully:

EffectWhat happensWhich way it acts
Wake, expressed as the wake fractionThe hull drags a layer of water along with it, so the water entering the propeller is slower than the ship. The speed of advance is therefore less than the ship speedHelps. The propeller does its work in slower water and needs less power for the same thrust
Thrust deduction, expressed as the thrust deduction fractionThe working propeller lowers the pressure ahead of itself and therefore increases the hull's resistance. The thrust required is greater than the towed resistance would suggestHurts. Some of the thrust produced is spent overcoming resistance the propeller itself created

The two combine into the hull efficiency, which - uniquely in this chain - can exceed unity, because the wake gain often outweighs the thrust deduction penalty. That is not a paradox and it is not an error: hull efficiency is not a thermodynamic efficiency but a ratio between two ways of accounting for the same work, and a candidate who states it and explains why has demonstrated that he understands the chain rather than memorised it.

What emerges at the end is the effective power - resistance multiplied by ship speed, the power that would tow the bare hull at that speed against the sum of frictional, residuary and appendage resistance, plus whatever the wind and the sea are adding on the day.

5. A) The efficiencies pulled together - the propulsive coefficient

The stem asks for stages, so the answer should close the limb by putting them back together. Two combined figures are used in practice, and the distinction between them is a reliable mark:

  • The quasi-propulsive coefficient - effective power divided by delivered power. It combines the open-water efficiency, the relative rotative efficiency and the hull efficiency, and it is therefore a statement about the hull and propeller together. It says nothing about the machinery.
  • The propulsive coefficient - effective power divided by shaft or brake power. It is the quasi-propulsive coefficient multiplied by the transmission efficiency, and it is therefore a statement about the whole installation.

No typical value is offered for any efficiency in this chain, and that is deliberate. Every one of them is specific to the hull form, the propeller, the machinery arrangement and the loading condition, and a figure carried from one ship to another is misleading rather than merely imprecise. What matters in the examination is that the chain, the names and the direction of each effect are right.

And the practical use of the whole limb is this: the chain is how a fouled hull, a damaged propeller and a worn engine are told apart. If shaft power is up for the same speed, the loss is aft of the shaft - hull or propeller. If fuel consumption is up for the same shaft power, the loss is in the engine. Without the chain the two look identical from the bridge.

6. B) What sea trials are for, and the conditions that make them valid

Sea trials exist to verify by measurement what has until then only been predicted. The naval architect's model tests and calculations, the engine builder's shop trials and the manoeuvring predictions are all forecasts; the trial is the first and only occasion on which the complete ship, in her own water, is made to demonstrate them. They serve four distinct purposes at once:

  • Contractual - the speed, power and consumption guarantees in the building contract are proved or not proved here, and money turns on the result.
  • Statutory and classification - flag State, class and the attending surveyors require certain demonstrations before the ship enters service.
  • Operational - the results become the manoeuvring information the ship carries for the rest of her life, which is the part that matters to those who will sail her.
  • Baseline - the trial figures are the datum against which every later performance measurement, and every argument about hull fouling, is judged.

The conditions are as important as the manoeuvres, because a trial run in the wrong conditions produces numbers that cannot be compared with anything. The standard requirements are:

  • Deep, unrestricted water. Shallow water increases resistance and changes both squat and turning behaviour, and confined water changes them again.
  • Low sea state and wind, with the limits agreed beforehand, and the state recorded.
  • The specified loading condition and trim, which is normally ballast for a newbuilding and must be recorded either way, since displacement changes everything measured.
  • A steady approach - the ship settled on a constant heading at constant revolutions before the manoeuvre begins, or the result belongs to the approach rather than to the ship.
  • Runs on reciprocal headings, so that current and tidal stream cancel in the mean. A speed trial run one way only measures the current as much as the ship.
  • A clean hull and propeller, and a calibrated instrument set - shaft torsionmeter, fuel meters, position and speed reference.

7. B) The navigation trials, and what each establishes

TrialWhat is doneWhat it establishes
Progressive speed trialMeasured runs at several power settings, each on reciprocal headings, with speed, shaft power and revolutions recordedThe power-speed and power-revolution curves, the contract speed at the contract power, and the datum for the ship's whole service life. This is the trial that closes the power chain of limb A
Endurance and fuel consumption trialA sustained run at a specified power for an agreed periodSpecific fuel consumption against the guarantee, and the stability of the plant under continuous load
Turning circleFrom a steady approach at a defined speed, rudder put hard over and held through at least 360 degrees. Run to both port and starboardAdvance, transfer, tactical diameter, drift angle and speed loss in the turn - the ship's turning ability, and the numbers a master needs to know before he needs them
Zig-zag manoeuvreRudder put over a set angle; when the heading has changed by a set amount the rudder is reversed to the same angle on the other side, and so on. Run in the two standard combinations - equal rudder and heading angles, at a smaller and at a larger valueYaw-checking and course-changing ability, read from the overshoot angles - how far the head continues to swing after the rudder is reversed. The single most informative manoeuvring trial there is
Spiral and reverse spiralSteady rates of turn are recorded against a series of rudder angles, from hard over to hard over and backWhether the ship is dynamically stable in course, and if not, the width of the loop - which tells the master how much rudder she needs simply to go straight
Pull-out testThe ship is brought to a steady turn, the rudder returned to midships, and the rate of turn recorded as it decaysA quicker check on course stability. On a stable ship the rate of turn decays to zero; on an unstable ship it settles at a residual rate
Crash stop, or stopping testFrom full ahead at a steady heading, the engine is ordered full astern and held until the ship is dead in the waterHead reach, track reach, lateral deviation and time to stop. Also a severe test of the engine's astern capability and of the shafting
Astern trialRunning astern at specified powers, and the ahead-astern-ahead sequenceAstern power, the number of consecutive starts available, and steering behaviour going astern
Minimum revolutions and steerage trialThe lowest stable engine speed is established, and the lowest ship speed at which the ship still answers her helmThe minimum manoeuvring speed - which decides what is possible in a channel or alongside
Steering gear trialThe rudder is run hard over to hard over at service speed and at the deepest draught, on the main and then on the auxiliary power unit, with changeover and emergency steering demonstratedThat the steering gear meets its required rate of movement and that control from the alternative position works
Anchoring trialAnchor let go and recovered from a specified depth, on both sidesWindlass hoisting capability and brake holding power - the trial most often treated as a formality and most missed when it was not run properly

Several other demonstrations are run in the same period and are worth naming even though they are not navigation trials in the strict sense: the inclining experiment to establish the lightship weight and centre of gravity; machinery trials including blackout and dead-ship recovery, alarm and safety-device demonstrations, and remote-control and bridge-control response; and vibration and noise measurement through the speed range to identify critical revolutions and any barred speed range.

8. B) What the results become

A trial that produces a report and nothing else has been half wasted. The results are turned into the documents the ship then carries and uses, which is the point at which the whole exercise becomes operational rather than contractual:

  • The manoeuvring booklet, holding the full trial results - turning circles, stopping distances, zig-zag data, and the behaviour in shallow water and in the ballast and loaded conditions.
  • The wheelhouse poster, being the essential figures displayed permanently where they will actually be read.
  • The pilot card, completed by the master and handed to the pilot on boarding - the single most useful piece of paper in the exchange.
  • The speed-power curve and the trial datum, against which every subsequent performance report is measured, and which is what makes an argument about hull fouling or a charterparty speed claim capable of being settled.

IMO's standards for ship manoeuvrability set the criteria against which turning, yaw-checking, course-keeping and stopping ability are judged, and the requirement that manoeuvring information be provided on board is what turns the trial results into the poster, the booklet and the card. Both are cited here by identity: no criterion value is quoted, because neither instrument was read at source for this answer.

And the sentence worth closing on, because it joins the two limbs the stem has deliberately put together: limb A is the theory of where the power goes, and the speed trial in limb B is the measurement that proves it. The trial produces the delivered power actually needed for a given speed; the chain explains why that number is what it is; and for the rest of the ship's life the difference between the two is the ship's condition.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Two limbs with no printed marks, so treat them as eight and eight. Limb A is a chain - name every stage, name the efficiency across every step, and finish by putting them back together. Limb B is a list with objectives, and the stem says so: it asks for the trials and the typical objectives of each test. A trial named without its objective earns half of what it could.

The single best way to open limb A is to write the chain down as a line before explaining any part of it: indicated, brake, shaft, delivered, thrust, effective. Six words, and the examiner can already see that you know the answer.

The chain in one line

INDICATED (in the cylinder, from the indicator diagram) → mechanical efficiency → BRAKE (at the coupling) → transmission and shaft efficiency → SHAFT (torsionmeter) → DELIVERED (at the propeller) → open-water and relative rotative efficiency → THRUST (thrust × speed of advance) → hull efficiency → EFFECTIVE (resistance × ship speed = towrope power).

Definitions that must be exact

  • Effective power = total resistance × ship speed. The power to tow the hull at that speed. Also called towrope power.
  • Thrust power = thrust × speed of ADVANCE - the speed of the water into the propeller, not the ship's speed. Getting this wrong unravels the rest of the limb.
  • Shaft power is measured by torsionmeter - torque × angular velocity - and is the last power in the chain that can be measured directly.
  • Delivered power cannot be measured at all; it is inferred from shaft power less the stern tube loss.

The two hull-propeller interaction effects - and which way each acts

Wake HELPS. The hull drags water along with it, so the propeller works in slower water than the ship is moving through - the speed of advance is less than the ship speed. Thrust deduction HURTS. The working propeller lowers the pressure ahead of itself and increases the hull's resistance, so more thrust is needed than the towed resistance suggests. Together they give hull efficiency, and stating that hull efficiency can exceed unity - because the wake gain usually outweighs the thrust deduction penalty - is the mark that separates a good answer from an adequate one.

QPC against propulsive coefficient

Quasi-propulsive coefficient = effective ÷ DELIVERED power - hull and propeller only, and it says nothing about the machinery. Propulsive coefficient = effective ÷ SHAFT or BRAKE power - the whole installation, being the QPC multiplied by the transmission efficiency. Knowing which denominator belongs to which is a small, reliable mark.

Do not quote efficiency values

No typical value is given anywhere in this answer for any efficiency, and none should be. Each is hull-form, propeller, machinery and loading specific, and a figure carried from one ship to another is misleading rather than approximate. Naming the chain, the efficiencies and the direction of each effect is what is being examined.

The trials that carry the most marks

  • Progressive speed trial - the power-speed curve, the contract speed, and the datum for the ship's life. Run on reciprocal headings or you are measuring the tide.
  • Turning circle - advance, transfer, tactical diameter, drift angle and speed loss. Both sides.
  • Zig-zag - the overshoot angles, giving yaw-checking and course-changing ability. The most informative single manoeuvre.
  • Spiral, reverse spiral and pull-out - course stability, and the width of the loop if she is unstable.
  • Crash stop - head reach, track reach and time to dead in the water.
  • Steering gear trial - hard over to hard over, main and auxiliary units, changeover and emergency steering.

Conditions - the marks nobody writes

Deep unrestricted water, low sea and wind, the specified loading condition, a steady approach, reciprocal headings, clean hull and calibrated instruments. Two or three lines on conditions will earn more than a tenth trial, because they show that you understand what a measurement is for. And the sharpest single point: shallow water changes both resistance and turning behaviour, so a trial run in shallow water measures a different ship.

Close both limbs together

The stem sets the two limbs side by side, and the connection is real: the speed trial is the measurement that closes the power chain. The chain predicts the delivered power needed for a speed; the trial measures what was actually needed; and for the rest of the ship's life the gap between them is the ship's condition. Say that in one sentence at the end and the answer reads as one argument rather than two.

Common mistakes

  • Muddling the order of the chain, or omitting delivered power between shaft and thrust power.
  • Defining thrust power with the ship's speed instead of the speed of advance.
  • Leaving out the hull-propeller interaction entirely - the wake and thrust deduction pair is where the thinking is.
  • Saying an efficiency greater than one is impossible. Hull efficiency commonly exceeds unity, and the reason is explainable.
  • Confusing the quasi-propulsive coefficient with the propulsive coefficient.
  • Quoting typical efficiency values as though they were general.
  • Listing trials without their objectives, when the stem asks for the objective of each test.
  • Forgetting the conditions - deep water, low sea state, loading condition, reciprocal headings.
  • Omitting the steering gear and anchoring trials, which are easy marks.
  • Stopping at the trial report and never mentioning the manoeuvring booklet, wheelhouse poster and pilot card.

Examiner traps

  • "What is effective power?" Resistance × ship speed - the power to tow the hull at that speed. Not anything the engine produces.
  • "Speed of advance or ship speed in thrust power?" Speed of advance, which is lower because of the wake.
  • "Can an efficiency exceed one?" Hull efficiency can, because the wake gain usually outweighs the thrust deduction penalty. It is a ratio of two accountings, not a thermodynamic efficiency.
  • "Where do you measure power on your ship?" Shaft power, by torsionmeter. Delivered power cannot be measured.
  • "Your speed is down. Engine or hull?" Compare shaft power against speed, and fuel against shaft power. The first isolates the hull and propeller, the second the engine.
  • "Why run a speed trial both ways?" To cancel current and tidal stream in the mean.
  • "Why must trials be in deep water?" Shallow water increases resistance and alters turning and squat - it is a different ship.
  • "What does a zig-zag tell you that a turning circle does not?" Yaw-checking ability - how quickly she stops swinging when you reverse the rudder, read from the overshoot angles.
  • "What is on the pilot card, and who fills it in?" The essential manoeuvring data, completed by the master and given to the pilot on boarding.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Show me your torsionmeter reading and tell me what you do with it.
  • What is your ship's tactical diameter loaded, and where would you find it?
  • How would you demonstrate the steering gear before departure?
  • How long does your ship take to stop from full ahead, and how far does she go?
  • What is a barred speed range and why does your ship have one?
  • How do you know your hull is fouled rather than your engine being down on power?

Memory framework

Limb A - six powers, five efficiencies. I-B-S-D-T-E: Indicated (1, 2), Brake (2), Shaft and Delivered (3), Thrust (4), Effective (4) - then pull them together as QPC and propulsive coefficient (5). Remember the two interaction effects by direction: wake helps, thrust deduction hurts. Limb B - conditions, then trials, then documents. CONDITIONS (6): deep water, calm, correct loading, reciprocal headings. TRIALS (7): speed, turning, zig-zag, spiral and pull-out, crash stop, astern, steering gear, anchor. DOCUMENTS (8): booklet, poster, pilot card, and the trial datum.

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from

The trial manoeuvres, their method and their objectivesEstablished shipbuilding and trials practice. The precise programme is contractual - contract, owner's specification and flag State requirements
The criteria for turning, yaw-checking, course-keeping and stoppingIMO standards for ship manoeuvrability - cited by identity only. No criterion value is quoted, because the instrument was not read at source
The manoeuvring booklet, wheelhouse poster and pilot cardThe requirement that manoeuvring information be provided on board - cited by identity only; no provision quoted
The steering gear demonstrationThe steering gear trial required before the ship enters service - cited by identity only; no time limit asserted
The Indian frameMerchant Shipping Act, 1958, administered by DG Shipping. Not engaged - this question carries no Indian statutory content

Uncertainty and evidence notes

This is the only wholly fresh question on the paper. No solved question anywhere in the MIW set treats either limb, so this answer was written from first principles with no earlier answer to build on and no existing verification record to lean on. The compensating obligation is that every statement carries its own grade, and it does: the power chain and its efficiencies are engineering reference, the trials are established shipbuilding practice, and the connection drawn between the two limbs is engineering judgement and is identified as such.

No numerical value or typical range is asserted for any efficiency - not for mechanical, transmission, shaft, open-water, relative rotative or hull efficiency, and not for the quasi-propulsive or propulsive coefficient. Each is hull-form, propeller, machinery and installation specific, and a range carried between ships would be misleading rather than merely imprecise.

No sea trial acceptance criterion is quoted as a number. The three IMO instruments touched - the standards for ship manoeuvrability, the requirement to provide manoeuvring information on board, and the steering gear trial - are cited by identity only. None was read at source in this session; no provision is quoted, no regulation number is asserted beyond the identity of the instrument, and the application thresholds by ship length, type and date of build are not stated. The existence of overshoot, advance, tactical diameter and stopping criteria is stated; their values are not.

No shipyard, model basin, designer, engine builder, classification society rule or software is named anywhere. And the trial programme described is the standard one rather than a closed statutory list: which trials are run, in what order and to what tolerance is fixed by the building contract, the owner's specification and the flag State's requirements. The stem does not say which machinery arrangement is intended, so the chain is given for a direct-coupled installation and the additional stages of a geared or diesel-electric plant are identified where they arise rather than assumed away.

This question carries no temporal exposure at all. Naval architecture does not date, the trial set has been stable for decades, and the three instruments touched were all long operative at this sitting. Nothing here turns on the 2023 boundaries that govern the rest of the paper.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________A. What the question is asking - the power chain, and where effective power sits
  2. __________A. A) Indicated power to brake power - the engine's own losses
  3. __________A. A) Brake power to shaft power to delivered power - the transmission
  4. __________A. A) Delivered power to thrust power to effective power - the propeller and the hull
  5. __________A. A) The efficiencies pulled together - the propulsive coefficient
  6. __________B). B) What sea trials are for, and the conditions that make them valid
  7. __________B). B) The navigation trials, and what each establishes
  8. __________B). B) What the results become
Flashcards 10

Limb A: 1 the whole chain and where effective power sits. 2 indicated to brake - the engine's losses. 3 brake to shaft to delivered - the transmission. 4 delivered to thrust to effective - the propeller and the hull. 5 the coefficients recombined. Limb B: 6 what trials are for, and the conditions. 7 the trials with their objectives. 8 what the results become.

Limb A is a chain and must be walked in order; limb B asks for objectives as well as trials, which is half its marks.

Indicated - in the cylinder, from the indicator diagram. Brake - at the engine coupling. Shaft - on the line shafting, by torsionmeter. Delivered - at the propeller, after the stern tube; not measurable, only inferred. Thrust - thrust multiplied by speed of advance. Effective - total resistance multiplied by ship speed, also called towrope power.

The chain in order is the spine of limb A, and delivered power is the stage most often omitted.

Effective power = total resistance × SHIP speed - the power that would be needed to tow the hull at that speed, which is why it is called towrope power. Thrust power = thrust × SPEED OF ADVANCE - the speed of the water entering the propeller, which is lower than the ship's speed because of the wake. Using ship speed for thrust power is the commonest error in the question.

These two definitions carry the limb; getting the speed of advance wrong unravels everything after it.

Wake: the hull drags a layer of water along with it, so the water entering the propeller is slower than the ship - the propeller works in slower water and needs less power for the same thrust. It HELPS. Thrust deduction: the working propeller lowers the pressure ahead of itself and so increases the hull's resistance, meaning more thrust is required than the towed resistance suggests. It HURTS. Together they give hull efficiency.

The two opposite effects are where the thinking in limb A lives, and both must be given with their direction.

Yes, and commonly is. The wake gain usually outweighs the thrust deduction penalty. It is not a thermodynamic efficiency but a ratio between two ways of accounting for the same work - the towed resistance of the hull against the thrust the propeller must actually produce - so a value above unity breaks no physical law.

A standard oral trap, and explaining why proves the chain is understood rather than memorised.

Quasi-propulsive coefficient = effective power ÷ DELIVERED power. It combines open-water efficiency, relative rotative efficiency and hull efficiency, so it describes the hull and propeller and says nothing about the machinery. Propulsive coefficient = effective power ÷ SHAFT or BRAKE power - the QPC multiplied by the transmission efficiency, describing the whole installation.

A small, reliable mark that turns entirely on which power is the denominator.

Deep, unrestricted water - shallow water alters resistance, squat and turning behaviour. Low sea state and wind, recorded. The specified loading condition and trim, since displacement changes everything measured. A steady approach before the manoeuvre begins. Runs on reciprocal headings, so current and tidal stream cancel in the mean. And a clean hull and calibrated instruments.

Conditions are the marks nobody writes, and they show that you understand what a measurement is for.

The turning circle gives advance, transfer, tactical diameter, drift angle and speed loss in the turn - the ship's turning ability - and is run to both sides. The zig-zag puts the rudder over, reverses it at a set heading change and repeats, giving the overshoot angles: how far the head keeps swinging after the rudder is reversed. That is yaw-checking and course-changing ability, which the turning circle cannot show.

The zig-zag is the most informative manoeuvring trial and the one candidates most often cannot explain.

The spiral and reverse spiral tests, in which steady rates of turn are recorded against a series of rudder angles from hard over to hard over and back; and the pull-out test, in which the ship is brought to a steady turn and the rudder returned amidships. On a stable ship the rate of turn decays to zero; on an unstable one it settles at a residual rate, and the spiral shows the width of the loop - how much rudder she needs simply to go straight.

Course stability is a distinct property from turning ability, and it is what makes a ship tiring or easy to steer.

Use the chain. Compare shaft power against speed: if more shaft power is needed for the same speed, the loss is aft of the shaft - fouled hull, damaged propeller, or added resistance. Then compare fuel consumption against shaft power: if more fuel is needed for the same shaft power, the loss is in the engine. From the bridge the two look identical; only the chain separates them.

This is the practical payoff of limb A, and it is the answer a Chief Engineer is expected to give.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Limb A - the chain. INDICATED (in the cylinder, from the indicator diagram) → mechanical efficiency, losing friction at rings, crosshead, crankpin and mains plus engine-driven auxiliaries → BRAKE (at the coupling) → transmission and shaft efficiency, losing the thrust block, line bearings, stern tube and any gearing → SHAFT (measured by torsionmeter, torque × angular velocity - the last directly measurable power) → DELIVERED (at the propeller; cannot be measured, only inferred) → open-water efficiency and relative rotative efficiency → THRUST POWER = thrust × SPEED OF ADVANCE (not ship speed) → hull efficiency, from the wake fraction (helps - the propeller works in slower water) and the thrust deduction fraction (hurts - the propeller raises hull resistance), which can exceed unity → EFFECTIVE POWER = total resistance × ship speed = towrope power. Combine as QPC = effective ÷ delivered (hull and propeller) and propulsive coefficient = effective ÷ shaft (whole installation). No typical values - all installation specific. Limb B - trials. Conditions first: deep unrestricted water, low sea and wind, specified loading, steady approach, reciprocal headings, clean hull, calibrated instruments. Then: progressive speed trial (power-speed curve, contract speed, service datum); endurance and consumption; turning circle (advance, transfer, tactical diameter, drift angle, both sides); zig-zag (overshoot angles - yaw-checking and course-changing ability); spiral, reverse spiral, pull-out (course stability); crash stop (head reach, track reach, time); astern and minimum steerage; steering gear hard over to hard over, main and auxiliary; anchoring. Results become the manoeuvring booklet, wheelhouse poster and pilot card.

Critical numbers
  • SIX powers in the chain: indicated, brake, shaft, delivered, thrust, effective
  • Effective power = total resistance × SHIP speed. Thrust power = thrust × SPEED OF ADVANCE
  • Quasi-propulsive coefficient divides by DELIVERED power; propulsive coefficient divides by SHAFT or BRAKE power
  • NO efficiency value or typical range is quoted anywhere - every one is hull-form, propeller and installation specific
  • NO sea trial acceptance criterion is quoted as a number - the manoeuvrability standards are cited by identity only
Critical regulation
None on limb A - no statutory instrument prescribes the power chain, and saying so is correct rather than evasive. On limb B, three instruments are engaged and all three are cited by identity only, with no criterion value and no provision quoted: the IMO standards for ship manoeuvrability, which set the criteria against which turning, yaw-checking, course-keeping and stopping ability are judged; the requirement that manoeuvring information be provided on board, which is what turns the trial results into the manoeuvring booklet, the wheelhouse poster and the pilot card; and the steering gear trial required before the ship enters service, in main and auxiliary modes.
Major trap
Defining thrust power on the ship's speed instead of the speed of advance. The speed of advance is lower because of the wake, and getting this wrong unravels the whole hull-propeller section. Second trap: skipping the hull-propeller interaction and going straight from the shaft to the ship - the wake and thrust deduction pair is where the thinking in limb A is, and stating that hull efficiency can exceed unity with the reason is what distinguishes a strong answer. Third: omitting delivered power from the chain, or confusing the quasi-propulsive coefficient with the propulsive coefficient. Fourth, and specific to limb B: listing trials without their objectives, when the stem asks for 'the typical objectives of each test' in terms - that is half the limb. Fifth: forgetting the trial conditions, above all deep unrestricted water and runs on reciprocal headings, without which the measurements mean nothing. Sixth: quoting typical efficiency values or trial criteria as numbers when both are installation and instrument specific.

Also on the platform: QP2303 Q9 - the oil analysis by which the machinery in this power chain is monitored in service · QP2303 Q4 - the emission control measures applied to the same main engine

First in set First set here. The same task returns at August 2023 Q6.

Longer-term signalNot set in recent papersNewly recurring topicNo currentness risk flagged
Understand this first

Start with why the claim in the question is so appealing and so useless. Somebody looks at a pile of accident reports, notices that in nearly all of them a person did something at the end, and writes down that people cause accidents. But of course a person did something at the end - the ship is run by people, so there is always a human act available to point at. The figure is really telling you where investigations stop, not why accidents happen. And where an investigation stops matters enormously, because everything upstream of the stopping point escapes examination. Now think about what is upstream. The engineer who opened the wrong valve did not choose to have two identical valves side by side, did not decide the lighting, did not set the watch pattern that had him awake for eighteen hours, and did not write the procedure that assumed a plant he does not have. Those things were decided by people ashore, months or years earlier, and every one of them was harmless on the day it was decided. They sit in the ship as traps waiting for an occasion - and the occasion, when it comes, gets called human error. So the whole shift the question is testing for is this: stop asking what the man did wrong, and start asking why it made sense to him at the time. Because a sensible, trained, careful person did it, and if you cannot see why he did it you have not understood the accident yet. The second limb is then just tidiness about words. The element is the whole subject of people in shipping. An error is one event that has already happened. Human factors is the science you use to stop the next one. Field, event, method - and only the last of the three can be applied before anything has gone wrong.

Remember7 route headings
Cover50 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 50 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. (a) Where the 80 per cent claim comes from, and what kind of statement it is

The examiner has printed a claim that circulates constantly in the industry, and the first thing a strong answer does is examine it rather than repeat it. It is not the finding of a study that four accidents in five are caused by defective people. It is an artefact of how investigations attribute causes.

The mechanism is simple, and worth setting out because everything in this limb follows from it. A ship is operated by people. Consequently a human action is present at the end of almost every causal chain - somebody opened something, closed something, ordered something, or failed to notice something. When an investigation reaches that action it has found something concrete, nameable and satisfying, and there is a strong tendency to stop there. Read carefully, the claim therefore says only this: in about eighty per cent of accidents, a human action can be identified somewhere in the chain. In a system operated by human beings that is close to a tautology.

So the figure is a stopping rule, not a finding - a description of where investigations tend to halt, dressed up as a description of why accidents happen. And reading it as a finding has two predictable and damaging consequences:

  • It produces blame. If people cause accidents, the response is to identify the person and discipline him - which teaches everyone else not to report, and removes the information the organisation needs.
  • It produces more procedure. If the person failed to follow the rules, the answer must be more rules - and a procedure written to close a gap that was never the real cause adds workload without adding safety.

Neither response makes the next accident less likely, which is the practical test of whether an explanation is any good.

2. (a) What 'a complex hierarchical socio-technical system' means for a ship

The phrase in the stem is doing real work, and the marks in this limb are in unpacking all three of its words against the ship you actually sail on.

WordWhat it meansOn a ship
Socio-technicalPerformance is produced by people and technology together, along with the procedures they work to and the environment they work in. Neither half can be assessed aloneA purifier does not run itself and an engineer cannot run it without it. What is delivered is the joint output of the machine, the man, the manual and the motion of the ship
ComplexMany parts, tightly coupled, interacting in ways that are not fully foreseeable. Small disturbances propagate; the behaviour of the whole is not the sum of the partsA blackout is never one failure. It is a generator, a protection setting, a standby that did not start, and an alarm that had been accepted an hour earlier
HierarchicalArranged in layers, each of which sets the conditions for the layer below itRegulator and flag State, then class, then the yard that built her, then the company, then the master, and at the bottom the watchkeeper with his hand on the valve

Two consequences follow from the hierarchy, and together they are the intellectual core of the limb.

First, decisions made in the upper layers create the conditions in which the lowest layer acts. The manning level, the watch pattern, the maintenance budget, the spare gear policy, the design of the alarm panel and the wording of the procedure are all settled by people who are not on board and who will not be present when the consequences arrive. The watchkeeper does not choose the conditions he works in; he only acts within them.

Second, those decisions leave latent conditions. A latent condition is a weakness built into the system that does no harm on its own and may lie dormant for years - two identical unlabelled valves side by side, a procedure that has never matched the plant, an alarm nobody can silence without acknowledging six others. It becomes an accident only when it combines with an active failure at the sharp end, on a particular night, with a particular person, under particular pressure.

That is why defences are described as layered, and why a casualty is properly described not as one failure but as the occasion on which the gaps in several defences happened to line up. Every one of those gaps was created by a decision, and most of the decisions were taken a long way from the ship.

3. (a) What 'human error' therefore means here, and what follows

Putting sections 1 and 2 together answers the limb directly. In a complex hierarchical socio-technical system, the term 'human error' denotes the last human action in the chain - the point at which the investigation happened to stop asking why. It is a label applied at a boundary of enquiry, not a description of a cause.

From which four things follow, and they are the substance of the eight marks:

  1. Human error is a symptom, not a cause. It is where the investigation should begin, not where it should end. An investigation that concludes 'human error' has stated its stopping point and called it a finding.
  2. The right question is not what he did wrong, but why it made sense to a competent person at the time. People do not come to work intending to make mistakes. If a reasonable, trained and conscientious person did that thing, then in his situation - with his information, his fatigue, his instrument, his time pressure - it seemed like the reasonable thing to do. Understanding why is the whole of the useful work.
  3. Errors are not eliminated by instructing people not to make them. Posting a notice saying 'be careful' changes nothing, because nobody was intending to be careless. Exhortation is the least effective of all interventions and is very often the only one taken.
  4. Errors are reduced by changing conditions. Interfaces and labelling, the design and the realism of procedures, manning and watch patterns, rest and fatigue management, tools, lighting, and above all a reporting culture in which people say what nearly happened without fear of what will happen to them for saying it.

This is not merely a theoretical position; it is the position international law takes. The Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008, directs a marine safety investigation at the circumstances and the causes of a casualty so that safety may be enhanced - expressly separating that enquiry from the apportionment of blame or liability. The reason is exactly the one above: an investigation conducted to find fault stops at the last human act, and an investigation conducted to prevent recurrence has to go past it.

4. (b) Human Element - the domain

Limb (b) turns on three terms that are used interchangeably in conversation and mean three quite different things. The cleanest way to hold them apart is to notice that each answers a different question.

Human Element is the domain - the subject area. It is the recognition that people are an integral part of the maritime system, and it is the broadest of the three terms. IMO frames it in the Human Element Vision, Principles and Goals at resolution A.947(23) as a complex, multi-dimensional issue affecting maritime safety, security and the marine environment.

Its scope is the point most often got wrong. The human element is not confined to seafarers. It spans ships' crews, shore-based management, regulators, recognized organizations, shipyards, designers and legislators - everyone whose decisions bear on how a ship is operated. Its regulatory expression is the ISM Code for organisation and management, STCW for competence and fitness for duty, and MLC 2006 for hours of rest and living and working conditions.

It answers the question: who and what is in scope?

5. (b) Human Error - the outcome

Human Error is an event. It is a planned action failing to achieve its intended outcome, or a plan that was inadequate for the outcome intended. It is the narrowest of the three terms and it is retrospective - there is no such thing as an error before it happens.

TypeWhat goes wrongExampleWhat actually fixes it
Skill-based slip or lapseRight intention, failed execution - the action was automatic and went astray, or a step was simply forgottenOpening the adjacent valve; omitting a step in a routine changeoverDesign - labelling, separation, layout, checklists
Rule-based mistakeA good rule applied in the wrong situation, or a bad rule applied correctlyFollowing the normal starting procedure in an abnormal condition it was never written forProcedures - and knowing which situation is which
Knowledge-based mistakeA novel situation reasoned through incorrectly, usually under time pressure and with incomplete informationDiagnosing an unfamiliar fault wrongly during a blackoutTraining, decision support, and time

Violations are a separate category and are not errors at all. A violation is deliberate - the person knew the rule and departed from it. They are conventionally distinguished as routine (the shortcut everybody takes), situational (the rule could not be complied with in the circumstances) and exceptional (a departure in an emergency). The important insight is that a routine violation is a statement about the procedure, not about the person: when everybody does it, the procedure does not fit the work, and disciplining an individual will not change that.

It answers the question: what happened?

6. (b) Human Factors - the discipline

Human Factors is the scientific discipline and its application. It is the study of the interaction between people, equipment, procedures, organisation and environment, and the use of that knowledge to design systems that fit the people who operate them - rather than selecting and training people to fit systems that do not.

It is the only one of the three that is proactive and prospective. Element is a field of concern and error is a past event; human factors is a method, and it can be applied to a plant that has not been built yet. Its working territory includes:

  • Workload, fatigue and circadian rhythm - watch patterns, port workload, and the hours at which people are asked to make decisions.
  • Controls, displays and alarms - legibility, grouping, coding, and above all alarm management, because an alarm system that floods is an alarm system that is ignored.
  • Automation and mode awareness - the recurring problem that the operator does not know what the system is currently doing or why.
  • Anthropometry and workspace - reach, access, lighting, noise, vibration and motion.
  • Procedure design - writing instructions that can actually be followed by the person, in the place, in the conditions.
  • Communication and team behaviour - briefing, challenge and response, and the willingness of a junior to speak.

It answers the question: how do we study this, and what do we change?

7. (b) The three distinguished through one incident

The stem asks for examples, and the most economical way to give them is to run one incident through all three terms. Take a constructed illustration - a fatigued engineer, at the end of a long port stay, opens the wrong valve and floods a space.

TermWhat it names in this incidentThe response it implies
Human errorThe act itself - opening that valve. A skill-based slip: the intention was correct and the execution went astrayA warning, a note in the record, a reminder to be careful. Changes nothing
Human factorsWhy the act happened - two identical unlabelled valves side by side, poor lighting at that position, and a watch pattern that put him there at the end of eighteen hoursRedesign - label and separate the valves, light the space, and fix the watch pattern
Human elementThe whole territory around it - the manning level that produced the eighteen hours, the training, the maintenance budget that left the labels off, and a reporting culture in which nobody had mentioned the valves beforeOrganisational change - manning, resources, and the safety management system under which all of it is decided

So the distinction, in one line each: the element is the field, the factors are the method, and the error is the event. Put the other way round - error is what you see, human factors is how you explain and prevent it, and the human element is the territory both of them sit in.

And the reason the distinction matters practically rather than merely semantically is in the third column of that table. Each term implies a different response, and only one of the three responses would stop it happening to the next engineer.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Eight and eight, both printed - and note that this paper splits the marks evenly where the same question elsewhere is set 6 and 10. Here limb (a) is worth exactly as much as limb (b), which means the analysis of the 80 per cent claim and of the socio-technical system is half the question, not a preamble to the definitions. Candidates who race through (a) to reach the three definitions throw away four marks.

Limb (a) is an argument: examine the claim, unpack the phrase, and state what follows. Limb (b) is a discrimination: three terms, three different questions answered, one worked example. Do not write limb (b)'s definitions inside limb (a).

The one sentence that carries limb (a)

The 80 per cent figure is a stopping rule, not a finding. It describes where investigations halt, not why accidents happen. A person is present at the end of almost every chain in a system operated by people, so finding one is close to a tautology. Write that, and the rest of the limb is the reasoning behind it.

Unpack all three words in the phrase

  • Socio-technical - people and technology produce the outcome jointly; neither can be assessed alone.
  • Complex - many tightly coupled parts interacting unforeseeably. A blackout is never one failure.
  • Hierarchical - layers, from regulator and flag through class, yard and company to the watchkeeper - and each layer sets the conditions for the one below.

Then the two consequences: upper-layer decisions create the conditions the sharp end works in, and they leave latent conditions that do no harm until they meet an active failure on a particular night. That is what layered defences and lined-up gaps mean.

The four things that follow, and the one question to ask

Error is a symptom, not a cause. Errors are not removed by telling people not to make them. They are reduced by changing conditions. And the question that replaces 'what did he do wrong?' is: why did that action make sense to a competent person at the time? If you write nothing else in limb (a), write that question - it is the whole of modern casualty thinking in nine words, and it is why the Casualty Investigation Code, MSC.255(84), is directed at circumstances and causes rather than at blame.

The three terms, one question each

TermWhat it isThe question it answersDirection
Human ElementThe domain - the broadest term. Framed by IMO at A.947(23); not confined to seafarersWho and what is in scope?Standing
Human ErrorThe outcome - an event, the narrowest termWhat happened?Retrospective
Human FactorsThe discipline - study and designHow do we study it, and what do we change?Prospective - the only proactive one

The error taxonomy, and the category that is not an error

  • Skill-based slip or lapse - right intention, failed execution. Fixed by design.
  • Rule-based mistake - good rule in the wrong situation, or a bad rule applied properly. Fixed by procedures.
  • Knowledge-based mistake - a novel situation reasoned through wrongly. Fixed by training and time.
  • Violations are NOT errors - they are deliberate. Routine, situational or exceptional. And a routine violation is a statement about the procedure, not about the person.

Run one incident through all three

The tired engineer who opens the wrong valve. The error is the act - a skill-based slip. The factors are the identical unlabelled valves, the lighting and the watch pattern. The element is the manning, the training, the budget and the reporting culture behind all of it. And note what each implies: a warning, a redesign, or a change of manning - only two of which would prevent a recurrence. One example, three terms, and the discrimination is demonstrated rather than asserted.

Common mistakes

  • Accepting the 80 per cent figure as a finding and then explaining why seafarers are careless. That is the trap the limb is built around.
  • Treating limb (a) as a preamble. On this paper it is worth eight marks, the same as limb (b).
  • Unpacking 'socio-technical' but ignoring 'complex' and 'hierarchical'. All three words carry marks.
  • Omitting latent conditions, without which the hierarchy argument has no conclusion.
  • Defining the human element as 'the seafarer'. It spans shore management, regulators, class, yards and legislators.
  • Treating human factors as a synonym for human error, when it is the discipline that studies and prevents it.
  • Calling a violation an error. It is deliberate, and the two need different responses.
  • Giving three definitions and no example, when the stem asks for examples expressly.
  • Quoting a statistic in reply. The figure in the stem is the thing being examined; offering another does not help.

Examiner traps

  • "Do you agree that 80 per cent of accidents are caused by human error?" Not as stated. A human action can be identified in about that proportion of chains, which is different - and nearly a tautology in a system run by people.
  • "Is human error a cause?" No - a symptom, and the point at which the investigation stopped asking why.
  • "How do you stop people making mistakes?" You do not. You change the conditions - labelling, layout, procedures, manning, rest, reporting.
  • "Give me a latent condition on your own ship." Two identical unlabelled valves; a procedure that does not match the plant; an alarm that floods.
  • "Is a violation an error?" No - it is deliberate. And a routine violation says the procedure does not fit the work.
  • "Is the human element about seafarers?" No - it includes shore management, regulators, class, shipyards and legislators.
  • "Which of the three is proactive?" Human factors, because it is a design discipline and applies before anything has gone wrong.
  • "Why does the Casualty Investigation Code separate the safety investigation from blame?" Because an enquiry conducted to find fault stops at the last human act, and one conducted to prevent recurrence has to go past it.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Tell me about a near miss you reported, and what happened to the report afterwards.
  • Would your junior engineer tell you if he had made a mistake nobody else had seen? What makes you sure?
  • How many alarms does your main engine control room show on a normal watch, and how many do you act on?
  • Show me two valves on this plant that could be confused, and tell me why they still can be.
  • How are your hours of rest recorded, and are the records true?
  • What did your company change after the last incident on this ship?

Memory framework

Limb (a): claim, phrase, consequence. The 80 per cent is a stopping rule (1); socio-technical, complex, hierarchical, giving latent conditions plus an active failure (2); therefore error is a symptom, and the question is why it made sense at the time (3). Limb (b): domain, event, discipline - then one incident. ELEMENT = who is in scope (4). ERROR = what happened (5). FACTORS = how we study and change it (6). Then the valve: act, conditions, organisation (7).

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from
Investigation directed at circumstances and causes, not blameCasualty Investigation Code, resolution MSC.255(84) of 16 May 2008, mandatory from 1 January 2010
IMO's framing of the human element and its scopeResolution A.947(23), Human Element Vision, Principles and Goals. Not held by MIW; cited by identity only
The organisational expression - management, resources, reportingISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92), cited at regime level
Competence and fitness for duty; hours of restSTCW and MLC 2006, cited at regime level
Latent conditions, active failures, layered defences, the error taxonomyEstablished safety-science reference. No author, publication or model name is cited, because MIW holds none
The worked incidentA constructed illustration. No named casualty appears anywhere

Uncertainty and evidence notes

The 80 per cent figure is not endorsed anywhere in this answer, and no alternative statistic is offered in its place. It is the examiner's printed premise and the answer treats it as a claim to be examined - which is the substance of limb (a). No casualty investigation report and no statistical study is cited, because MIW holds none.

No author, publication, model name or named casualty appears. The organisational-accident frame - latent conditions, active failures and layered defences with gaps that line up - and the error taxonomy of slips, lapses, rule-based and knowledge-based mistakes are declared as established safety-science reference and are explained mechanistically rather than attributed to their originators, because MIW holds no such source. The worked incident closing limb (b) is a constructed engine-room illustration and is presented as one.

The instruments are cited at regime level or by identity only. Resolution A.947(23) is not held by MIW: it is named for the Organization's framing of the human element, with no text quoted and no paragraph asserted. MIW holds no text-bearing copy of the ISM Code, of STCW or of MLC 2006, and no provision number is asserted for any of them. Only the Casualty Investigation Code was read at source, and then only for its identity and its stated purpose.

The dated position is stable and two future items are deliberately absent. Everything relied on here was operative in March 2023 and nothing on this question turns on a boundary. The MLC 2006 2022 amendments did not enter into force until 23 December 2024, twenty-one months after this examination, and MSC.560(108) on mandatory training against harassment and bullying was adopted in 2024 and entered into force in 2026. Both are human-element material and neither is named anywhere. Resolution A.947(23) is a 23rd Assembly instrument and is entirely unaffected by the 33rd Assembly boundary of 6 December 2023 that governs other questions on this paper.

Four printed divergences from the stem set elsewhere are preserved rather than repaired, including an opening quotation mark that this paper never closes. They are reproduced exactly as printed in the transcribed question, and are recorded in the paper's anchor. Where the sentence is quoted back in the answer it is given in a form the reader can follow.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) (a) Where the 80 per cent claim comes from, and what kind of statement it is
  2. __________(a) (a) What 'a complex hierarchical socio-technical system' means for a ship
  3. __________(a) (a) What 'human error' therefore means here, and what follows
  4. __________(b) (b) Human Element - the domain
  5. __________(b) (b) Human Error - the outcome
  6. __________(b) (b) Human Factors - the discipline
  7. __________(b) (b) The three distinguished through one incident
Flashcards 10

Limb (a): 1 where the 80 per cent claim comes from and what kind of statement it is. 2 socio-technical, complex and hierarchical unpacked, ending on latent conditions. 3 what human error therefore means, and the four consequences. Limb (b): 4 human element, the domain. 5 human error, the event. 6 human factors, the discipline. 7 one incident read three ways.

This paper prints 8 and 8, so limb (a) carries half the marks and needs three developed sections rather than a preamble.

The figure describes where investigations stop, not why accidents happen. Read as a finding it produces blame and more procedure, and neither makes the next accident less likely.

This is the whole of limb (a) in one answer, and the trap the question is built around.

Socio-technical: performance is the joint output of people, technology, procedures and environment - neither half can be assessed alone. Complex: many tightly coupled parts interacting in ways not fully foreseeable, so a blackout is never one failure. Hierarchical: layers from regulator and flag through class, yard and company to the watchkeeper - and each layer sets the conditions for the one below it.

All three words carry marks, and the hierarchy is what leads to latent conditions.

A weakness built into the system by an earlier decision - manning, budget, layout, procedure wording, alarm design - that does no harm on its own and may lie dormant for years. It becomes an accident only when it combines with an active failure at the sharp end on a particular occasion. Defences are layered, and a casualty is the occasion on which the gaps in several of them line up.

Without latent conditions, the hierarchy argument in limb (a) has no conclusion.

It is a symptom, not a cause - a label applied at the point where the investigation stopped asking why. The question that replaces it is: why did that action make sense to a competent person at the time? Nobody comes to work intending to make a mistake, so if a trained and conscientious person did it, it seemed reasonable in his situation - and understanding why is the whole of the useful work.

Nine words that contain the entire modern approach to casualty investigation.

Human element is the domain - the broadest term, framed by IMO at resolution A.947(23), and not confined to seafarers: it spans shore management, regulators, recognized organizations, shipyards and legislators. It answers who is in scope. Human error is the event - narrowest and retrospective. It answers what happened. Human factors is the discipline, the only proactive one. It answers how we study it and what we change.

Three terms, three different questions answered - which is the cleanest way to keep them apart under pressure.

Skill-based slips and lapses - right intention, failed execution; fixed by design. Rule-based mistakes - a good rule in the wrong situation, or a bad rule applied correctly. Knowledge-based mistakes - a novel situation reasoned through wrongly under pressure. Violations are NOT errors: they are deliberate, and are classed routine, situational or exceptional. A routine violation is a statement about the procedure, not about the person.

Conflating violations with errors is the discrimination the limb tests, and each type needs a different remedy.

No, and this is the commonest misunderstanding of the term. IMO frames it as a complex, multi-dimensional issue involving the entire spectrum of human activity in shipping - ships' crews, shore-based management, regulators, recognized organizations, shipyards, designers and legislators. Confining it to the crew reproduces exactly the error that limb (a) exposes.

The scope of the term is where the mark is, and getting it wrong contradicts the first limb.

A fatigued engineer opens the wrong valve and floods a space. The error is the act - a skill-based slip. The factors are the conditions - two identical unlabelled valves, poor lighting, and a watch pattern that put him there after eighteen hours. The element is the territory - manning, training, the budget that left the labels off, and a reporting culture in which nobody had raised the valves. Each implies a different response: a warning, a redesign, or a change of manning.

The stem asks for examples, and one incident read three ways demonstrates the distinction instead of asserting it.

Because they cannot be done together. The Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008 and mandatory from 1 January 2010, directs a marine safety investigation at the circumstances and causes of a casualty so that safety may be enhanced. An enquiry conducted to apportion fault stops at the last human act, and an enquiry conducted to prevent recurrence has to go past it - and people only tell the truth to the second kind.

It is the point at which limb (a)'s argument becomes international law rather than opinion.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Limb (a). The 80 per cent claim is not a finding but a stopping rule - a human act is present at the end of almost every chain in a system run by people, so identifying one is nearly a tautology; read as a finding it produces blame and more procedure, neither of which prevents recurrence. Socio-technical = people and technology produce the outcome jointly; complex = tightly coupled parts interacting unforeseeably; hierarchical = regulator, flag, class, yard, company, master, watchkeeper, each layer setting the conditions for the one below. Upper-layer decisions leave latent conditions that do no harm until they meet an active failure and the gaps in the defences line up. So 'human error' names the last human act, where the investigation stopped - a symptom, not a cause. Ask why the action made sense to a competent person at the time. Errors fall with changed conditions, not with exhortation - which is why the Casualty Investigation Code, MSC.255(84) of 16 May 2008 seeks circumstances and causes, not blame. Limb (b). ELEMENT = the domain, the broadest term, framed at A.947(23), not confined to seafarers - it includes shore management, regulators, class, yards and legislators; expressed through ISM, STCW and MLC. Answers who is in scope. ERROR = the event, narrowest and retrospective - skill-based slips and lapses (fix by design), rule-based mistakes, knowledge-based mistakes; violations are deliberate and are not errors, and a routine violation indicts the procedure. Answers what happened. FACTORS = the discipline, the only proactive one - workload and fatigue, controls, displays and alarms, automation and mode awareness, workspace, procedure design, team communication. Answers how we study it and what we change. One incident three ways: the tired engineer at the wrong valve - error is the act, factors are the unlabelled valves and the watch pattern, element is the manning and the culture.
