Critical numbers
  • The 80 per cent figure is the examiner's premise and is NOT endorsed. No alternative statistic is offered anywhere in this answer
  • Casualty Investigation Code - resolution MSC.255(84), adopted 16 May 2008, mandatory from 1 January 2010
  • Human Element Vision, Principles and Goals - resolution A.947(23). Not held; cited by identity only
  • Three error types plus a fourth category that is not an error at all: slips and lapses, rule-based mistakes, knowledge-based mistakes - and violations
  • This paper prints 8 and 8. The same question elsewhere prints 6 and 10, so limb (a) is worth twice as much here
Critical regulation
The Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008 and mandatory from 1 January 2010, directs a marine safety investigation at the circumstances and the causes of a casualty so that safety may be enhanced, and separates that enquiry from the apportionment of blame or liability. That separation exists for exactly the reason limb (a) is about: an investigation conducted to find fault stops at the last human act, and one conducted to prevent recurrence has to go past it.
Major trap
Accepting the 80 per cent figure as a measured finding and then writing an essay on why seafarers are careless. The limb is built to catch precisely that, and the figure is a stopping rule describing where investigations halt. Second trap: treating limb (a) as a preamble to the definitions, when on this paper it is printed at eight marks - the same as limb (b) - and the same question elsewhere splits 6 and 10. Third: defining the human element as the seafarer, when it expressly spans shore management, regulators, recognized organizations, shipyards and legislators. Fourth: treating human factors as a synonym for human error, when one is the discipline that prevents and the other is the event that happened. Fifth: calling a violation an error - violations are deliberate, and a routine violation is evidence against the procedure rather than against the person. Sixth: giving three definitions and no example, when the stem asks for examples in terms.

Also on the platform: QP2303 Q7 - corrective and preventive action, which is what an organisation does with what it learns here · QP2303 Q5 - the machinery and trials whose operation the human element sits around

First in set First set here. The same task returns at November 2024 Q8.

Longer-term signalNewly recurring topicActive in recent papersNo currentness risk flagged
Understand this first

Both halves of this question are really about the same instinct, which is why the examiner has put them together. Start with the port State officer. He has not come aboard to survey your ship - he has come to check that the certificates somebody else issued are being lived up to. So he looks at the paperwork, walks through the engine room and the accommodation, and forms an impression. If nothing he sees contradicts the certificates, that is the end of it, and he leaves. What lets him go further is clear grounds, and the two words that define it are worth holding on to: he needs evidence - something actually observed, not a feeling about the flag or the owner - and the discrepancy has to be substantial, going to whether the ship complies rather than to whether she is tidy. And here is the part engineers underestimate: he does not need to find a single broken thing. If your watchkeeper cannot show him how the steering gear changeover works, that is clear grounds on its own, because a ship's equipment is only worth what the people using it know about it. Now the second half. When something does go wrong, there are three quite different things you can do about it and they get muddled constantly. You can fix the broken thing - that is a correction, and it is where most safety management systems quietly stop. You can find out why it broke and change that, so it does not break again - that is corrective action, and it only counts if you go back later and check it worked. Or you can look at everything else that could break the same way, and get to it first - that is preventive action. One word separates the last two: corrective action follows something that has happened; preventive action forestalls something that has not. And the connection back to the first limb is exact. The deficiency the officer wrote on your report is corrective action for your ship - and preventive action for every other ship in the fleet that he has not visited yet.

Knowledge map

Bullet answer — points to write
a) · 8 marks
  1. Where the power comes from, and what an inspection is before clear grounds exist
    • The power is in the conventions - SOLAS I/19, MARPOL 5 and 6, Load Lines 21, STCW X and I/4, MLC Title 5.2
    • IMO's Procedures tell an officer how to exercise it and supply the vocabulary - they do not create it
    • The adopting resolution only invites Governments to implement them
    • The initial inspection is limited: certificates and documents, and the general condition of the ship
    • If the certificates are in order and nothing contradicts them, the inspection ends there
    • Clear grounds is therefore the boundary between an hour's visit and a day alongside
  2. 'Clear grounds' - the definition, and the two words that carry it
    • Evidence that ship, equipment or crew does not correspond substantially with convention requirements
    • OR that master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures for safety or pollution prevention
    • EVIDENCE excludes suspicion, reputation, rumour and the flag - something must be observed
    • SUBSTANTIALLY excludes the trivial, the cosmetic and the isolated minor defect
    • Crew unfamiliarity is a free-standing head - a materially perfect ship can be opened up on it alone
  3. The circumstances that create clear grounds - with examples
    • Documents - certificates absent, expired, invalid, mismatched or apparently falsified; an Oil Record Book that will not reconcile
    • Equipment - required equipment absent, or present but bypassed, blanked, corroded or plainly unmaintained
    • Condition - heavy corrosion, unsafe machinery spaces, unsanitary accommodation; a report from a pilot or terminal
    • People - crew unable to start the emergency generator or change over the steering gear; rest hours inconsistent with the watch pattern
    • ISM-related findings are a class apart - certificates missing or mismatched, uncontrolled or superseded documentation, a master who cannot explain his authority
    • They point at the system, so nothing else on board can be relied on - which widens the inspection
  4. What follows once clear grounds exist
    • A more detailed inspection, scoped by the grounds - not a general re-survey
    • Rectification, mostly before departure or within a stated period
    • Detention only on the test: clearly hazardous to safety, health or the environment, or so numerous as to show systemic failure
    • Notification to the flag State in sufficient detail, and to the recognized organization that issued the certificates
    • Proceeding to a repair port on conditions where the defect cannot be remedied where she lies

    • Operated regionally - the Indian Ocean MOU, DG Shipping, and the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958

    • The difference reduces to one word: DETECTED against POTENTIAL
    • Correction is NOT corrective action - the commonest failure in the subject
    • They are not degrees of the same thing
  2. Corrective action - what it actually involves
    • The ISM safety management system must provide for reporting, investigation and analysis, and for corrective action
    • Contain and correct - and do not stop there

    • Decide the action against the cause, not against the event; assign an owner and a date

  3. Preventive action - where it comes from

Memory cue. Limb a - before, trigger, examples, after. BEFORE (1): power in the conventions; initial inspection is certificates and general condition. TRIGGER (2): EVIDENCE of a SUBSTANTIAL departure, or crew unfamiliarity. EXAMPLES (3): documents, equipment, condition, people - ISM findings widen everything. AFTER (4): scoped detailed inspection, detain only if clearly hazardous, notify flag and RO. Limb b - three terms, one word. Correction fixes the thing (5); corrective action removes the cause of a DETECTED one and must be verified (6); preventive action removes the cause of a POTENTIAL one (7); one cooler tube shows all three (8).

Remember8 route headings
Cover53 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 53 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. Where the power comes from, and what an inspection is before clear grounds exist

The phrase clear grounds cannot be explained without first saying what it is a threshold between, and the marks in the first half of limb a depend on getting that structure right.

The power to inspect at all comes from the conventions, each of which carries its own control article - SOLAS regulation I/19, MARPOL articles 5 and 6, Load Lines 1966 article 21, STCW article X and regulation I/4, and MLC 2006 Title 5.2. IMO's consolidated Procedures for Port State Control tell an officer how to exercise that power and supply the common vocabulary; they do not create it, because the resolution adopting them merely invites Governments to implement them. That distinction is worth a sentence in the answer, and it is why the substance below does not depend on which edition of the Procedures happens to be current.

The initial inspection is deliberately limited. On boarding, the officer is entitled to look at:

  • the certificates and documents required by the conventions, checking that they are present, valid and issued by or on behalf of the flag State;
  • the general condition of the ship, including the engine room, the deck, the accommodation and hygiene - what an experienced eye takes in while walking through.

If the certificates are in order and nothing seen contradicts them, that is where the inspection ends. The officer has no general licence to take the ship apart. What entitles him to go further is precisely the existence of clear grounds - and that is why the term matters commercially as much as legally: it is the boundary between an hour's visit and a day alongside.

2. 'Clear grounds' - the definition, and the two words that carry it

Clear grounds are evidence that the ship, her equipment or her crew does not correspond substantially with the requirements of the relevant conventions, or that the master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to the safety of ships or the prevention of pollution.

Two words in that formulation do all the work, and a candidate who identifies them has essentially answered the limb:

WordWhat it excludesConsequence
EVIDENCESuspicion, reputation, rumour, the flag she flies, and the officer's general feeling about the shipThere must be something observed. Clear grounds are found, not assumed
SUBSTANTIALLYThe trivial, the cosmetic and the isolated minor defectA ship is not opened up because of one burnt-out lamp. The discrepancy must go to compliance, not to tidiness

There is a third element that is easy to miss and is genuinely different in kind: the crew's unfamiliarity with essential procedures is itself clear grounds, independently of the condition of any equipment. A ship may be in perfect material order and still be opened up because the watchkeepers cannot demonstrate how they would use what they have.

3. The circumstances that create clear grounds - with examples

The stem asks for examples in terms, and the most useful way to give them is to group them by what the officer was looking at when the clear grounds arose.

Where they ariseExamples
Documents and certificatesA certificate absent, expired or invalid; a certificate that does not match the ship or her particulars; apparent falsification; an Oil Record Book with gaps, or entries that do not reconcile with the sludge and bilge quantities actually on board; a garbage or ballast record that has plainly been written up in one sitting
EquipmentPrincipal equipment or arrangements required by the conventions and simply not there; equipment present but visibly unmaintained, corroded, blanked, disconnected or bypassed; an oily water separator whose 15 ppm alarm and stopping arrangement cannot be demonstrated; lifeboat or fire-fighting equipment obviously not exercised
Condition of the shipSerious deterioration or heavy corrosion of the hull, hatch covers or structure; plainly unsafe conditions in the machinery spaces - accumulated oil, defeated guards, leaking fuel lines; obviously unsanitary accommodation or galley; a report from the pilot, the terminal or another authority that the ship is deficient
People and proceduresThe crew unable to demonstrate familiarity with essential operations - starting the emergency generator, changing over the steering gear, operating the fixed fire-fighting system, mustering for abandonment; the master unable to explain his authority under the safety management system; watchkeepers whose certification or rest hours plainly do not support the watch pattern being worked

One class of clear grounds deserves separate treatment because of what it implies. Where the deficiency points at the safety management system rather than at an item - a Safety Management Certificate or Document of Compliance missing, invalid or not matching the ship; system documentation that is uncontrolled, superseded, or in a language the crew cannot read; the master unaware of the review he is required to make - the officer is entitled to conclude that nothing else on board can be relied upon either, because the system that was supposed to be maintaining all of it is not working. That is why an ISM-related finding tends to widen an inspection rather than narrow it.

4. What follows once clear grounds exist

  1. A more detailed inspection, and it is directed at the areas in which the clear grounds arose. It is not a general re-survey of the ship - the clear grounds define its scope as well as authorising it.
  2. Rectification. Deficiencies are recorded, and most are cleared before departure or within a stated period.
  3. Detention, but only on a defined test: where the deficiencies are clearly hazardous to safety, health or the environment, or where they are so numerous that together they show the safety management system has failed. Detention is not the ordinary outcome of a detailed inspection.
  4. Notification. The flag State is informed in sufficient detail for it to assess the severity, and so is the recognized organization that issued the certificates - which is the point at which this question meets the RO question on this same paper.
  5. Proceeding to a repair port where the deficiency cannot be remedied where she lies, on conditions, with the next port and the flag State notified.

And two limits run through all of it. First, every convention carrying a control article also carries a duty that all possible efforts shall be made to avoid a ship being unduly detained or delayed, with compensation for loss where she is - MARPOL article 7(2) being the clearest expression of it. Second, the whole system is operated regionally: port States coordinate through memoranda of understanding, India being a member of the Indian Ocean MOU, with inspections in Indian ports carried out by surveyors of DG Shipping under the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. A memorandum of understanding is an administrative agreement between authorities and not a convention, and it is worth saying so.

5. The three terms that must be separated

Limb b asks for a difference between two terms, and the reliable way to establish it is to introduce a third term that is habitually confused with both. All three are defined by what they act upon:

TermActs uponTimingIn one sentence
Correction (or immediate remedial action)The non-conformity itselfNowFix the thing that is wrong
Corrective actionThe CAUSE of a DETECTED non-conformityAfter it has happenedStop it happening again
Preventive actionThe CAUSE of a POTENTIAL non-conformityBefore it has happenedStop it happening at all

So the difference the stem asks for reduces to one word: DETECTED against POTENTIAL. Corrective action responds to something that has actually occurred and removes the cause so that it does not recur. Preventive action responds to something that has not occurred - a risk, a weakness, a trend, a near miss - and removes the cause so that it never does.

Two consequences follow immediately, and both carry marks:

  • Correction is not corrective action, and confusing them is the commonest failure in the whole subject. Replacing a failed component is a correction. It leaves the cause exactly where it was, and the same component will fail again. A great many safety management systems are full of closed non-conformities that were only ever corrected.
  • The two are not different degrees of the same thing. They are distinguished by whether an event has occurred, not by how thorough the response was.

6. Corrective action - what it actually involves

Corrective action is a process and not a decision, and the ISM Code requires the company to have procedures for it - the safety management system must provide for non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences to be reported to the company, investigated and analysed with the object of improving safety and pollution prevention, and for corrective action to be implemented. The steps are:

  1. Contain, and correct. Make the situation safe and deal with the immediate defect. This is the correction, and it is where the process is not allowed to stop.
  2. Establish what actually happened, from records and from the people involved, before forming a view about why.
  3. Find the cause - and go past the first plausible one. 'The engineer opened the wrong valve' is not a cause; it is where the enquiry stopped. Why were two identical valves unlabelled? Why had nobody reported it? Why did the procedure not match the plant?
  4. Decide the action, and decide it against the cause found rather than against the event. That may be a change to a procedure, to the planned maintenance system, to labelling or layout, to training, or to manning.
  5. Assign it, with an owner and a date. An action without either is a note.
  6. Implement, and record.
  7. VERIFY EFFECTIVENESS. This is the step most often omitted and the one an auditor will test: not was the action done, but did it work - has the non-conformity stopped recurring? An action closed without verification has not been closed.
  8. Feed it back, through the management review and to the rest of the fleet.

The inputs to corrective action are, by definition, things that have already gone wrong: internal and external audit non-conformities, port State control deficiencies and detentions, class conditions and recommendations, accidents, hazardous occurrences, equipment failures and defect reports.

7. Preventive action - where it comes from

Preventive action uses the same machinery but is fed by a completely different set of inputs - things that have not happened, or that have happened somewhere else:

  • Near misses and hazardous occurrences. The most valuable input there is, and the one that depends entirely on people being willing to report - which is why a blame culture destroys preventive action before it destroys anything else.
  • Risk assessment of a job, a modification or an unusual operation, carried out before the work rather than after the injury.
  • Audit observations - findings that are not non-conformities but point at a weakness.
  • Trend analysis. Rising alarm counts, creeping defect backlogs, repeated minor findings in the same area, deteriorating oil analysis results. A trend is a non-conformity that has not arrived yet.
  • Fleet and industry experience. A casualty on a sister ship, a flag State or class circular, an engine builder's service letter, a published investigation report. Somebody else has already had the accident, which is the cheapest possible way to learn.
  • The port State control record of other ships. A deficiency recurring across the fleet is a warning to every ship that has not yet been inspected.

Preventive action is the harder discipline, for two reasons worth stating. First, it competes for resources against work that is visibly urgent, and nothing visibly bad is happening. Second, when it succeeds, nothing happens - which is very difficult to point at. It survives only where it is deliberately planned, resourced and reviewed, and it is the first thing to disappear on a ship that is behind on everything else.

8. The two worked through one defect, and how the limbs connect

Take a single, ordinary engine-room event: a sea water cooler tube fails and floods the lubricating oil system.

ResponseWhat is doneWhat it achieves
CorrectionPlug or renew the tube, clean and renew the oil charge, return the plant to serviceThe ship works again. The cause is untouched and the next tube will go
Corrective actionEstablish why it failed - erosion at the inlet, exhausted cathodic protection, a cooling water treatment regime not being followed, an inspection interval that had quietly been extended. Then change the thing that allowed it: revise the PMS interval, restore and monitor the protection, re-train on water treatment, and verify at the next opening that the wastage has stoppedThat cooler will not fail the same way again
Preventive actionAsk which other coolers on this ship - and on her sisters - share the material, the duty and the treatment regime, and inspect or re-interval them before any of them leaks. Issue the finding to the fleetThe failure does not happen on the other units at all

And that example shows why the examiner has put these two limbs on the same question, which is worth a closing sentence. A port State control deficiency is an input to corrective action: something was found, on this ship, and the cause must be removed so that it is not found again. The same deficiency, read across the fleet, is an input to preventive action: the ships that have not yet been inspected can be put right before anyone looks. A company that only ever responds to what inspectors find is running its safety management system on corrective action alone - and it will be inspected, and detained, at exactly the rate its worst ship deserves.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Eight and eight, both printed - one of only two questions on this paper that print a complete sixteen. The two limbs are unrelated at first sight: limb a is enforcement law, limb b is safety-management vocabulary. Answer them separately and properly, then join them in one closing sentence, which is where the extra mark lives.

Limb a asks with examples and limb b asks for a difference. Both of those instructions are marking schemes in miniature: a definition of clear grounds without examples is half an answer, and two descriptions without a stated point of difference is not a comparison.

Set up limb a with the threshold

Clear grounds is a boundary, so say what lies on each side of it. Before: the officer may look at certificates and documents and the general condition of the ship, and no more. After: he may carry out a more detailed inspection, directed at the areas the clear grounds arose in. Stating the boundary first makes the definition mean something.

The definition, and the two words in it

Evidence that the ship, her equipment or her crew does not correspond SUBSTANTIALLY with the requirements of the conventions, or that master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to safety or pollution prevention.

  • EVIDENCE - excludes suspicion, reputation and the flag. Something must have been observed.
  • SUBSTANTIALLY - excludes the trivial. One failed lamp is not clear grounds.
  • And the third element people forget: unfamiliarity of the crew is itself clear grounds, whatever the state of the equipment.

Examples - group them, do not list them

Documents, equipment, condition, people. Four headings, two or three examples each, and the answer looks organised rather than remembered: certificates absent or expired and an Oil Record Book that will not reconcile; equipment missing, bypassed or plainly unmaintained; heavy corrosion and unsafe machinery spaces; and a crew who cannot start the emergency generator or change over the steering gear.

The ISM-related class, and why it widens everything

Where the finding points at the system rather than at an item - certificates missing or not matching, uncontrolled or superseded documentation, documentation in a language the crew cannot read, a master who cannot explain his authority - the officer is entitled to conclude that nothing else on board can be relied on either. That is why an ISM finding widens an inspection instead of narrowing it, and saying so shows you understand what the officer is actually reasoning about.

Limb b - introduce a third term to define the two

TermActs uponIn one sentence
CorrectionThe non-conformity itselfFix the thing that is wrong
Corrective actionThe cause of a DETECTED non-conformityStop it happening again
Preventive actionThe cause of a POTENTIAL non-conformityStop it happening at all

The difference reduces to one word: DETECTED against POTENTIAL. Both attack a cause; they differ in whether the event has already happened. And correction is not corrective action - that is the confusion the limb is really testing, and a safety management system full of closed non-conformities that were only corrected is the practical proof of it.

The step everyone omits

Verification of effectiveness. Not was the action taken, but did it work - has the non-conformity stopped recurring? An action closed without verification has not been closed, and an auditor will go straight to it. Write the word 'effectiveness' into the answer.

Where each one's inputs come from

  • Corrective - audit non-conformities, PSC deficiencies and detentions, class conditions, accidents, hazardous occurrences, defect reports. Things that went wrong here.
  • Preventive - near misses, risk assessments, audit observations, trend analysis, fleet and industry experience, circulars and service letters, other ships' PSC records. Things that went wrong elsewhere, or have not gone wrong yet.

One example, three responses

A cooler tube fails and floods the lubricating oil. Correction: plug the tube, renew the oil. Corrective action: find why it wasted - erosion, exhausted cathodic protection, water treatment not followed, an interval quietly extended - change that, and verify at the next opening. Preventive action: check every other cooler of the same material and duty on this ship and her sisters before one of them leaks. One example, three answers, and the distinction is demonstrated rather than asserted.

Common mistakes

  • Defining clear grounds as 'anything that looks wrong'. It is evidence of a substantial departure.
  • Omitting the crew unfamiliarity element, which is a free-standing head of clear grounds.
  • Giving the definition and no examples, when the stem asks for them in terms.
  • Saying the port State's power comes from the IMO Procedures. It comes from the conventions; the Procedures are guidance.
  • Treating the detailed inspection as a general re-survey. Its scope is set by the clear grounds.
  • Saying every deficiency leads to detention. Detention needs deficiencies clearly hazardous to safety, health or the environment, or so numerous as to show systemic failure.
  • Confusing correction with corrective action - the central error of limb b.
  • Saying preventive action is 'more thorough' corrective action. The difference is detected against potential, not degree.
  • Omitting verification of effectiveness.
  • Never joining the two limbs, when the examiner has plainly put them together for a reason.

Examiner traps

  • "What may the officer look at before he has clear grounds?" Certificates and documents, and the general condition of the ship. Nothing more.
  • "Is a dirty engine room clear grounds?" Only if it evidences a substantial departure - accumulated oil, defeated guards, leaking fuel lines. Untidiness alone is not.
  • "Your crew cannot start the emergency generator, but everything is certificated. Clear grounds?" Yes - unfamiliarity with essential procedures stands on its own.
  • "Where does the port State's power come from?" The control article of each convention - SOLAS I/19, MARPOL 5 and 6, Load Lines 21, STCW X.
  • "You replaced the failed pump. Is that corrective action?" No - that is a correction. Corrective action removes the cause.
  • "Give me a preventive action you took this month." It must be something that had not gone wrong here - a near miss, a trend, a service letter, a sister ship's casualty.
  • "How do you know your corrective action worked?" Verification of effectiveness - the non-conformity has stopped recurring, and there is a record saying so.
  • "A PSC deficiency - corrective or preventive?" Corrective for this ship; preventive for the rest of the fleet. That answer wins the question.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Show me your last PSC report and take me through what you did about each item.
  • Who raises a non-conformity on your ship, and what happens to it after you sign it off?
  • How many near misses were reported on your last voyage, and what came of them?
  • What did your company do after the last detention anywhere in the fleet?
  • How would you demonstrate to a PSCO that your crew are familiar with the steering gear changeover?
  • What is the difference between a non-conformity and a major non-conformity?

Memory framework

Limb a - before, trigger, after. BEFORE (1): the power is in the conventions; the initial inspection is certificates and general condition. TRIGGER (2): evidence of a substantial departure, or crew unfamiliarity. EXAMPLES (3): documents, equipment, condition, people - and ISM findings widen everything. AFTER (4): detailed inspection scoped by the grounds, rectify, detain only if clearly hazardous, notify flag and RO. Limb b - three terms, one word. Correction fixes the thing (5); corrective action removes the cause of a DETECTED one (6); preventive action removes the cause of a POTENTIAL one (7); and one cooler tube shows all three (8).

Uncertainty and currency

The edition of IMO's consolidated Procedures for Port State Control in circulation at this sitting is not held by MIW, and that limitation is stated rather than worked around. The only edition available for this answer is the set adopted by the 33rd Assembly on 6 December 2023 - nine months after this examination, and whose document was not even issued until the following January. Those resolutions are future for this paper and appear nowhere in this answer. The Procedures are therefore cited by identity and by concept only: no paragraph number is asserted anywhere, and no wording is quoted from any edition.

That limitation costs this answer very little, and the reason is worth understanding rather than merely accepting. The consolidated Procedures are recommendatory - the resolution adopting them merely invites Governments to implement them - so they are not the source of the port State's power. The power is in the conventions, whose control articles are held, and which did not move between this sitting and any later one. Limb a is anchored there, and the Procedures supply vocabulary rather than authority. An answer that named a resolution as the source of the legal power would be both wrong in principle and fragile in date.

No numeric threshold is asserted anywhere in this answer - not for steering gear, not for emergency power, not for any equipment. Equipment requirements are stated in function rather than by value, because the main and the auxiliary steering-gear requirements are widely conflated in secondary material and a value carried from the wrong one is a confident mistake. Nor is any detention statistic, deficiency code or targeting-factor value given: these are regional administrative data, they change continuously, and none is needed.

Limb b rests on the ISM Code as it stood at this sitting, on a held official chain, and it is cited at the level of its functional requirements - reporting, investigating and analysing non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences; implementing corrective action; and verifying through internal audit and management review. No provision is quoted and no element number is asserted. The three defined terms are carried as established quality-management vocabulary and are not attributed to any standard by number, because MIW holds no quality-management standard - they are defined by what each acts upon, which is the distinction the printed limb asks for and which is stable across the systems in use.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) Where the power comes from, and what an inspection is before clear grounds exist
  2. __________a) 'Clear grounds' - the definition, and the two words that carry it
  3. __________a) The circumstances that create clear grounds - with examples
  4. __________a) What follows once clear grounds exist
  5. __________b) The three terms that must be separated
  6. __________b) Corrective action - what it actually involves
  7. __________b) Preventive action - where it comes from
  8. __________b) The two worked through one defect, and how the limbs connect
Flashcards 10

Limb a: 1 where the power comes from, and the limited initial inspection. 2 the definition, and the two words that carry it. 3 examples grouped as documents, equipment, condition and people. 4 what follows the trigger. Limb b: 5 the three terms defined by what each acts upon. 6 corrective action as a process. 7 preventive action and its inputs. 8 one defect answered three ways, joining the limbs.

Limb a is a threshold question and needs both sides of the boundary; limb b needs three terms to define the two the stem asks about.

Evidence that the ship, her equipment or her crew does not correspond substantially with the requirements of the conventions, or that master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to safety or the prevention of pollution. EVIDENCE excludes suspicion, reputation and the flag - something must be observed. SUBSTANTIALLY excludes the trivial and the cosmetic.

The two qualifying words are the definition; without them 'clear grounds' means whatever the officer feels.

Yes. Unfamiliarity of the master or crew with essential shipboard procedures relating to safety or pollution prevention is a free-standing head of clear grounds, independent of the condition of any equipment. Equipment is worth only what the people operating it know about it.

The element candidates most often omit, and a standard oral question.

Documents - certificates absent, expired, invalid, not matching the ship or apparently falsified; an Oil Record Book that will not reconcile with what is on board. Equipment - required equipment missing, or present but bypassed, blanked, corroded or plainly unmaintained. Condition - heavy corrosion, unsafe machinery spaces, unsanitary accommodation. People - inability to start the emergency generator or operate the fixed fire-fighting system; rest hours inconsistent with the watch pattern.

The stem asks for examples in terms, and grouping them shows organisation rather than recall.

No. The power is in the convention control articles - SOLAS I/19, MARPOL 5 and 6, Load Lines 21, STCW X and I/4, MLC Title 5.2. IMO's consolidated Procedures are recommendatory; the adopting resolution only invites Governments to implement them. And the detailed inspection is directed at the areas in which the clear grounds arose - it is not a general re-survey of the ship.

Both halves are commonly got wrong, and the first is what makes the answer independent of which edition is current.

Correction acts on the non-conformity itself - fix the thing that is wrong. Corrective action acts on the cause of a DETECTED non-conformity, so it does not recur. Preventive action acts on the cause of a POTENTIAL non-conformity, so it never occurs. The difference the question asks about reduces to one word: DETECTED against POTENTIAL.

Introducing the third term is what makes the difference between the other two precise rather than approximate.

No - that was a correction. It dealt with the non-conformity itself and left the cause exactly where it was, so the same failure will return. Corrective action requires the cause to be found and removed, and it is not complete until effectiveness has been verified - not 'was the action taken' but 'did it stop recurring'.

The central confusion of limb b, and the reason so many safety management systems are full of closed but unresolved findings.

From near misses and hazardous occurrences, risk assessments, audit observations that fall short of non-conformities, trend analysis - alarm counts, defect backlogs, deteriorating oil analysis - and fleet and industry experience: a sister ship's casualty, a circular, a service letter, another ship's PSC record. It is harder because it competes against visibly urgent work, and because when it succeeds nothing happens, which is impossible to point at.

Naming real inputs proves the concept is understood operationally rather than as a definition.

Correction: plug or renew the tube, renew the oil charge, return the plant to service. Corrective action: establish why it wasted - erosion, exhausted cathodic protection, water treatment not followed, an interval quietly extended - change that, and verify at the next opening that the wastage has stopped. Preventive action: inspect or re-interval every other cooler of the same material and duty on this ship and her sisters before one of them leaks.

One event answered three ways demonstrates the distinction instead of asserting it, which is what the stem's 'difference' is asking for.

Both, depending on which ship you are talking about. For the ship inspected it is a detected non-conformity and therefore an input to corrective action. For every other ship in the fleet it is a potential one, and therefore an input to preventive action. A company that responds only to what inspectors find is running on corrective action alone, and will be detained at the rate its worst ship deserves.

It joins the two limbs, which is almost certainly why the examiner set them on the same question.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Limb a. The power to inspect is in the conventions - SOLAS I/19 with IX/6, XI-1/4, XI-2/9; MARPOL articles 5 and 6; Load Lines article 21; STCW article X and regulation I/4; MLC Title 5.2. IMO's Procedures are guidance and only invite implementation. The initial inspection is limited to certificates and documents and the general condition of the ship. CLEAR GROUNDS = EVIDENCE that the ship, her equipment or her crew does not correspond SUBSTANTIALLY with convention requirements, OR that master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures. Evidence excludes suspicion and reputation; substantially excludes the trivial; crew unfamiliarity stands on its own. Examples by group: documents (certificates absent, expired, mismatched, falsified; Oil Record Book not reconciling); equipment (missing, bypassed, blanked, unmaintained); condition (heavy corrosion, unsafe machinery spaces, unsanitary accommodation); people (cannot start the emergency generator or change over the steering gear). ISM-related findings point at the SYSTEM, so nothing else can be relied on - they widen the inspection. Then: detailed inspection scoped by the grounds, rectification, detention only if clearly hazardous or so numerous as to show systemic failure, notification to flag State and RO, repair port on conditions - and always avoid undue detention or delay. Limb b. CORRECTION acts on the non-conformity itself - fix the thing. CORRECTIVE ACTION acts on the cause of a DETECTED non-conformity - stop recurrence. PREVENTIVE ACTION acts on the cause of a POTENTIAL non-conformity - stop occurrence. The difference is DETECTED against POTENTIAL. Corrective process: contain and correct, establish what happened, find the cause and go past the first one, act on the cause, assign owner and date, implement, VERIFY EFFECTIVENESS, feed back. Preventive inputs: near misses, risk assessment, audit observations, trends, fleet and industry experience, other ships' PSC records.

clear groundsevidencesubstantiallyinitial inspectionmore detailed inspectioncrew familiarityessential shipboard proceduresISM related deficiencydetainable deficiencyclearly hazardousnotification to flag Stateundue detentionSOLAS I/19MARPOL articles 5 and 6Indian Ocean MOUDG Shippingcorrectioncorrective actionpreventive actiondetected non-conformitypotential non-conformityroot causeverification of effectivenessnear misshazardous occurrencerisk assessmenttrend analysismanagement reviewinternal auditISM Code
Critical numbers
  • Clear grounds has TWO qualifying words - EVIDENCE and SUBSTANTIALLY - plus a free-standing head of crew unfamiliarity
  • FOUR groups of examples: documents, equipment, condition, people
  • THREE terms in limb b - correction, corrective action, preventive action - distinguished by what each acts upon
  • The whole of limb b's difference is ONE word: DETECTED against POTENTIAL
  • NO numeric equipment threshold is asserted anywhere in this answer, and no paragraph of the Procedures is cited
Critical regulation
SOLAS regulation I/19, control: a ship in a port of another Contracting Government is subject to control by officers duly authorised to verify that the certificates are valid; and where there are clear grounds for believing that the condition of the ship or of her equipment does not correspond substantially with the particulars of those certificates, steps shall be taken to ensure that the ship shall not sail until she can proceed to sea without danger to the ship or persons on board - every effort being made to avoid a ship being unduly detained or delayed. The corresponding control provisions are MARPOL articles 5 and 6, Load Lines 1966 article 21, STCW article X and regulation I/4, and MLC 2006 Title 5.2. IMO's consolidated Procedures supply the vocabulary and the sequence; they are recommendatory and confer no power, and the edition in circulation at this sitting is not held by MIW, so no paragraph is cited.
Major trap
Treating clear grounds as anything that looks wrong. It is evidence of a substantial departure, and both words exclude something. Second trap: forgetting that crew unfamiliarity is a free-standing head - a materially perfect ship can be opened up on it alone. Third: saying the power comes from the IMO Procedures; it comes from the conventions, and the Procedures only invite Governments to implement them. Fourth: treating the detailed inspection as a general re-survey, when its scope is set by the grounds that triggered it. Fifth, and the whole of limb b's trap: confusing correction with corrective action - replacing the failed part is a correction and leaves the cause exactly where it was. Sixth: describing preventive action as more thorough corrective action, when the difference is detected against potential - a difference in kind, not degree. Seventh: omitting verification of effectiveness, which is the step an auditor goes to first.

Also on the platform: QP2303 Q8 - the Recognized Organization whose certificates the inspection tests, and how its flag State monitors it · QP2303 Q6 - the human element behind a non-conformity, and why blame is not corrective action

First in set First set here. The same task returns at June 2024 Q3.

Understand this first

Two different things wear the same face in this question, and the whole of it is about telling them apart. A classification society is a private business. You pay it, it writes its own rules about how a ship should be built and maintained, and it surveys your ship against those rules. Nothing about that is law. IACS is the club those societies formed among themselves, so that their private rules would not drift apart and so that they could speak with one voice at IMO - and it too makes no law. When IACS agrees a Unified Requirement, nothing happens to your ship until each Member writes it into its own rules; that is the mechanism by which a private agreement between companies eventually reaches steel. The second thing is quite different. Every flag State has statutory duties under the conventions - to survey ships and to issue certificates - and almost none of them has the surveyors to do it worldwide. So they delegate, usually to the same societies, which then act in an entirely separate capacity as Recognized Organizations. That is public work done on a government's behalf. IMO eventually wrote a Code setting out what an organization must be like before a flag State may use it, and what the flag State must do to keep an eye on it afterwards - and the reason the Code exists at all is the obvious conflict in the arrangement, since the organization is being paid by the owner whose ship it is certifying on the government's behalf. Hold on to one sentence and the rest arranges itself around it: the flag State can hand over the work, but it can never hand over the responsibility.

Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

Bullet answer — points to write
a)
  1. What IACS is and how it is organised
    • A non-governmental association of classification societies with consultative status at IMO
    • It makes no law and issues no certificate - its output reaches ships through Members' Rules or through Administrations relying on it
    • Council - governing body, one Representative per Member; strategy, budget, admission, adopts Resolutions
    • General Policy Group - executive deputy to Council, the Members' Permanent Representatives, runs the work programme
    • Panels and Expert Groups - the technical bodies that draft and revise the Resolutions
    • Permanent Secretariat in London; a quality oversight function over Members' quality systems
    • No membership figure and no panel count is stated - both have changed and neither is needed
  2. UR, UI and PR - what each one acts upon
    • All three are IACS Resolutions; they differ in what they act upon
    • UR acts on the society's own Rules - a minimum technical requirement each Member builds in
    • A UR reaches a ship through class rules, never directly
    • UI acts on an IMO convention - a common interpretation where a provision is vague or left to the Administration
    • A UI interprets; it does not create a class requirement
    • PR acts on the society itself - survey conduct, transfer of class, reporting, outstanding conditions
    • The test: ship, convention, society
  3. Does it signify to owners and operators - and why
    • Yes, substantially - and the question wants reasons, not a yes
    • A UR is a floor - a predictable standard whichever Member classes the ship
    • But a new UR applied to existing ships is an unbudgeted fleet cost
    • No rule-shopping between Members for a materially softer technical rule
    • A UI reduces delay - the cost of a disputed interpretation is the ship alongside at port State control
    • A PR has commercial bite - transfer of class does not shed outstanding conditions or overdue surveys
    • The limit: IACS Resolutions bind Members, not owners, and a non-IACS society is outside the system
b)
  1. What a Recognized Organization is
    • An organization to which a flag Administration has delegated statutory survey and certification work
    • Most commonly, but not necessarily, a classification society
    • The delegation is of function, not of responsibility - the Administration remains answerable
    • Class and RO are different capacities - private and contractual against public and statutory
    • A society is an RO only for the flag that delegated, and only to the extent delegated
    • The same surveyor may act in both capacities in one attendance
    • Recognition and authorization are two separate acts - assessment, then specified delegation
  2. The RO Code and its salient features
    • Adopted by MSC.349(92) of 21 June 2013 and by MEPC.237(65)
    • A harmonised, transparent and independent standard replacing scattered Assembly criteria
    • Mandatory from 1 January 2015 by MSC.350(92), MSC.356(92) and MEPC.238(65)
    • Amending SOLAS, MARPOL Annexes I and II and the 1988 Load Line Protocol; SOLAS XI-1/1 is the hook
    • Part 1 general, Part 2 mandatory requirements, Part 3 oversight guidelines
    • Parts 1 and 2 bind; Part 3 is recommendatory - the most examinable structural fact
    • On the organization: legal personality, competence, ethics, independence, resources, subcontracting control, complaints and appeals, vertical contract audit, and a quality system certified by an accredited independent body
    • On the flag State: rest it on its conventional basis, SPECIFY the functions, make a formal written agreement, and notify the Secretary-General
  3. How Administrations monitor Recognized Organizations
    • Before: assess against Part 2, confirm capability, and reduce the delegation to a written agreement with scope and limits
    • During, mechanism one - ACCESS to the quality system, internal instructions, fleet documentation and casualty statistics
    • Mechanism two - a system to ensure adequacy: reporting, additional flag State inspections, consultation between the authorized organizations
    • Mechanism three - auditing, including vertical contract audits and witnessed surveys, with reliance permitted on accredited independent audits
    • Mechanism four - a managed programme with objectives, resources, records and periodic review
    • Performance indicators: port State detentions, internal audits, third-party quality audits, previous monitoring, and the condition of the ships certified
    • The authorization is function-specific, so it can be narrowed as readily as withdrawn; failure to report is a compliance failure
    • The Administration is itself audited under the Member State Audit Scheme against the III Code, A.1070(28), and the obligations at A.1157(32)
    • The work may be delegated; the responsibility cannot

Memory cue. Limb a - what it is, three letters, why you care. IACS makes no law (1); ship, convention, society for UR, UI, PR (2); floor, no shopping, less delay, transfer bites, and Resolutions bind Members only (3). Limb b - is, Code, monitor. Delegation of function, not responsibility (4); three parts, two mandatory, made binding through the conventions (5); before, during and above, closing on the duty that cannot be delegated (6).

Remember6 route headings
Cover45 core points beneath them

Memorise the 6 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 45 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. What IACS is and how it is organised

The International Association of Classification Societies is a non-governmental association of classification societies holding consultative status at IMO. The first thing to say about it is what it is not: it is not a regulator, it makes no law, and it issues no certificate. Its output reaches a ship in one of only two ways - through a Member society's own Rules, or because an Administration chooses to rely on it. Getting that clear at the outset is worth a mark on its own, because most weak answers treat IACS as though it were a branch of IMO.

BodyWhat it does
CouncilThe governing body, with one Representative from each Member. Sets strategy, approves the budget, decides admission and withdrawal of Members, and adopts IACS Resolutions
General Policy GroupThe executive deputy to Council, made up of the Members' Permanent Representatives. Runs the work programme and approves the technical output for adoption
Panels and Expert GroupsThe technical bodies. They draft and revise the Resolutions, each covering a subject area such as hull, machinery, survey or statutory matters
Permanent SecretariatBased in London. Administers the association and carries its representation at IMO
Quality oversight functionOversees Members' quality management systems and the periodic independent verification on which continued membership depends

No membership figure and no panel count is given here, deliberately. Both have changed more than once, and neither is needed to answer the question. A candidate who quotes a number risks being wrong for the year in question and gains nothing if he is right.

2. UR, UI and PR - what each one acts upon

All three are IACS Resolutions. They are not three grades of the same thing - they differ in what they act upon, and that is the whole of the distinction:

Acts uponWhat it is, and how it reaches a ship
UR - Unified RequirementThe society's own RulesA minimum technical requirement which every Member must build into its Rules, normally within a period set when the Resolution is approved. How it reaches a ship: Never directly. Only through the classification rules of the society that classes the ship
UI - Unified InterpretationAn IMO conventionA common interpretation of a convention provision that is vague, or expressly left to the Administration's satisfaction. How it reaches a ship: through the surveyor applying the convention - and only where the Administration accepts the interpretation
PR - Procedural RequirementThe society itselfA requirement about how the society works - conduct of surveys, transfer of class, reporting, and the handling of outstanding conditions. How it reaches a ship: through the society's own procedures, and it binds the society rather than the ship

The three-word test for holding them apart under examination pressure: ship, convention, society. A UR is about the ship. A UI is about a convention. A PR is about the society.

3. Does it signify to owners and operators - and why

The stem asks a yes-or-no question, but the marks are entirely in the reasons. The answer is yes, substantially, and it signifies in four distinct ways:

  • A UR is a floor. It gives the owner a predictable minimum technical standard whichever IACS Member classes the ship, so a newbuilding specification does not have to be re-argued society by society. The corollary is commercial and unwelcome: a new UR applied to existing ships can be an unbudgeted fleet cost.
  • It removes rule-shopping. An owner cannot move between IACS Members in search of a materially softer technical rule, because the floor is common. That is a discipline on competitors as much as on the owner himself.
  • A UI reduces regulatory risk, which for an operator means reduced delay. The real cost of a disputed convention interpretation is not the argument - it is the ship alongside while the argument is had at a port State control inspection.
  • A PR has direct commercial bite. The Procedural Requirements on transfer of class mean that changing society does not shed outstanding conditions of class or overdue surveys; the gaining society takes them over. Transfer is not an escape route, and an owner who plans one on that assumption is planning badly.

And the limit must be stated, because it is the discriminating point. IACS Resolutions bind Members, not owners and not flag States. They have no statutory force of their own. A society outside IACS is not bound by any of them, and a ship classed with such a society is outside the system altogether - which is a matter the owner, the charterer and the underwriter each have their own reasons to care about.

4. What a Recognized Organization is

This opens limb b. A Recognized Organization is an organization to which a flag Administration has delegated statutory survey and certification work - surveys and certificates required by the conventions, issued on the Administration's behalf. It is most commonly, but not necessarily, a classification society.

Three distinctions carry the marks here, and all three are regularly missed:

  • The delegation is of function, not of responsibility. The Administration remains answerable for the ship's compliance and for the conduct of the organization it authorized. It has handed over the work, not the duty.
  • Classification society and RO are not synonyms. They are two capacities. Class is a private contractual arrangement between the society and the owner, against the society's own Rules. RO work is public, statutory and done for a flag State. The same surveyor may act in both capacities in a single attendance, and the two must not be confused when a dispute arises about what was surveyed and on whose authority.
  • A society is an RO only for the flag that delegated to it, and only to the extent of that delegation. The same society may hold quite different scopes from different Administrations - authorized for load line but not for ISM, or for one ship type and not another. There is no such thing as being an RO in general.

It follows that recognition and authorization are two separate acts. Recognition is the Administration's assessment of the organization against the mandatory requirements. Authorization is the later, specific delegation of named functions for named conventions. An organization may be recognized and still not authorized for a particular ship type or a particular convention, and an Administration is required not to authorize functions beyond what the organization can actually do.

5. The RO Code and its salient features

How it came about, and how a code becomes binding. Before 2013 the criteria for recognising organizations were scattered across several Assembly resolutions and were applied unevenly. The Code for Recognized Organizations replaced them with a single harmonised, transparent and independent international standard. It was adopted by resolution MSC.349(92) on 21 June 2013 and by resolution MEPC.237(65), and it was made mandatory from 1 January 2015 by MSC.350(92), MSC.356(92) and MEPC.238(65), which amended SOLAS, MARPOL Annexes I and II and the 1988 Load Line Protocol to refer to it - with SOLAS regulation XI-1/1 as the hook. Recognise the technique, because IMO uses it repeatedly: a code is written as a free-standing document and then made binding by amending the conventions that point at it.

Salient feature one - three parts, and only two of them bind. This is the most examinable structural fact in the whole subject:

PartContentStatus
Part 1General provisions - purpose, definitions, application, the power to delegate, and notification to the Secretary-GeneralMandatory
Part 2Mandatory requirements - what the organization must satisfy, and what the flag State must do when it authorizesMandatory - it says shall
Part 3Guidelines for flag State oversight of Recognized OrganizationsRecommendatory - it says should

The adopting resolution recommends that Governments apply Part 3 unless their own national arrangements provide at least equivalent safety - which is what makes Part 3 guidance with teeth rather than guidance that can simply be ignored.

Salient feature two - what the organization itself must satisfy. Demonstration is the organization's burden; confirmation is the flag State's:

  • General requirements - legal personality, demonstrated competence and experience, ethical conduct, and independence.
  • Management and organization - a documented structure, defined responsibilities, and control over its exclusive surveyors and its offices worldwide.
  • Resources - qualified surveyors and auditors, training and qualification against defined standards, and technical and research capability.
  • Processes - including the control of subcontracting, and defined handling of complaints and appeals.
  • Performance measurement - internal audit, vertical contract audit of a complete certification history, and control of statutory deficiencies.
  • A quality management system no less effective than the recognised international quality standard, certified by an accredited body that is independent of the organizations and of their associations.

Independence is the head the Code is really defending. An organization that surveys a ship, classes the same ship, and depends commercially on the owner it is surveying has a conflict that the entire structure above is designed to contain.

Salient feature three - what the flag State must do when it authorizes. It must rest the delegation on its conventional basis and use it only for its own ships; it must specify the functions - function by function and convention by convention, including the conditions for withdrawal and cancellation; it must reduce the delegation to a formal written agreement covering the legal basis, reporting, national interpretations and information exchange; and it must notify the Secretary-General of its list of Recognized Organizations and of the conditions of the authority delegated to each.

6. How Administrations monitor Recognized Organizations

The third sentence of the limb, and it divides cleanly into what happens before delegation, what happens during it, and what happens to the Administration itself.

Before delegation: assess the organization against Part 2, determine its technical, managerial and research capability, confirm it can actually perform the functions contemplated, and reduce the delegation to a written agreement stating scope, limits, national requirements and interpretations, the instructions to be followed where a ship is found unfit to proceed, and the records to be kept.

During delegation, the oversight programme runs on four mechanisms:

  1. Access. The Administration must be able to reach into the organization - its quality management system, its internal instructions and circulars, its fleet documentation, and its casualty statistics.
  2. A system to ensure adequacy. Reporting from the organization, additional flag State inspections of ships it has certified, and consultation between the several organizations authorized by the same flag.
  3. Auditing. The Administration audits, and is permitted to rely on audits by an accredited independent body - conducted with ethical conduct, fair presentation and due professional care. In practice this includes vertical contract audits and witnessed surveys, which test the actual attendance rather than the paperwork about it.
  4. Programme management. The whole of the above is run as a managed programme with stated objectives, allocated resources, records, and periodic review - not as a set of occasional interventions.

The performance indicators on which the review turns are: port State control detention performance of the ships the organization has certified, the results of its internal audits, the results of the third-party quality system audit, the findings of previous monitoring, and the condition of the ships certified. Casualties attributable to the delegation belong here too.

The sanctions are graduated, and the graduation is the point. Because the authorization is specified function by function, it can be narrowed as readily as it can be suspended or withdrawn - an Administration dissatisfied with an organization's handling of one convention need not, and usually will not, terminate the whole relationship. And a failure by the organization to report as agreed is a compliance failure, not poor service: it goes to the authorization itself.

Finally, the Administration is itself monitored. Under the IMO Member State Audit Scheme it is audited against the III Code - resolution A.1070(28), mandatory from 1 January 2016 - whose Part 2 expressly requires an oversight programme for delegated authority. The obligations against which it is measured are those in the non-exhaustive list at resolution A.1157(32), adopted by the 32nd Assembly and the instrument in force at this sitting.

Which closes the limb on the sentence that the whole of it exists to establish: a flag State may delegate the work, but it cannot delegate the responsibility.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Two limbs, sixteen marks, and they are about two entirely different things that candidates routinely merge. Limb a is about IACS - a private industry association that makes no law. Limb b is about the RO Code - a mandatory IMO instrument about flag States delegating statutory work. The overlap is only that the same societies appear in both. An answer that treats IACS as a regulator, or the RO Code as an IACS document, has lost the discrimination the question is built on.

Note also that limb b is three questions in one sentence: what an RO is, the salient features of the Code, and how Administrations monitor. Answer all three explicitly. Candidates who write at length on the first two and give monitoring a sentence lose a third of the limb.

The three-word test for UR, UI and PR

Ship, convention, society. A UR acts on the society's Rules and therefore on the ship. A UI acts on an IMO convention, giving a common reading of a vague or discretionary provision. A PR acts on the society itself - how it surveys, how it transfers class, how it reports. If those three words are written down first, the rest of limb a's first half writes itself.

The sentence that shows limb a is understood

IACS makes no law. Its Resolutions bind its Members, not owners and not flag States, and they reach a ship only through class Rules or because an Administration chooses to rely on them. State that, and then the significance limb has something to argue about; omit it, and everything else reads as though IACS were a regulator.

Significance - four reasons and a limit

  • A UR is a floor - a predictable minimum whichever Member classes the ship. But a new UR on existing ships is an unbudgeted cost.
  • No rule-shopping between Members for a softer technical standard.
  • A UI cuts delay, because the real cost of a disputed interpretation is the ship alongside at a port State control inspection.
  • A PR has commercial bite - transfer of class does not shed outstanding conditions or overdue surveys.
  • The limit: IACS Resolutions bind Members only. A non-IACS society is outside the system entirely.

Class against RO - the distinction the examiner is hunting

Class is private and contractual, against the society's own Rules. RO work is public and statutory, done on behalf of a flag State. The same society, and often the same surveyor on the same day, acts in both capacities. And a society is an RO only for the flag that delegated, and only to the extent of that delegation - there is no such thing as being an RO in general. Add that delegation is of function, not of responsibility, and the definitional part of limb b is complete.

The single most examinable fact about the RO Code

Three parts, two of which bind. Part 1 general and Part 2 mandatory requirements are mandatory; Part 3, the flag State oversight guidelines, is recommendatory - and the adopting resolution recommends applying it unless national arrangements give at least equivalent safety. Part 2 says shall; Part 3 says should. If you remember one thing about the Code, remember this.

How a code becomes binding - a pattern worth carrying

The RO Code was adopted by MSC.349(92) of 21 June 2013 and MEPC.237(65), then made mandatory from 1 January 2015 by MSC.350(92), MSC.356(92) and MEPC.238(65), which amended SOLAS, MARPOL Annexes I and II and the 1988 Load Line Protocol - SOLAS XI-1/1 being the hook. The same technique makes the ISM Code, the ISPS Code and the III Code binding. Recognising the pattern is worth more than memorising this one instance.

Monitoring - before, during, and above

BEFORE: assess against Part 2, confirm capability, and put the delegation into a written agreement with stated scope and limits. DURING: access to the system, a mechanism to ensure adequacy, auditing including vertical contract audits and witnessed surveys, and the whole run as a managed programme. ABOVE: the Administration is itself audited under the Member State Audit Scheme against the III Code, A.1070(28), measured against the obligations listed in A.1157(32). Then close on the sentence: the work can be delegated, the responsibility cannot.

Common mistakes

  • Treating IACS as a regulator or as a part of IMO. It is a private association with consultative status and no legal authority.
  • Saying the RO Code is an IACS document. It is an IMO instrument, adopted by MSC and MEPC resolutions.
  • Confusing classification with statutory work, or treating 'classification society' and 'Recognized Organization' as synonyms.
  • Saying Part 3 is mandatory. It is guidance - though guidance the adopting resolution presses hard.
  • Answering the significance limb with a bare yes. The marks are entirely in the reasons.
  • Giving a membership figure or a panel count. Both have changed, and neither is needed.
  • Answering the definition and the salient features and then giving monitoring one line, when it is a third of the limb.
  • Forgetting that the authorization is function-specific, and therefore that it can be narrowed rather than only withdrawn.
  • Saying the flag State transfers responsibility along with the work. It does not.

Examiner traps

  • "Does IACS issue certificates?" No. Its Members do, in their own right or as ROs for a flag State. IACS itself issues none.
  • "What is the difference between a UR and a UI?" A UR changes the society's Rules; a UI interprets an IMO convention. Different targets entirely.
  • "Your ship transfers class. Do the outstanding conditions go away?" No - the Procedural Requirements on transfer of class carry them across to the gaining society.
  • "Is your classification society your Recognized Organization?" Only for the flags that have delegated to it, and only for the functions delegated. Ask which hat the surveyor is wearing today.
  • "Which parts of the RO Code are mandatory?" Parts 1 and 2. Part 3 is recommendatory.
  • "Who is responsible if the RO gets it wrong?" The Administration. Function is delegated; responsibility is not.
  • "How would a flag State find out that an RO was performing badly?" Port State control detentions on the ships it certifies, audit results, vertical contract audits, witnessed surveys, and casualty statistics.
  • "Who audits the flag State?" IMO, under the Member State Audit Scheme, against the III Code.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Who classes your ship, and which flags has that society been authorized by?
  • Your surveyor attends for a class survey and a statutory survey on the same day. What changes between the two?
  • What is a condition of class, and what happens to it if the owner changes society?
  • Give me an example of something in your ship's construction that came from a Unified Requirement.
  • If you disagree with a surveyor's interpretation of a convention, who decides?
  • What could cause a flag State to withdraw part of an RO's authorization?

Memory framework

Limb a: what it is, the three letters, and why you care. IACS makes no law (1); ship, convention, society for UR, UI, PR (2); floor, no rule-shopping, less delay, transfer bites - and Resolutions bind Members only (3). Limb b: is, Code, monitor. An RO is a delegate of a flag State, function not responsibility (4); the Code is three parts, two mandatory, made binding through the conventions (5); monitoring is before, during and above, closing on the work delegated but not the duty (6).
