Uncertainty and currency

One date on this question moves, and it is the obligations list. The non-exhaustive list of IMO instrument obligations against which a flag State is audited was carried by resolution A.1157(32) at this sitting. The 33rd Assembly replaced it with A.1187(33) on 6 December 2023 - nine months after this examination - and a later answer on this same question naturally cites the newer resolution. It must not be read back into this sitting. The correction was made here from the dates and not inherited. MIW holds no copy of A.1157(32); it is cited by identity, adopting session and revocation date only, and no clause of it is quoted.

Everything else on this question is temporally stable. The RO Code was adopted in 2013 and became mandatory on 1 January 2015; the III Code became mandatory on 1 January 2016. Neither moved before this sitting or in the period examined afterwards, and the December 2023 Assembly change replaced the obligations list, not the Code. The Indian frame is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and DG Shipping; the 2025 Act is three years after this sitting and is not cited.

No provision of the RO Code is quoted anywhere, and that is a limitation rather than a stylistic choice. The copy available for this answer is an image-only scan of unconfirmed edition, so no sentence of the Code could be read at source. The Code's parts, the mandatory and recommendatory split and the salient features are carried at the level MIW's own verification records establish them, and no sentence of the Code appears in quotation marks.

MIW holds no IACS procedural document at all - only its own class and survey notes - so the whole of limb a is graded industry guidance rather than primary, and is presented as such. No membership count and no panel count is asserted, and no technical panel constituted after this sitting is named: the panel structure changed in January 2024, so a settled figure taken from later material would be wrong for this date. The period for incorporating a Unified Requirement into Members' Rules is given as the normal position rather than as an invariable rule.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) What IACS is and how it is organised
  2. __________a) UR, UI and PR - what each one acts upon
  3. __________a) Does it signify to owners and operators - and why
  4. __________b) What a Recognized Organization is
  5. __________b) The RO Code and its salient features
  6. __________b) How Administrations monitor Recognized Organizations
Flashcards 10

Limb a: 1 what IACS is and its organs. 2 UR, UI and PR by what each acts upon. 3 significance to owners, with the limit. Limb b: 4 what a Recognized Organization is. 5 the RO Code, how it became binding, and its three parts. 6 how Administrations monitor, closing on the duty that cannot be delegated.

The two limbs are about two different systems that share the same actors, and the structure is what keeps them apart.

A Unified Requirement acts on the society's own Rules - a minimum technical requirement each Member must build in, reaching a ship only through class rules. A Unified Interpretation acts on an IMO convention, giving a common reading where a provision is vague or left to the Administration. A Procedural Requirement acts on the society itself - survey conduct, transfer of class, reporting. The test: ship, convention, society.

They are not three grades of the same thing; they differ in what they act upon, and that is the whole distinction.

No to both. It is a non-governmental association of classification societies with consultative status at IMO. Its Resolutions bind its Members, not owners and not flag States, and they reach a ship only through a Member's Rules or because an Administration chooses to rely on them. A society outside IACS is not bound by any of them.

Treating IACS as a regulator is the commonest way to lose the whole of limb a.

An organization to which a flag Administration has delegated statutory survey and certification work - usually but not necessarily a classification society. Class is private and contractual, against the society's own Rules; RO work is public and statutory, done for a flag State. The delegation is of function, not of responsibility, and a society is an RO only for the flag that delegated and only to the extent of that delegation.

The two capacities are the substance of the definition, and the same surveyor may hold both in one attendance.

It was adopted by resolution MSC.349(92) of 21 June 2013 and by MEPC.237(65), then made mandatory from 1 January 2015 by MSC.350(92), MSC.356(92) and MEPC.238(65), which amended SOLAS, MARPOL Annexes I and II and the 1988 Load Line Protocol to refer to it. SOLAS regulation XI-1/1 is the hook. This is IMO's standard technique for making a code binding.

Adoption and entry into force are separate steps, and recognising the pattern is worth more than memorising this one instance.

Parts 1 and 2. Part 1 is general provisions and Part 2 is the mandatory requirements on the organization and on the flag State - Part 2 says shall. Part 3, the flag State oversight guidelines, is recommendatory and says should, though the adopting resolution recommends applying it unless national arrangements provide at least equivalent safety.

The single most examinable structural fact in the subject, and a very common error.

Before: assess against Part 2, confirm capability, and put the delegation into a written agreement with stated scope and limits. During: access to the quality system, instructions, fleet documentation and casualty statistics; a system to ensure adequacy through reporting and additional flag inspections; auditing, including vertical contract audits and witnessed surveys; and a managed programme with objectives and periodic review.

Monitoring is a third of limb b and is the part candidates most often reduce to a single line.

They go with the ship. The IACS Procedural Requirements on transfer of class require the gaining society to take over outstanding conditions of class and overdue surveys. Transfer is not a way of shedding them, which is the clearest commercial illustration of why a Procedural Requirement matters to an owner.

The significance limb asks why any of this matters commercially, and this is the sharpest single example.

IMO, under the Member State Audit Scheme, against the III Code - resolution A.1070(28), mandatory from 1 January 2016 - whose Part 2 expressly requires an oversight programme for delegated authority. The obligations against which it is measured are the non-exhaustive list at resolution A.1157(32), the instrument in force at this sitting.

It closes the limb on the point the whole of it exists to make: the work may be delegated, the responsibility may not.

Recognition is the Administration's assessment of an organization against the mandatory requirements of the Code. Authorization is the later, specific delegation of named functions for named conventions. An organization may be recognized and still not authorized for a particular ship type or convention - and because the authorization is specified function by function, it can be narrowed as readily as suspended or withdrawn.

The distinction explains why enforcement is graduated rather than all-or-nothing, which is how it actually works in practice.

Quick revision
15-second recall
IACS = a non-governmental association of classification societies, consultative status at IMO, makes no law and issues no certificate. Organs: Council (one Representative per Member, adopts Resolutions), General Policy Group (Permanent Representatives, runs the work programme), Panels and Expert Groups (draft the technical Resolutions), Permanent Secretariat, quality oversight of Members. UR acts on the society's RULES - a minimum technical requirement reaching the ship only through class rules. UI acts on an IMO CONVENTION - a common interpretation of a vague or discretionary provision. PR acts on the SOCIETY - survey conduct, transfer of class, reporting. Test: ship, convention, society. Significance: a UR is a floor and stops rule-shopping but can cost an existing fleet; a UI cuts delay at port State control; a PR means transfer of class does not shed outstanding conditions; limit - Resolutions bind Members, not owners. RO = an organization to which a flag Administration has delegated statutory survey and certification - function delegated, responsibility not; class and RO are different capacities; an RO only for the flag that delegated and only to the extent delegated. RO Code: adopted MSC.349(92) 21 June 2013 and MEPC.237(65); mandatory 1 January 2015 by MSC.350(92), MSC.356(92), MEPC.238(65) amending SOLAS, MARPOL I and II and the 1988 Load Line Protocol, hooked at SOLAS XI-1/1. Part 1 general and Part 2 mandatory bind; Part 3 oversight guidelines are recommendatory. Monitoring: assess and agree in writing before; then access, adequacy, auditing and a managed programme; indicators are detentions, audits and the condition of the ships certified. The Administration is itself audited under IMSAS against the III Code A.1070(28) and the obligations at A.1157(32).

Critical regulation

Major trap
Merging the two limbs - treating IACS as a regulator, or the RO Code as an IACS document. IACS is a private association that makes no law; the RO Code is an IMO instrument adopted by MSC and MEPC resolutions. Second trap: saying Part 3 is mandatory. Parts 1 and 2 bind; Part 3 is guidance. Third: answering the significance limb with a bare yes, when the marks are entirely in the reasons. Fourth: treating 'classification society' and 'Recognized Organization' as synonyms - they are two capacities, and a society is an RO only for the flag that delegated and only to the extent delegated. Fifth: saying the flag State transfers responsibility with the work. It does not. Longer-term signalNewly recurring topicActive in recent papersRising topicFramework since amendedCurrentness check pending
Understand this first

Every machine on the ship is sealed, and you cannot look inside it while it runs. The oil can - it touches every working surface continuously and carries away a sample of everything that happens in there. Oil analysis is nothing more than the business of reading that sample. The reason there are four techniques rather than one is that they are answering different questions and each is blind to the others' subjects. Two of them look at the machine: spectrometry tells you what the debris is made of, and particle count tells you how much of it there is and how big it is - and you need both, because spectrometry physically cannot see coarse particles, so a clean spectrometric report on a spalling bearing is a false reassurance rather than good news. The other two look at the oil: FTIR tells you what the oil has chemically become, and Base and Acid Number tell you how much fight it has left. Once you see it that way, the interpretation the question demands stops being a feat of memory. You are not reading numbers, you are reading pairs. Iron rising tells you very little on its own; iron rising with the base number gone tells you the oil stopped protecting the metal, while iron rising with the base number healthy tells you something entirely different and warns you that adding more oil will make it worse. And then the last idea, which is what the stem's opening sentence is really driving at: the analysis does not maintain anything. It tells you where to look. A programme that produces reports nobody reads is a cost with no benefit.

Knowledge map
Lubricating Oil Condition Analysis Techniques
  1. 1Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for
    • A running machine is a sealed box; the oil is the only thing that comes out of it
    • It has been in contact with every working surface inside
    • Is the machine wearing? - spectrometry and particle count
  2. A)
    1. 2A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis
      • Sample excited in a plasma or across a rotating-disc electrode arc
      • Each element emits at characteristic wavelengths; intensity gives concentration in ppm
      • Wear metals - iron from liners, rings, gears, shafting; copper, lead, tin from bearings; chromium from rings and plating; aluminium from pistons
    2. 3What spectrometry cannot see, and why that governs its use
      • The method needs complete atomisation of the particle in the excitation source
      • Coarse particles are not fully atomised, so response falls away with increasing size
      • It therefore reports essentially the fine fraction only
  3. B)
    1. 4B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy
      • Infrared through a thin film; molecular bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers
      • All wavelengths gathered at once as an interferogram and Fourier transformed to a spectrum
      • A DIFFERENTIAL measurement - compared against the spectrum of the same oil unused
  4. C)
    1. 5C) Particle count
      • Solid particles counted and sized, usually by optical light obscuration
      • Pore blockage where the oil is too dark; microscopy is the referee method
      • Reported as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands, condensed to a cleanliness code
  5. D)
    1. 6D) Base Number against Acid Number
      • Both titrations, both in mg KOH per gramme - which is why they are confused
      • BN = alkaline reserve from overbased detergent additives; it FALLS in service
      • AN = acidic constituents from combustion and oxidation; it RISES
  6. 7Correct interpretation - reading the four results together
    • No single result diagnoses anything; pairs and trends do
    • Iron up + residual BN near exhaustion = corrosive wear (in a cylinder, cold corrosion)
    • Iron up + BN healthy = NOT corrosion; more oil will make it worse
  7. 8Corrective measures - what is actually done
    • The sequence: verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil
    • Re-sample before acting - a large share of alarming results are sampling artefacts
    • Confirm physically - magnetic plugs, filters cut open, centrifuge sludge, ferrography for debris shape, direct inspection
  8. 9Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything
    • Same point, same way, same interval; machine at temperature and oil circulating
    • From a live line upstream of the filter, never the drain or the tank bottom
    • Flush the point; use clean dedicated bottles - a dirty bottle condemns a healthy machine
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

Bullet answer — points to write
  1. Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for
    • A running machine is a sealed box; the oil is the only thing that comes out of it
    • It has been in contact with every working surface inside
    • Is the machine wearing? - spectrometry and particle count
    • Is the oil still fit? - FTIR, and BN with AN
    • Is anything getting in? - all four, from different directions
    • The governing rule: trend against that machine's own history, not the absolute value
A)
  1. A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis
    • Sample excited in a plasma or across a rotating-disc electrode arc
    • Each element emits at characteristic wavelengths; intensity gives concentration in ppm
    • Wear metals - iron from liners, rings, gears, shafting; copper, lead, tin from bearings; chromium from rings and plating; aluminium from pistons
    • Contaminants - silicon from dirt and abrasive; sodium and boron from coolant; vanadium and nickel from residual fuel
    • Additive elements - calcium and magnesium (detergent), zinc and phosphorus (anti-wear)
    • Additives falling = depletion; rising = the wrong oil was added
    • Silicon reads two ways - dirt ingress in service, or sealant and antifoam residue right after an overhaul
  2. What spectrometry cannot see, and why that governs its use
    • The method needs complete atomisation of the particle in the excitation source
    • Coarse particles are not fully atomised, so response falls away with increasing size
    • It therefore reports essentially the fine fraction only
    • Normal rubbing wear is fine and is seen well
    • Bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting are coarse and may not be seen
    • So a clean spectrometric report is NOT evidence of a healthy machine
    • Which is exactly why particle count, ferrography and plug inspection sit alongside it
B)
  1. B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy
    • Infrared through a thin film; molecular bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers
    • All wavelengths gathered at once as an interferogram and Fourier transformed to a spectrum
    • A DIFFERENTIAL measurement - compared against the spectrum of the same oil unused
    • Practical consequence: retain a sealed sample of every oil delivery
    • Detects oxidation, nitration, sulphation, water and glycol
    • Soot appears as a broad baseline rise, not a discrete peak
    • Also fuel dilution and additive depletion
    • Fast, small sample, no reagents - but semi-quantitative and blind to wear metals and particles
C)
  1. C) Particle count
    • Solid particles counted and sized, usually by optical light obscuration
    • Pore blockage where the oil is too dark; microscopy is the referee method
    • Reported as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands, condensed to a cleanliness code
    • Decisive for hydraulic, servo and control systems where clearances are microns
    • The direct measure of filtration and centrifuge performance and of ingress
    • It sees the coarse particles spectrometry misses - the two are complementary
    • Limits: counts water droplets and air bubbles as particles; never says what they are
D)
  1. D) Base Number against Acid Number
    • Both titrations, both in mg KOH per gramme - which is why they are confused
    • BN = alkaline reserve from overbased detergent additives; it FALLS in service
    • AN = acidic constituents from combustion and oxidation; it RISES
    • An oil can carry both at once - separate titrations of separate species
    • Two standard BN methods give systematically different values - never trend them together
    • BN governs cylinder and trunk-piston oils, matched to the sulphur actually burned
    • AN governs turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils, which carry no reserve
    • Since the 0.50 per cent limit of 1 January 2020, BN is matched to the fuel in use - and over-alkalinity deposits abrasive calcium ash
  1. Correct interpretation - reading the four results together
    • No single result diagnoses anything; pairs and trends do
    • Iron up + residual BN near exhaustion = corrosive wear (in a cylinder, cold corrosion)
    • Iron up + BN healthy = NOT corrosion; more oil will make it worse
    • Particle count up + wear metals normal = filtration or centrifuge failure, or ingress
    • Particle count up + spectrometry clean = coarse debris - the most dangerous case
    • Silicon up + iron and chromium up = abrasive dirt ingress
    • Sodium and boron up + glycol and water on FTIR = coolant leak
    • Vanadium and nickel + fuel on FTIR + viscosity down = fuel dilution
    • Oxidation up + AN up + no wear metals = the oil is degrading, the machine is fine
    • Additive elements up = the wrong oil was added
    • Two disciplines: trend, do not spot-read; and rate of change beats level
  2. Corrective measures - what is actually done
    • The sequence: verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil
    • Re-sample before acting - a large share of alarming results are sampling artefacts
    • Confirm physically - magnetic plugs, filters cut open, centrifuge sludge, ferrography for debris shape, direct inspection
    • Abrasive ingress - find the entry path (filters, seals, breathers), not just more filtration
    • Coolant leak - pressure-test coolers, jackets, covers; glycol destroys bearing metal
    • Fuel dilution - injection equipment, timing, combustion; then renew, because diluted oil cannot be recovered
    • Water - find the source and run the purifier correctly (gravity disc, throughput, temperature)
    • Rising particle count - filters, differential pressure, bypass, purifier operation; then the ingress path
    • Low residual BN in cylinder oil - raise feed rate or move to a higher-BN oil matched to fuel sulphur, and confirm by scrape-down
    • High AN and oxidation - find the cause, then renew, cleaning the system before recharging
    • Additive depletion - renew; additives cannot be topped up
    • Record and close the loop in the PMS - unrecorded action destroys the trend
    • Standing caution: every limit comes from the builder and the oil supplier for THAT installation
  3. Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything
    • Same point, same way, same interval; machine at temperature and oil circulating
    • From a live line upstream of the filter, never the drain or the tank bottom
    • Flush the point; use clean dedicated bottles - a dirty bottle condemns a healthy machine
    • Retain a sample of every new oil - FTIR needs its reference
    • Label fully: ship, machine, point, hours since change and since overhaul, grade, top-ups
    • Trend, and read the rate of change rather than the level
    • Plan it in the PMS under the ISM maintenance regime at chapter 10
    • Close: analysis does not maintain machinery - it tells you where to look

Memory cue. Three questions, four techniques, two pairs, one sequence. QUESTIONS: is it wearing, is the oil fit, is anything getting in (1). TECHNIQUES: spectrometry says WHAT (2, and its limitation is step 3), particle count says HOW MUCH and HOW BIG (5), FTIR says what the OIL has become (4), BN and AN say how much FIGHT is left (6). PAIRS: iron with residual BN, and particle count with spectrometry (7). SEQUENCE: verify, confirm physically, correct the cause, then correct the oil (8), on samples taken properly (9).

Remember9 route headings
Cover75 core points beneath them

Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 75 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for

A running machine is a sealed box. The one thing that comes out of it continuously, and that has been in contact with every working surface inside it, is the oil. Whatever wears, whatever leaks in and whatever the oil itself becomes is carried in that sample. This is why used-oil analysis is the foundation of condition-based maintenance and not an accessory to it.

The four techniques named in this question are not four ways of doing the same job. Between them they answer three different questions, and setting that out before describing any instrument is what turns the rest of the answer into an argument rather than a list:

The question being askedWhich techniques answer it
Is the machine wearing?Elemental spectrometry and particle count
Is the oil still fit to remain in service?FTIR, and Base Number with Acid Number
Is anything getting in that should not be there?All four, each from a different direction

One rule governs the use of all of them, and it is worth stating at the outset because every later section depends on it: the trend against that machine's own history is worth more than any single value. An absolute figure means very little until you know where that particular machine normally sits.

2. A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis

Principle. A measured quantity of oil is excited - in a plasma, or across the arc of a rotating-disc electrode. The atoms of each element present emit light at wavelengths characteristic of that element, and the intensity at each of those wavelengths is proportional to how much of the element is there. The instrument reads many elements at once and reports each in parts per million.

What the results mean. The elements sort into three families, and reading them as families rather than as a column of numbers is the difference between a report and a diagnosis:

FamilyTypical elementsWhat their presence tells you
Wear metalsIron; copper, lead, tin; chromium; aluminium; silver; nickelIron from liners, rings, gears and shafting. Copper, lead and tin from bearings and bushes. Chromium from rings and plated surfaces. Aluminium from pistons and some bearing materials. Each element points at a different part of the machine, which is what makes the technique diagnostic rather than merely alarming
ContaminantsSilicon; sodium and boron; vanadium and nickelSilicon from airborne dirt through a defective filter, seal or breather - and silicon is abrasive. Sodium and boron from coolant leakage. Vanadium and nickel are carried in residual fuel, so their appearance in a crankcase oil indicates fuel finding its way in
Additive elementsCalcium and magnesium; zinc and phosphorus; bariumCalcium and magnesium from detergent and dispersant additives; zinc and phosphorus from anti-wear additives. A falling trend is additive depletion and marks the end of the oil's useful life. A rising trend almost always means the wrong oil has been put in

Silicon needs a sentence of its own, because it reads two ways. A rising silicon trend in normal service is dirt ingress, and it is serious, because silica is harder than the metal it is circulating between. A single high silicon result immediately after an overhaul is usually silicone sealant or antifoam residue and is not. Knowing which of the two you are looking at requires the maintenance history, not the report.

3. What spectrometry cannot see, and why that governs its use

This is the most important qualification in the whole question and it is the one most often left out. The method depends on the particle being completely atomised in the excitation source. Coarse particles are not, so the response falls away progressively as particle size increases. In practice the technique reports essentially the fine fraction only.

The consequence has to be stated plainly, because it changes how the result may be used. Normal rubbing wear generates fine debris and is seen very well. Bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting generate coarse debris and may not register at all. So a satisfactory spectrometric report is not evidence that the machine is in good order; it is evidence that the fine fraction is normal, which is a very much weaker statement and is sometimes a dangerously reassuring one. This limitation is the entire reason that particle count, ferrography and magnetic plug inspection belong alongside spectrometry rather than as alternatives to it.

4. B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy

Principle. Infrared energy is passed through a thin film of the oil. Molecular bonds absorb infrared at wavenumbers characteristic of the bond, so the pattern of absorption is a fingerprint of what molecules are present. Instead of scanning one wavelength at a time, the instrument collects all wavelengths simultaneously as an interferogram and applies a Fourier transform to convert it into a spectrum - which is what the name describes and why the technique is fast.

It is a differential measurement, and this is the point candidates lose. The spectrum of the used oil is compared against the spectrum of the same oil unused, and what is reported is the difference. Without a new-oil reference the result is very much weaker. That is a technical fact with a direct practical instruction attached: retain a sealed sample of every oil delivery taken aboard.

What it detects, each in its own characteristic absorption region:

  • Oxidation - the principal measure of the oil's thermal and chemical ageing.
  • Nitration - from combustion products, and prominent in gas and dual-fuel engines.
  • Sulphation - from sulphur oxides, and therefore directly related to the sulphur of the fuel being burned.
  • Water, and glycol where coolant is finding its way in.
  • Soot - which appears as a broad rise in the baseline rather than as a discrete peak, because soot scatters rather than absorbing selectively.
  • Fuel dilution and additive depletion.

Strengths and limits. It is quick, needs a very small sample, uses no reagents and returns several parameters at once, which makes it the natural routine screen. But it is semi-quantitative - very good on trend, weaker on absolute value - and it is completely blind to wear metals and to particles. It describes the oil. It says nothing about the machine.

5. C) Particle count

Principle. The solid particles suspended in the oil are counted and sized. The usual method is automatic optical light obscuration: the oil passes through a cell across a light beam, and each particle casts a shadow whose magnitude gives its size. Where the oil is too dark or too heavily sooted for light to pass, pore-blockage methods are used instead, and microscopic counting remains the referee method.

Reporting. Results are given as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands - the number of particles larger than each of several sizes - and those bands are condensed into a cleanliness code for trending and for specification. No code or band table is reproduced here, because MIW does not hold the standard and because in service the target code is set by the equipment manufacturer for that system.

Why it matters. It is decisive for hydraulic, servo and control systems, where clearances are measured in microns and a particle population that would be harmless in a crankcase will destroy a servo valve. It is the direct measure of filtration and centrifuge performance and of ingress through seals and breathers - a rising count with unchanged wear metals usually means the cleaning has failed, not that the machine has. And crucially it sees the coarse particles that spectrometry misses, which is what makes the two complementary.

Limits. Optical counters count water droplets and air bubbles as particles, so a wet or aerated sample reads falsely high and must be settled, with water measured separately where necessary. And the technique reports how many and how big, never what: it cannot tell steel from sand from soot.

6. D) Base Number against Acid Number

These two are confused more often than any other pair in oil analysis, and there is a good reason for it: both are titrations, and both are reported in the same units - milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil. They are nonetheless measurements of opposite things.

Base Number (BN)Acid Number (AN)
What is measuredThe alkaline reserve conferred by overbased detergent additivesThe quantity of acidic constituents present in the oil
Where it comes fromDeliberately formulated into the oil by the blenderCombustion acids, and oxidation of the oil itself
Direction in serviceFALLS as acid is neutralised and additive is consumedRISES as acidic species accumulate
Unitsmg KOH per grammemg KOH per gramme - the same units, which is the source of the confusion
What an adverse value meansThe alkaline reserve is exhausted and the oil can no longer protect the metal against acid attackAcid is present and attacking, or the oil is heavily oxidised

Three points separate a good answer from an adequate one:

  • An oil can carry a significant Base Number and a significant Acid Number at the same time. They are separate titrations of separate species, and weak organic acids can coexist with an alkaline reserve. A candidate who treats them as two ends of one scale misreads every report of this kind.
  • Two standard titration methods for Base Number are in general use - one using a strongly acidic titrant, one a weaker one - and they return systematically different values on the same oil, the stronger method giving the higher figure. Results from the two must never be trended together: a change of laboratory or method then looks exactly like a change in the oil.
  • Which of the two numbers governs depends on the oil. Cylinder oils and trunk-piston engine oils carry a deliberate alkaline reserve to neutralise combustion acid, so BN is the controlling parameter. Turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils carry no alkaline reserve at all, so BN is meaningless for them and AN is the governing parameter - a rising AN being the direct measure of oxidative degradation.

On cylinder oils in particular, the relationship with the fuel changed when the 0.50 per cent m/m limit on the sulphur content of fuel oil used outside emission control areas took effect on 1 January 2020. Less sulphur means less acid formed and, in principle, a lower base number required. But over-alkalinity is not a safe error in the other direction: detergent that finds no acid to neutralise deposits as hard calcium ash, which is abrasive and polishes the liner. Base number is therefore matched to the fuel actually being burned and reviewed at every changeover.

7. Correct interpretation - reading the four results together

This is what the stem's opening sentence is demanding, and it is the point at which the four techniques stop being a list. The governing rule is that no single result diagnoses anything. Pairs and trends do.

What the results show togetherInterpretationConfidence
Iron rising, residual Base Number falling toward exhaustionCorrosive wear - alkaline reserve is being consumed faster than it is being supplied. In a cylinder this is cold corrosionHigh - the clearest signature in the subject
Iron rising, Base Number still healthyNot corrosion. Look for abrasive ingress, scuffing or a mechanical cause - and note that raising the feed rate will make matters worse, not betterHigh
Wear metals normal, particle count climbingFiltration or centrifuge failure, or ingress. The machine is not necessarily wearing faster; the oil is simply not being cleanedHigh
Particle count climbing while spectrometry stays cleanSuspect coarse debris that spectrometry cannot see - bearing fatigue or spalling. Confirm physically at onceMedium - and the most dangerous case on the list, because the spectrometric report looks reassuring
Silicon rising together with iron and chromiumAbrasive dirt ingress through a defective air filter, seal or breather, producing three-body abrasive wearHigh
Silicon high alone, shortly after an overhaulSilicone sealant or antifoam residue rather than dirt. Re-sample before acting on itHigh
Sodium and boron rising with glycol and water on FTIRCoolant leak. Find it before the bearings do - glycol is far more damaging to bearing metal than water aloneHigh
Vanadium and nickel present, fuel dilution on FTIR, viscosity fallingFuel dilution from leaking injection equipment or poor combustionHigh
Oxidation and Acid Number both rising, wear metals normalThe oil is degrading and the machine is not - overheating, air entrainment, or an over-extended drain intervalHigh
Copper, lead or tin rising aloneBearing distress. Copper alone can also come from cooler-tube corrosion, so identify the source before condemning a bearingMedium
Additive elements falling steadilyAdditive depletion - the oil is at the end of its useful lifeHigh
Additive elements risingThe wrong oil has been added. Check the topping-up records before anything elseHigh

Two disciplines govern the whole of it. First, trend rather than spot-read: a value has meaning only against that machine's own history, taken the same way from the same point. Second, rate of change matters more than level - a moderate iron figure that has doubled in a month is far more serious than a higher figure that has been flat for a year.

8. Corrective measures - what is actually done

The stem asks for corrective measures, so the answer has to say what the Chief Engineer does. The general sequence on any adverse result is: verify the result, confirm it physically, correct the cause, and only then correct the oil.

  1. Verify before acting. Re-sample properly - same point, same method, machine at operating temperature with the oil circulating - and re-test. A large share of alarming results are sampling artefacts, and taking a machine out of service on a bad sample is an expensive way to learn that.
  2. Confirm physically. Where wear is indicated, go and look: magnetic plugs and chip detectors, filter elements cut open and examined, centrifuge sludge, ferrography for the shape of the debris, and direct inspection where it is accessible - scavenge ports, crankcase, gear teeth, bearing clearances. Ferrography is the technique that reveals the morphology of the debris and therefore the wear mechanism, which no elemental figure can.
  3. Deal with abrasive ingress at the entry path - air filters, seals, breathers, hatch and cover joints - and not by adding filtration alone. Filtration removes the consequence and leaves the cause running.
  4. Deal with a coolant leak immediately. Locate it by pressure-testing coolers, jackets and covers. Glycol attacks bearing metal and forms sludge, and a coolant leak left in service will ruin bearings long before the wear metals reach any alarm value.
  5. Deal with fuel dilution at the injection equipment, timing and combustion, and by checking for leakage past pumps and pipework. Then renew the oil, because diluted oil cannot be recovered.
  6. Deal with water by finding the source - cooler leaks, condensation, steam coils, stern tube ingress - and by running the purifier correctly: correct gravity disc, correct throughput, correct temperature. Water is the one contaminant the ship can usually remove for itself.
  7. Deal with a rising particle count by checking and renewing filters, verifying filter differential pressure and bypass operation, and reviewing purifier throughput, temperature and discharge interval. Then look for the ingress path.
  8. Deal with a low residual Base Number in a cylinder oil by raising the feed rate or moving to a higher-BN oil, matched to the sulphur of the fuel actually burned - and confirm the change worked by scrape-down analysis rather than assuming it. Do the reverse where residual BN is running high, because over-alkalinity deposits abrasive calcium ash.
  9. Deal with high Acid Number and high oxidation in a system oil by investigating the cause first - overheating, air entrainment, contamination, an over-extended drain interval - then by partial or complete renewal, cleaning the system before recharging so that the new charge is not contaminated on the first day.
  10. Deal with additive depletion by renewal. Additives cannot be topped up in service, and attempting it usually produces an incompatibility rather than a recovery.
  11. Record the action and close the loop. Every action taken, and the analysis result that follows it, goes into that machine's history under the planned maintenance system. Unrecorded corrective action is indistinguishable from no corrective action, and it destroys the trend on which the next diagnosis depends.

And the standing caution, which belongs in the answer explicitly: every condemning limit and target value comes from the engine builder and the oil supplier for that specific installation. None is quoted here and none should be carried from one ship to another - the same figure on a different engine, oil or duty can be entirely wrong.

9. Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything

  • Sample from the same point, in the same way, at the same interval, with the machine at operating temperature and the oil circulating. Oil drawn from a settled sump is not the fluid that is in service.
  • Take the sample from a live line or a dedicated sampling point, upstream of the filter, and never from the drain or the bottom of a tank.
  • Flush the sampling point first and use clean, dedicated bottles. A contaminated bottle will produce a particle count that condemns a healthy machine.
  • Keep a retained sample of every new oil delivered - FTIR is a differential measurement and is much weaker without its reference.
  • Label completely: ship, machine, sampling point, running hours since the last change and since overhaul, oil grade, and any top-up made.
  • Trend the results, and read the rate of change rather than the absolute value.
  • Plan the sampling in the planned maintenance system under the ISM maintenance regime at chapter 10, so that it happens whether or not anyone remembers it.

The closing point is the one the stem's preamble is really driving at: analysis does not maintain machinery. It tells you where to look. Its whole value is realised in the interpretation and the corrective action that follow, and a programme that generates reports nobody reads and acts on is a cost with no benefit attached.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks, four named sub-items - and a preamble that quietly adds two more tasks. The stem's first sentence says that periodical oil analysis, its correct interpretation and corrective measures, are of critical significance, and the command verb is "discuss", not "briefly discuss". That is an instruction, not scene-setting. So the shape is roughly half the answer across the four techniques and half on reading and acting on the results. A candidate who describes the four instruments beautifully and stops has answered a different and much easier question.

Open with the three questions

Is the machine wearing? Is the oil still fit? Is anything getting in? Spectrometry and particle count answer the first; FTIR with BN and AN answer the second; all four answer the third from different directions. Two sentences, and the four techniques become a system instead of a list - which is exactly what "discuss" is asking for.

The qualification most candidates omit

Spectrometry reports the fine fraction only. The method needs the particle to be completely atomised in the excitation source, and coarse particles are not - so the response falls away as size increases. Normal rubbing wear is fine and is seen well; bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting are coarse and may be invisible. A clean spectrometric report is therefore not evidence of a healthy machine. Write that sentence down: it is the single most valuable thing in the question, and it is the reason particle count exists alongside spectrometry rather than instead of it.

FTIR - two words to get right

Interferogram and differential. The instrument gathers all wavelengths at once as an interferogram and Fourier transforms it into a spectrum - that is what the name means and why it is fast. And it works by subtracting the spectrum of the same oil unused, so without a new-oil reference the result is much weaker. That carries a practical instruction with it: retain a sealed sample of every oil delivery.

The four techniques in one table

Tells you aboutSeesBlind to
SpectrometryThe machineWhat the debris is, elementallyCoarse particles; the condition of the oil
FTIRThe oilChemical change - oxidation, nitration, sulphation, water, soot, fuel, glycolWear metals; particles
Particle countThe machine and the filtrationHow many and how bigWhat the particles are; counts water and air as particles
BN / ANThe oilAlkaline reserve and acid loadEverything mechanical

The pairing to carry into the hall: spectrometry says WHAT, particle count says HOW MUCH and HOW BIG. Neither is sufficient alone, and that is the practical point of the entire question.

BN and AN - the three things that score

  1. Same units, opposite directions. Both in mg KOH per gramme - which is why they are confused. BN falls, AN rises.
  2. An oil can show both at once. Separate titrations of separate species; not two ends of one scale.
  3. Which one governs depends on the oil. Cylinder and trunk-piston oils carry an alkaline reserve, so BN governs. Turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils have no reserve, so AN governs and measures oxidative degradation.

And the trap inside the trap: two standard BN titration methods are in use and they give systematically different values on the same oil, the stronger titrant returning the higher figure. Never trend them together - a change of laboratory can look exactly like a change in the oil.

Interpretation - learn the pairs, not the values

An examiner asking for "correct interpretation" wants diagnostic pairs:

  • Iron up + residual BN near exhaustion = corrosive wear. In a cylinder, cold corrosion.
  • Iron up + BN healthy = not corrosion. More oil will make it worse.
  • Particle count up + wear metals normal = filtration or centrifuge failure, or ingress.
  • Particle count up + spectrometry clean = coarse debris spectrometry cannot see. The most dangerous case, because the report looks reassuring.
  • Silicon up + iron and chromium up = abrasive dirt ingress.
  • Sodium and boron up + glycol and water on FTIR = coolant leak.
  • Vanadium and nickel + fuel dilution on FTIR + viscosity down = fuel dilution.
  • Oxidation up + AN up + no wear metals = the oil is degrading and the machine is fine.
  • Additive elements up = somebody has added the wrong oil.

Corrective measures - the sequence

Verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil. Re-sample before acting, because a large share of alarming results are sampling artefacts. Then go and look: magnetic plugs, filters cut open, centrifuge sludge, ferrography - ferrography being the one that gives you the shape of the debris and therefore the wear mechanism. Then attack the cause - the leaking seal, the failing cooler, the injection equipment - and only after that renew or adjust the oil. Adding filtration to deal with dirt ingress treats the consequence and leaves the cause running.

Quote no numbers - and say why

No condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, cleanliness code, base number or wear rate should be offered as a general rule. Every one is engine-builder, oil, application and service specific, and a figure carried from one installation to another is a genuine hazard as well as an examination risk. Writing "the limit comes from the engine builder's service letter and the oil supplier's recommendation for this installation" scores better than a half-remembered figure that may belong to a different machine - and it is the answer a Chief Engineer is supposed to give.

Common mistakes

  • Describing the four techniques and never answering the stem's express demand for interpretation and corrective measures - roughly half the marks.
  • Omitting the particle-size limitation of spectrometry, and so implying that a clean report means a healthy machine.
  • Treating spectrometry and particle count as alternatives rather than as complements.
  • Saying FTIR measures wear metals. It is blind to them entirely.
  • Forgetting that FTIR is a differential measurement requiring a new-oil reference.
  • Assuming BN and AN cannot both be significant in the same sample.
  • Applying BN to a turbine or hydraulic oil, which carries no alkaline reserve.
  • Quoting condemning limits, ppm values or cleanliness codes as though they were general rules.
  • Ignoring sampling technique, without which every number in the report is unreliable.

Examiner traps

  • "Your spectrometric report is clean. Is the engine healthy?" No - it says the fine fraction is normal. Coarse debris from fatigue or spalling may not appear at all.
  • "What does FTIR see that spectrometry cannot, and the reverse?" FTIR sees the chemical condition of the oil; spectrometry sees the elements. Neither sees the other's subject.
  • "Why is water a problem for a particle counter?" Optical counters count droplets and air bubbles as particles, so a wet or aerated sample reads falsely high.
  • "Can an oil show a healthy Base Number and a significant Acid Number together?" Yes - separate titrations of separate species.
  • "Would you trend Base Number results from two different laboratories?" No - two standard methods exist and give systematically different values.
  • "Iron is up. Do you increase the cylinder oil feed rate?" Only if the residual BN is low. If BN is healthy the cause is not corrosion and more oil will make it worse.
  • "What tells you the shape of the wear debris?" Ferrography - and the shape identifies the wear mechanism.
  • "Where do your condemning limits come from?" The engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier for this installation. Not from a textbook, and not from the last ship.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Show me where you take your main engine system oil sample, and talk me through doing it.
  • Your last report shows sodium and boron rising. What is your first action?
  • What is a scrape-down analysis, and how does it differ from a system oil analysis?
  • How does your purifier remove water, and how would you know that it was working?
  • Which of your oils has no base number at all, and what do you monitor on it instead?
  • You get an alarming result on a Sunday at sea. What do you do before you do anything else?

Memory framework

Three questions, four techniques, two pairs, one sequence. THREE QUESTIONS: is it wearing, is the oil fit, is anything getting in. FOUR TECHNIQUES: spectrometry says WHAT, particle count says HOW MUCH and HOW BIG, FTIR says what the OIL has become, BN and AN say how much fight is LEFT. TWO PAIRS for diagnosis: iron with residual BN, and particle count with spectrometry. ONE SEQUENCE for action: verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil.

Regulation and source map

  • NO IMO INSTRUMENT, INDIAN STATUTE OR CLASS RULE PRESCRIBES A LUBRICATING OIL ANALYSIS PROGRAMME. None is named here, because naming one would give the answer an authority it does not have.
  • MARPOL Annex VI - the 0.50 per cent m/m limit on the sulphur content of fuel oil used outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020 and therefore three years old at this sitting. Used only to explain cylinder oil base number selection; no provision is quoted and no regulation number is asserted.
  • ISM Code chapter 10 - maintenance of the ship and equipment, the regime under which sampling and analysis are planned and recorded. The held chain runs A.741(18) as amended through MSC.353(92), which is the text in force at this sitting.
  • The cleanliness code used for reporting particle counts - named as the reporting convention only. NOT HELD by MIW; no code, band or table reproduced.
  • The two standard titration methods for Base Number - described by chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy. NOT HELD by MIW and no standard number asserted.
  • Engine builder service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation - the only correct sources for condemning limits, alarm levels and target values. Not held; none reproduced.

Uncertainty and currency

This question carries no statutory content, and the answer says so expressly rather than borrowing authority from an unrelated instrument. Atomic emission spectrometry, infrared spectroscopy, particle counting and acid-base titration are not dated law and carry no amendment boundary, so the reversal problem that governs the rest of this paper does not touch it. The answer would read the same at any sitting in this period.

The no-figures rule is absolute here and is the most important evidential feature of the answer. No condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, cleanliness code, base number figure or wear rate appears as a general rule anywhere. Every one is engine-builder, oil, application and service specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard as well as an evidential defect. The engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation are the governing sources, and MIW holds neither. A deliberate consequence of that discipline is that nothing in this answer can go stale through a re-issued service letter or a revised standard.

The infrared absorption regions are named by what they indicate and not by wavenumber, because the positions shift with chemical environment and the integration windows are instrument and method specific. The two standard Base Number titration methods are not identified by standard number; they are distinguished by their chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy between them. The cleanliness code is named as a reporting convention only. No oil company, analyser manufacturer, engine builder, laboratory or proprietary condition-monitoring programme is named anywhere.

The interpretation, corrective-measures and sampling material is engineering judgement, reasoned from the properties of the four techniques. It answers what the printed preamble expressly demands. It is not a manufacturer's recommendation and is not presented as one, and a diagnosis is never asserted from numbers alone - each pairing carries a stated confidence and directs the reader to physical confirmation.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for
  2. __________A) A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis
  3. __________A) What spectrometry cannot see, and why that governs its use
  4. __________B) B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy
  5. __________C) C) Particle count
  6. __________D) D) Base Number against Acid Number
  7. __________Correct interpretation - reading the four results together
  8. __________Corrective measures - what is actually done
  9. __________Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything
Flashcards 10

1 Why analyse, and the three questions. 2 Elemental spectrometry. 3 Its particle-size limitation. 4 FTIR. 5 Particle count. 6 BN against AN - steps 2 to 6 being the four printed sub-items. 7 Correct interpretation, by diagnostic pairs. 8 Corrective measures. 9 Sampling and trending.

The printed preamble expressly demands interpretation and corrective measures, and the verb is 'discuss', not 'briefly discuss'. Sections 7 and 8 are roughly half the marks and are what most candidates never write.

Is the machine wearing? - elemental spectrometry and particle count. Is the oil still fit? - FTIR, and Base and Acid Number. Is anything getting in? - all four, from different directions. The pairing to remember: spectrometry says WHAT the debris is; particle count says HOW MUCH and HOW BIG. FTIR and BN/AN say nothing about the machine at all - they describe the oil.

Framing them as a system rather than a list is what 'discuss' is asking for, and it makes the interpretation section reconstructable from first principles.

No. The method needs the particle to be completely atomised in the excitation source, and coarse particles are not - so the response falls away as size increases and the technique reports essentially the fine fraction only. Normal rubbing wear is seen well; bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting are coarse and may not be seen at all. That is exactly why particle count, ferrography and magnetic plug inspection sit alongside it.

The single most valuable point in the question, the one most often omitted, and the standard oral trap.

Infrared passes through a thin film of oil and molecular bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers. All wavelengths are gathered at once as an interferogram and Fourier transformed into a spectrum, which is what makes it fast. It is a differential measurement against the same oil unused, so without a new-oil reference it is much weaker. It detects oxidation, nitration, sulphation, water, glycol, soot as a baseline rise, and fuel dilution - and is blind to wear metals.

'Differential' is the word candidates miss, and it carries a standing instruction: retain a sealed sample of every oil delivery.

Solid particles are counted and sized, usually by optical light obscuration. Pore-blockage methods are used where the oil is too dark, and microscopy is the referee method. Results are counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands, condensed to a cleanliness code. It is decisive for hydraulic and servo systems, measures filtration performance, and sees the coarse particles spectrometry misses. Blind spots: it counts water and air as particles, and never says what they are.

Complementary to spectrometry rather than an alternative to it - and the water-and-air limitation explains a great many false alarms.

Both are titrations reported in mg KOH per gramme of oil, which is why they are confused. BN is the alkaline reserve from overbased detergent additives and it FALLS in service. AN is the acidic constituents present and it RISES. An oil can carry both at once - separate titrations of separate species. BN governs cylinder and trunk-piston oils; AN governs turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils, which carry no reserve at all.

Three separate marks live here: same units, opposite directions, and which one governs which oil.

No. Two distinct standard titration methods for Base Number are in use - one with a strongly acidic titrant, one with a weaker one - and they return systematically different results on the same oil, the stronger method giving the higher figure. Trending them together makes a change of laboratory or method look exactly like a change in the oil.

A quiet trap that catches experienced engineers as often as candidates, because the report looks identical either way.

Iron up + residual BN near exhaustion = corrosive wear; in a cylinder, cold corrosion. Iron up + BN healthy = not corrosion, and more oil will worsen it. Particle count up + wear metals normal = filtration failure or ingress. Particle count up + spectrometry clean = coarse debris, the most dangerous case. Silicon with iron and chromium = dirt ingress. Sodium and boron with glycol = coolant leak. Oxidation and AN up with no wear metals = the oil is degrading, not the machine.

The stem demands 'correct interpretation'. No single result diagnoses anything - pairs and trends do, and rate of change beats absolute level.

Verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil. Re-sample and re-test first, because many alarming results are sampling artefacts. Then go and look: magnetic plugs, filters cut open, centrifuge sludge, ferrography for debris shape. Then attack the cause - the leaking seal, the failing cooler, the injection equipment - because filtration removes the consequence and leaves the cause running. Only then renew or adjust the oil, and record every action, or the trend is destroyed.

This is the second half of what the stem asks for, and 'correct the cause before the oil' is the sentence that shows it has been understood.

From the engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for that specific installation - never from a textbook and never from the last ship. Every condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, cleanliness code and base number target is engine, oil, application and service specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard as well as an examination risk.

Saying this scores better than a remembered number, and it is the answer a Chief Engineer is supposed to give.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Four techniques answering three questions. Elemental spectrometry - the sample is excited in a plasma or rotating-disc arc, each element emits at characteristic wavelengths and intensity gives ppm; reads wear metals (iron, copper, lead, tin, chromium, aluminium), contaminants (silicon from dirt, sodium and boron from coolant, vanadium and nickel from residual fuel) and additive elements (calcium, magnesium, zinc, phosphorus). Its critical limitation: coarse particles are not fully atomised, so it reports the fine fraction only - fatigue, spalling and scuffing debris may be invisible, so a clean report is not a healthy machine. FTIR - bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers, all gathered as an interferogram and Fourier transformed; a differential measurement against the same oil unused; detects oxidation, nitration, sulphation, water, glycol, soot (a baseline rise), fuel dilution and additive depletion; fast but semi-quantitative and blind to wear metals. Particle count - light obscuration, pore blockage on dark oil, microscopy as referee; counts per millilitre in cumulative bands condensed to a cleanliness code; decisive for hydraulics, the measure of filtration performance, and it sees what spectrometry misses - but counts water and air as particles and never says what they are. BN and AN - both mg KOH/g, BN falls, AN rises, and both can be present at once; two standard BN methods differ systematically so never trend them together; BN governs cylinder and trunk-piston oils, AN governs turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils. Interpret by pairs: iron up with BN gone = corrosion; iron up with BN healthy = not corrosion; count up with metals normal = filtration failure; count up with spectrometry clean = coarse debris, the dangerous case. Then verify, confirm physically, correct the cause, correct the oil, on properly taken samples that are trended.
Keywords
condition monitoringatomic emission spectrometryrotating disc electrodeparts per millionwear metalscontaminantsadditive elementsfine fractionincomplete atomisationferrographyFTIRinterferogramFourier transformdifferential measurementnew oil referenceoxidationnitrationsulphationsoot baseline riseglycolparticle countlight obscurationpore blockagecumulative size bandscleanliness codebase numberacid numbermg KOH per grammealkaline reservetwo titration methodsscrape-downmagnetic plugpurifiertrendingrate of change
Critical numbers
  • 0.50 per cent m/m limit on the sulphur content of fuel oil used outside emission control areas, in force 1 January 2020 - three years and two months before this sitting, and the only figure quoted in this answer
  • NO condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, cleanliness code, base number figure or wear rate is quoted as a general rule
  • Both BN and AN are expressed in milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil - the same units, opposite meanings
  • The governing sources for every limit: the engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for that specific installation
Critical regulation
None. No IMO instrument, Indian statute or classification rule prescribes a lubricating oil analysis programme, and saying so expressly is correct rather than evasive. MARPOL Annex VI is named only for the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit that changed how cylinder oil base number is selected. Sampling and analysis are planned and recorded under ISM Code chapter 10 as part of the maintenance regime, on the held chain A.741(18) as amended through MSC.353(92).
Major trap
Describing the four techniques and never answering the stem's express demand for correct interpretation and corrective measures - which the opening sentence states and which 'discuss' rather than 'briefly discuss' reinforces. That is roughly half the marks. Second trap: omitting the particle-size limitation of spectrometry, and so implying that a clean spectrometric report means a healthy machine. It does not - coarse debris from bearing fatigue, spalling and scuffing may not be seen at all. Third: assuming BN and AN cannot both be significant in the same sample, or applying BN to a turbine or hydraulic oil that carries no alkaline reserve. Fourth: trending base numbers across two different laboratories or methods, when the two standard methods give systematically different values on the same oil. Fifth: quoting condemning limits, ppm values or cleanliness codes as general rules - every one is engine-builder, oil and application specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard.

Also on the platform: QP2303 Q4 - the sulphur and combustion chemistry behind the acid load these numbers measure · QP2303 Q5 - engine power and the machinery whose condition this analysis reports on

March 2023 rapid revision

Every question in one pass. Generated from each question's Quick Revision block — open the full answer from the question number.
