Q1 UNCLOS Pollution Definition, Coastal State Obligations, and the Three Compensation Tiers
16 marks		 The Convention's definition of pollution, and what makes it unusually wide → The general obligation, and why it is not confined to any zone → The coastal State's obligations - rule-making → The coastal State's obligations - enforcement, and its limits → Why the oil pollution compensation regime has three tiers → Tier one - the shipowner and his insurer → Tiers two and three - the Funds, and who actually provides the money → What the three tiers do NOT cover - and the trap UNCLOS article 1(1)(4): pollution of the marine environment means the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water and reduction of amenities. And article 235(2): States shall ensure that recourse is available in accordance with their legal systems for prompt and adequate compensation or other relief in respect of damage caused by pollution of the marine environment by natural or juridical persons under their jurisdiction - which is the obligation that the three-tier compensation regime discharges for oil.
UNCLOS in force 16 November 1994 and UNAMENDED - the held text is the sitting text		 Writing about the maritime zones instead of about the obligations. The zones are not asked for, and article 192 is precisely not zone-limited - introducing them works against the point limb A is making. Second trap: paraphrasing the definition loosely and losing 'or energy' and 'or is likely to result', which are the two phrases that make it wide. Third: treating the coastal State as the only enforcement actor, when there are three and the flag State's duty is primary. Fourth, and the biggest on limb B: saying the Funds are financed by shipowners or by governments. They are financed by receivers of contributing oil - the cargo interest. Fifth: naming the Bunkers Convention 2001 as one of the three tiers; it is a separate, single-tier regime with no fund behind it. Sixth: forgetting that tier three is optional, so the same casualty attracts different compensation in different States. Seventh: quoting monetary limits, which are amended by tacit acceptance and therefore carry a date.
Q2 Decarbonisation in Shipping, IMO Ambitions and the Two Short-Term Measures
16 marks		 What decarbonisation means in shipping → The instrument that frames it at this sitting → The levels of ambition → The pathway - short-term, mid-term and long-term → The first short-term measure - the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index → The second short-term measure - the Carbon Intensity Indicator → The guidelines that make the two measures operable → What the two measures do and do not achieve MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, as introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), which entered into force on 1 November 2022 with the requirements applying from 1 January 2023. It carries the two short-term measures: the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index, a one-off technical requirement under which a ship's attained EEXI must not exceed the required EEXI derived from the design-index reference line with a reduction factor; and the Carbon Intensity Indicator, an annual operational requirement under which the attained annual operational CII is compared with a required value and the ship rated A to E, a rating of D for three consecutive years or E for one year requiring a plan of corrective actions in an enhanced SEEMP. The policy frame is the Initial IMO Strategy, resolution MEPC.304(72) of 2018. No provision of Annex VI is quoted here and no numeric factor is asserted.
THREE levels of ambition, all against a 2008 baseline		 Answering on the wrong Strategy. IMO replaced its greenhouse gas strategy four months after this examination, and nearly every source a candidate will have seen is written on the later one - with a net-zero formulation and 2030 and 2040 checkpoints that did not exist at this sitting. The operative instrument here is the Initial Strategy of 2018, with its three levels of ambition against a 2008 baseline. Second trap: naming EEDI as one of the two short-term measures. EEDI is the pre-existing measure for new ships; the two asked for are EEXI and CII. Third: describing mid-term measures as agreed - they are candidate measures. Fourth: saying EEXI is annual (it is one-off) or that CII is a design calculation (it is operational, from actual fuel and distance). Fifth: forgetting the D-three-years-or-E-once trigger and the SEEMP corrective action plan, which together are the entire enforcement mechanism. Sixth: ignoring the guidelines, when the stem asks for them in terms - the regulation states the requirement and the guidelines carry the arithmetic. Seventh: confusing the intensity ambition with the absolute one, and so failing to see that neither short-term measure can deliver the third level of ambition.
Q3 Perils of the Sea, Cover for Uninsured Perils, and Due Diligence
16 marks		 What 'perils of the sea' actually means → The maritime perils that are covered but are NOT perils of the sea → How an insured covers a peril that is not an insured peril - with examples → The perils whose cover is qualified by due diligence - the Inchmaree group → Where the onus of proof lies, and why it matters → The Indian frame, and what it means on board The Rules for Construction of Policy in the Schedule to the Marine Insurance Act, 1963: 'perils of the seas' refers only to fortuitous accidents or casualties of the seas and does not include the ordinary action of the winds and waves. The rule number is deliberately not asserted, because the Act's text is not held by MIW - the rule is stated as law and its address is not.
Marine Insurance Act, 1963 - the Indian statute. NOT the UK Act of 1906, which is the model only		 Treating 'perils of the sea' as everything a marine policy covers. It is the narrowest term in the question, and the marks are in showing how narrow. Second trap: putting the Master, Officers and Crew into the due-diligence list - they are deliberately absent, because their negligence is the insured peril itself. Third: putting the onus on the assured. The due-diligence provision is a proviso limiting liability, so the underwriters must prove want of diligence. Fourth: saying latent defect cover replaces the defective part - it pays the resulting damage unless additional perils cover has been bought. Fifth, and fatal in an Indian examination: citing the UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 as the governing statute. It is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963.
Q4 Primary versus Secondary NOx Reduction, Scavenge Air Moisturizing and EGR
16 marks		 Why NOx forms at all, and the three levers every measure pulls → A) Primary measures - preventing NOx from forming → A) Secondary measures - removing NOx after it has formed → A) Primary against secondary - the comparison the limb asks for → A) The regulatory frame that decides which you need → B) Scavenge Air Moisturizing → B) Exhaust Gas Recirculation → B) SAM against EGR - what each costs to fit and to run MARPOL Annex VI regulation 13 controls nitrogen oxides from marine diesel engines. At this sitting the applicable text is the Revised Annex VI introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), which entered into force on 1 November 2022 - established from the resolution's own operative paragraph 3, because secondary summaries of this amendment circulate with the date one year late. The regulation sets three tiers of progressively stricter limits, each expressed as a function of the engine's rated speed; which tier applies is fixed by the ship's construction date, and the strictest tier depends besides on operation within a designated NOx emission control area, so the engine runs in two modes and the changeover is recorded with date, time and position. Certification is under the revised NOx Technical Code 2008 and compliance in service rests on the NOx Technical File. No provision is quoted and no limit value is asserted.
The Revised MARPOL Annex VI, introduced by resolution MEPC.328(76), entered into force 1 NOVEMBER 2022 - four months before this sitting, from the resolution's own operative paragraph 3		 Calling exhaust gas recirculation a secondary measure. All of its plant sits outside the cylinder, which makes it look like after-treatment - but it works by preventing formation, so it is primary. Classify by where the NOx goes, not by where the equipment is bolted. Second trap: offering an exhaust gas scrubber as a NOx measure - it addresses sulphur oxides and particulates and does essentially nothing for NOx. Third: saying the nitrogen comes from the fuel; it comes overwhelmingly from the air. Fourth: describing primary and secondary measures separately and never comparing them, when the stem prints vis-a-vis. Fifth: omitting the NOx-fuel consumption trade-off, which is the whole reason the choice exists. Sixth: forgetting the water mist catcher on a SAM system or the scrubber on an EGR loop - the two components that make each system survivable. Seventh: quoting limit values or percentage reductions as though they were general figures.
Q5 Power Transformation from Indicated to Effective Power, and Sea Trial Manoeuvres
16 marks		 What the question is asking - the power chain, and where effective power sits → A) Indicated power to brake power - the engine's own losses → A) Brake power to shaft power to delivered power - the transmission → A) Delivered power to thrust power to effective power - the propeller and the hull → A) The efficiencies pulled together - the propulsive coefficient → B) What sea trials are for, and the conditions that make them valid → B) The navigation trials, and what each establishes → B) What the results become None on limb A - no statutory instrument prescribes the power chain, and saying so is correct rather than evasive. On limb B, three instruments are engaged and all three are cited by identity only, with no criterion value and no provision quoted: the IMO standards for ship manoeuvrability, which set the criteria against which turning, yaw-checking, course-keeping and stopping ability are judged; the requirement that manoeuvring information be provided on board, which is what turns the trial results into the manoeuvring booklet, the wheelhouse poster and the pilot card; and the steering gear trial required before the ship enters service, in main and auxiliary modes.
SIX powers in the chain: indicated, brake, shaft, delivered, thrust, effective		 Defining thrust power on the ship's speed instead of the speed of advance. The speed of advance is lower because of the wake, and getting this wrong unravels the whole hull-propeller section. Second trap: skipping the hull-propeller interaction and going straight from the shaft to the ship - the wake and thrust deduction pair is where the thinking in limb A is, and stating that hull efficiency can exceed unity with the reason is what distinguishes a strong answer. Third: omitting delivered power from the chain, or confusing the quasi-propulsive coefficient with the propulsive coefficient. Fourth, and specific to limb B: listing trials without their objectives, when the stem asks for 'the typical objectives of each test' in terms - that is half the limb. Fifth: forgetting the trial conditions, above all deep unrestricted water and runs on reciprocal headings, without which the measurements mean nothing. Sixth: quoting typical efficiency values or trial criteria as numbers when both are installation and instrument specific.
Q6 Human Error in a Socio-Technical System, and Human Element versus Human Factors
16 marks		 (a) Where the 80 per cent claim comes from, and what kind of statement it is → (a) What 'a complex hierarchical socio-technical system' means for a ship → (a) What 'human error' therefore means here, and what follows → (b) Human Element - the domain → (b) Human Error - the outcome → (b) Human Factors - the discipline → (b) The three distinguished through one incident The Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008 and mandatory from 1 January 2010, directs a marine safety investigation at the circumstances and the causes of a casualty so that safety may be enhanced, and separates that enquiry from the apportionment of blame or liability. That separation exists for exactly the reason limb (a) is about: an investigation conducted to find fault stops at the last human act, and one conducted to prevent recurrence has to go past it.
The 80 per cent figure is the examiner's premise and is NOT endorsed. No alternative statistic is offered anywhere in this answer		 Accepting the 80 per cent figure as a measured finding and then writing an essay on why seafarers are careless. The limb is built to catch precisely that, and the figure is a stopping rule describing where investigations halt. Second trap: treating limb (a) as a preamble to the definitions, when on this paper it is printed at eight marks - the same as limb (b) - and the same question elsewhere splits 6 and 10. Third: defining the human element as the seafarer, when it expressly spans shore management, regulators, recognized organizations, shipyards and legislators. Fourth: treating human factors as a synonym for human error, when one is the discipline that prevents and the other is the event that happened. Fifth: calling a violation an error - violations are deliberate, and a routine violation is evidence against the procedure rather than against the person. Sixth: giving three definitions and no example, when the stem asks for examples in terms.
Q7 PSC Clear Grounds for a More Detailed Inspection, and Corrective versus Preventive Action
16 marks		 Where the power comes from, and what an inspection is before clear grounds exist → 'Clear grounds' - the definition, and the two words that carry it → The circumstances that create clear grounds - with examples → What follows once clear grounds exist → The three terms that must be separated → Corrective action - what it actually involves → Preventive action - where it comes from → The two worked through one defect, and how the limbs connect SOLAS regulation I/19, control: a ship in a port of another Contracting Government is subject to control by officers duly authorised to verify that the certificates are valid; and where there are clear grounds for believing that the condition of the ship or of her equipment does not correspond substantially with the particulars of those certificates, steps shall be taken to ensure that the ship shall not sail until she can proceed to sea without danger to the ship or persons on board - every effort being made to avoid a ship being unduly detained or delayed. The corresponding control provisions are MARPOL articles 5 and 6, Load Lines 1966 article 21, STCW article X and regulation I/4, and MLC 2006 Title 5.2. IMO's consolidated Procedures supply the vocabulary and the sequence; they are recommendatory and confer no power, and the edition in circulation at this sitting is not held by MIW, so no paragraph is cited.
Clear grounds has TWO qualifying words - EVIDENCE and SUBSTANTIALLY - plus a free-standing head of crew unfamiliarity		 Treating clear grounds as anything that looks wrong. It is evidence of a substantial departure, and both words exclude something. Second trap: forgetting that crew unfamiliarity is a free-standing head - a materially perfect ship can be opened up on it alone. Third: saying the power comes from the IMO Procedures; it comes from the conventions, and the Procedures only invite Governments to implement them. Fourth: treating the detailed inspection as a general re-survey, when its scope is set by the grounds that triggered it. Fifth, and the whole of limb b's trap: confusing correction with corrective action - replacing the failed part is a correction and leaves the cause exactly where it was. Sixth: describing preventive action as more thorough corrective action, when the difference is detected against potential - a difference in kind, not degree. Seventh: omitting verification of effectiveness, which is the step an auditor goes to first.
Q8 IACS Structure and UI, UR, PR; Recognized Organizations and the RO Code
16 marks		 What IACS is and how it is organised → UR, UI and PR - what each one acts upon → Does it signify to owners and operators - and why → What a Recognized Organization is → The RO Code and its salient features → How Administrations monitor Recognized Organizations The Code for Recognized Organizations, adopted by resolution MSC.349(92) of 21 June 2013 and by resolution MEPC.237(65), and made mandatory from 1 January 2015 by MSC.350(92), MSC.356(92) and MEPC.238(65), which amended SOLAS, MARPOL Annexes I and II and the 1988 Load Line Protocol to refer to it - SOLAS regulation XI-1/1 being the provision that gives it force. Parts 1 and 2 are mandatory; Part 3, the flag State oversight guidelines, is recommendatory, and the adopting resolution recommends applying it unless national arrangements provide at least equivalent safety.
RO Code adopted by MSC.349(92) on 21 June 2013 and by MEPC.237(65)		 Merging the two limbs - treating IACS as a regulator, or the RO Code as an IACS document. IACS is a private association that makes no law; the RO Code is an IMO instrument adopted by MSC and MEPC resolutions. Second trap: saying Part 3 is mandatory. Parts 1 and 2 bind; Part 3 is guidance. Third: answering the significance limb with a bare yes, when the marks are entirely in the reasons. Fourth: treating 'classification society' and 'Recognized Organization' as synonyms - they are two capacities, and a society is an RO only for the flag that delegated and only to the extent delegated. Fifth: saying the flag State transfers responsibility with the work. It does not. Sixth, and specific to this sitting: citing the obligations list at A.1187(33), which was adopted nine months after this examination. The operative instrument here is A.1157(32).
Q9 Lubricating Oil Condition Analysis Techniques
16 marks		 Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for → A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis → What spectrometry cannot see, and why that governs its use → B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy → C) Particle count → D) Base Number against Acid Number → Correct interpretation - reading the four results together → Corrective measures - what is actually done → Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything None. No IMO instrument, Indian statute or classification rule prescribes a lubricating oil analysis programme, and saying so expressly is correct rather than evasive. MARPOL Annex VI is named only for the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit that changed how cylinder oil base number is selected. Sampling and analysis are planned and recorded under ISM Code chapter 10 as part of the maintenance regime, on the held chain A.741(18) as amended through MSC.353(92).
0.50 per cent m/m limit on the sulphur content of fuel oil used outside emission control areas, in force 1 January 2020 - three years and two months before this sitting, and the only figure quoted in this answer		 Describing the four techniques and never answering the stem's express demand for correct interpretation and corrective measures - which the opening sentence states and which 'discuss' rather than 'briefly discuss' reinforces. That is roughly half the marks. Second trap: omitting the particle-size limitation of spectrometry, and so implying that a clean spectrometric report means a healthy machine. It does not - coarse debris from bearing fatigue, spalling and scuffing may not be seen at all. Third: assuming BN and AN cannot both be significant in the same sample, or applying BN to a turbine or hydraulic oil that carries no alkaline reserve. Fourth: trending base numbers across two different laboratories or methods, when the two standard methods give systematically different values on the same oil. Fifth: quoting condemning limits, ppm values or cleanliness codes as general rules - every one is engine-builder, oil and application specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard.
